The indented `return: 1` line is an ordinary runtime return inside the `if` body. The final `return: n * factorial(n - 1)` is terminal return for the surrounding `fn` definition.

8.3. Terminal Return as Executable Structural Close

A terminal return is a `return` source item written in a structural trailer position for an open executable definition. The common compact position is the source level of the executable definition itself, where a structural close of that executable definition is expected.

If an executable definition is opened at source level N, then its ordinary
vertical body items are normally written at level N + 1. A `return` line at level
N is not a sibling root item. It is parsed as terminal return for the currently
open executable definition when that definition's resolved receiver admits the
return form.

Here "at the source level of the executable definition" means the level of the opening source item, such as `fn: name` or `sub: name`, not the ordinary body level. A `return` or `return: value` item written at level N + 1 or deeper is a runtime return inside the executable body. It exits the executable at runtime, but it is not the structural trailer of the executable definition.

Terminal return is equivalent to appending the corresponding runtime `return` as
the final body item and then closing the executable definition by its admitted
executable close target. For an `fn`, this is equivalent to appending `return:
value` to the body and closing the function with its receiver-exposed name, such
as `end: square`.

If the terminal `return` item is written at a smaller source level than the executable definition itself, it may still act as the terminal trailer for the visible executable tail when the normal source-level cut reaches that tail and all intervening frames admit such closure. In that case, the `return` role belongs to the executable tail. Enclosing non-executable frames are closed by the source-level cut, not because `return` is a general close receiver for them.

Example:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
return: x * x

is source-semantically equivalent to:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
    return: x * x
end: square

The first form is the canonical compact form for a short function. The second form is an explicit expanded explanation of the same source semantics.

Example with an enclosing level receiver:

L3:
    sub: helloMain
        print: "Hello"
    return
end: L3

means:

L3:
    sub: helloMain
        print: "Hello"
        return
    end: helloMain
end: L3

The source-level `return` at the level of `sub: helloMain` closes the `sub: helloMain` definition. The following `end: L3` closes only the enclosing level receiver.

Example where the same terminal return item is written at a lower source level:

L3:
    sub: helloMain
        print: "Hello"
return

means, when the active level profile admits this cut:

L3:
    sub: helloMain
        print: "Hello"
        return
    end: helloMain
end: L3

Terminal return may close the open executable tail in the same checked way as the executable's admitted `end:` target. For an `fn`, that target is the function name exposed by the `fn` receiver, such as `end: square`. It is not a general close form for arbitrary Structures. It is valid only for executable definitions or executable-like receivers that explicitly admit terminal return.

A terminal `return` may also cut through still-open nested body frames above its target level in the same structural-trailer sense as `end: <name>` or an allowed nameless delimiter, provided all intervening frames admit such closure.

Invalid examples:

return: 1

at root level with no open executable is invalid.

config: app
return: 1

is invalid unless `config` is an executable-like receiver that explicitly admits terminal return. `return` does not replace `end:` for ordinary data, model, configuration or registry Structures.

8.4. Runtime Return vs Terminal Return

The source level separates the two roles.

Runtime return inside a body:

fn: abs (int(x)) (int)
    if: x < 0
        return: -x
return: x

Here the indented `return: -x` line is runtime control inside the `if` body. It does not structurally close the `fn` source item. The final `return: x` is terminal return at the function level and closes the function.

Terminal value return may use values prepared in named return variables:

fm: `estimate harvest` (PlantBed(bed)) (f32(harvest), f32(redApples), f32(greenApples))
    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
return: harvest redApples greenApples

This is equivalent to appending a body-level `return: harvest redApples greenApples` and closing the function.

8.5. Named Return Variables

Multiple return values may be represented by declared return variables inside an executable body when the `fm` return-description Structure gives them names.

Example:

fm: `estimate harvest` (PlantBed(bed))
    (
        f32: harvest
        f32: redApples
        f32: greenApples
    )
    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
return: harvest redApples greenApples

The named return variables denote fields of the already existing return
Structure. The terminal `return:` finishes their updates, forwards that
Structure reference and closes the function definition. It does not allocate or
copy the return Structure.

An `fm` return-description Structure with several fields defines one arena-owned
return Structure. Updating its declared fields and returning an existing
Structure reference are source-semantically equivalent when the produced
Structure's descriptor implements the declared return descriptor.

Example positional structural return:

fm: `split harvest` (PlantBed(bed))
    (
        f32: harvest
        f32: redApples
        f32: greenApples
    )
return: bed\area 0 0

Here the `return:` tail supplies three field values consumed by the return
signature. The values are installed in the already existing return Structure;
the `return` control operation then forwards its reference.

8.6. Function Name Rebind

The current function name is not a special return target. It is an ordinary name in the shared lexical namespace.

If an execution profile exposes a local result binding with the same visible
name as the function, the ordinary head/update form may rebind that name when
the old result descriptor implements the descriptor of the pending value:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
    square: x * x
return: square

This is not a dedicated return form. The line `square: x * x` is an ordinary
plain-name rebind in the active lexical namespace view. It appends a new
occurrence, and the visible name `square` denotes that newest result binding, so
recursive lookup through `square(...)` is no longer available through that same
visible name in the remaining local scope.

The function still returns explicitly with `return: square`. The canonical return mechanism for an `fn` is `return: value`. When written in a valid terminal trailer position, `return: value` also closes the function definition.

8.7. Implicit Return at Function End

Implicit return at function end is not a core source form in this revision.

In the core profile, an explicit structural close such as `end: f` does not append an implicit runtime return for a value-producing function.

The preferred style is:

fn: f (int(x)) (int)
return: x + 1

rather than relying on a value left in a local binding at structural close.

8.8. Return Source Semantics

At the source-semantic level, every `fn` has one logical single-value return.
Primitive results may use a direct primitive representation. Every ordinary
Structure or Array result uses its reference representation. A Structure or
array is never passed or returned as an aggregate value and is never implicitly
copied.

At the source-semantic level, `fm` has one logical return Structure. The
return-description Structure explicitly declares the returned fields. This is
the multiple-return mechanism. The returned Structure may be consumed as one
value or unpacked/mapped as a sequence of returned values where the receiving
receiver signature admits it. The field types themselves do not define the
`fn`/`fm` boundary; the boundary is whether the source return is one value or an
explicit return Structure.

For a Structure or Array result, `return: expression` forwards the exact
reference produced by `expression` after its descriptor implements the declared
return descriptor. Return lowering must not
allocate, clone, copy, materialize or promote the referenced object, and it must
not change its address or identity. The local binding that contains the
reference may cease to exist; the referenced object belongs to its arena, not
to that local reference slot.

Evaluating `expression` may itself execute an explicit Structure or Array
constructor whose declared semantics allocate a new object in the selected
arena. That allocation belongs to the constructor and produces the reference
which `return` forwards. The `return` receiver must not add another result
object, copy or promotion.

No ordinary Structure or Array return is invalid merely because the reference
was bound or the object was created lexically inside the function. Ordinary
local Structures and arrays are arena-managed reference objects from the moment
they are materialized; they are not C automatic aggregate values. Before an
arena containing a returned reference is cut, the runtime publishes that
reference to the caller/continuation and retains, detaches or reparents its
already existing arena region as required. These owner-graph operations preserve
the address and must not allocate on the return path. Section 19.29 defines the
owner ordering.

For `fm`, positional or named return fields populate an already existing
arena-owned result Structure. The caller may provide an explicit target arena
and result before entry, or the callee may create it once in its prologue before executing the
body. The return operation forwards its reference after the field updates; it
does not construct the result at the exit point.

The `return:` receiver consumes its tail as the current function result and
exits. For a `sub` or no-value label/procedure executable, a bare `return` exits
without producing a value. Terminal return additionally closes the current
executable definition in source structure when the active executable receiver
admits that return form in a trailer position.

Primitive ABI details and the physical representation of a reference are
translator/runtime implementation details. The reference-only Structure/Array
rule, stable identity and absence of allocation/copy at `return` are
source-semantic requirements and are not implementation options.

The error mechanism is not specified by this document. A function may represent an erroneous result as one of the values allowed by its declared return type unless a later error-system specification defines a separate convention.

?
The general error/status system is not specified in this document. It may be defined later as ordinary return values, tagged result Structures, receiver-level diagnostics or another mechanism.
?

---ANSI C---
Possible return-lowering variants for an ANSI C implementation:

variant 1:
A primitive `fn` return lowers to the active profile's chosen primitive C
representation.

variant 2:
An ordinary Structure or Array `fn` return lowers to the exact existing
reference representation produced by the return expression.

variant 3:
An `fm` multi-field source result uses an arena-owned output Structure passed
through a hidden or explicit reference. That Structure exists before `return`.

variant 4:
An `fm` callee creates its arena-owned result Structure once in the function
prologue before executing the body. Every return path updates and forwards that
same reference; the return operation performs no allocation or copy.

variant 5:
An `fm` return Structure may be optimized away when the translator can map its
fields directly to the consuming context without changing observable Structure
identity or the reference-only contract.

variant 6:
Terminal return lowers as an ordinary body-level return followed by no further generated body statements. Source-level structural closure is resolved before lowering.

These variants are implementation strategies only. No ANSI C variant may
return an ordinary Structure or Array as a C aggregate value or
allocate/copy/materialize it in the return path. The language-level rule remains
that `return: value` returns the declared single value from an `fn` or the
reference to the declared return Structure from an `fm`, bare `return` exits a
no-value executable when admitted, and terminal return also closes the current
executable definition.
---ANSI C---

9. Type and Model Forms

9.0. Default C99 Bootstrap Type and Dependency Map

The default ANSI C / C99 bootstrap profile may use the following implementation mapping for primitive and service-level source types. This is an implementation/dependency map, not a separate source grammar.

    i/u      -> C integer          -> no vcpkg
    bool     -> C int / bool       -> no vcpkg
    bigint   -> GMP mpz_t          -> vendored `third_party/gmp.zip`
    real     -> MPFR mpfr_t        -> vendored `third_party/mpfr.zip`
    decimal  -> decNumber         -> vendored `third_party/decNumber.zip`
    SQL      -> SQLite            -> vendored `third_party/sqlite.zip`
    Regex    -> PCRE2 8/16/32     -> vendored `third_party/pcre2.zip`

The default project bootstrap keeps third-party source archives under
`third_party/*.zip`. A full local build expands them into `build/third_party/`
and emits static libraries into `build/libs/`. `vcpkg` may be used as a
download/cache helper for upstream archives, but the default build does not
depend on live package-manager libraries. The bundled PCRE2 profile builds
8-bit, 16-bit and 32-bit code-unit libraries so raw length-based payloads can be
interpreted by the receiver profile instead of being forced through a single
null-terminated UTF-8-only representation.

The `i/u` family denotes implementation-profile integer types over C integer
storage. `bigint`, `real`, `decimal`, `SQL` and `Regex` are ordinary
names/receivers/descriptors in the active lexical namespace view when the active
profile explicitly installs those bindings. Their physical representation and linked libraries
belong to the selected L1/runtime profile.

9.1. Type and Model Information

Type and model information is represented by explicit descriptor Structures
bound in the namespace. A descriptor declaration or import introduces its name
explicitly. An ordinary table may contain descriptor data, but placing a row in
a table does not introduce a namespace binding unless an explicit namespace
import/declaration receiver consumes that data.

The structural core does not require a separate `field` receiver. In
declaration context, a visible primitive or model constructor consumes a raw
identifier token and creates the requested value binding. A primitive
constructor initializes a primitive. A Structure or Array constructor
materializes the value in the selected arena and binds its reference. Neither
operation creates a class, layout, backend or field table.

Example:

    u32: id
    Text: crop
    f64: harvest 0.0

Conceptual meaning:

    declare binding `id` with type `u32`
    declare binding `crop` with type `Text`
    declare binding `harvest` with type `f64` and default/initial value `0.0`

The token after the type constructor is not evaluated as an existing value. It
is the raw name proposed by the declaration. If the exact name is already
visible, the form is a redeclaration governed by
`implements(OldType, NewType)` as specified in section 7.3.1.

9.1.1. Nested Descriptor and Constructor Forms

Type/element and mutability descriptors plus Structure/Array constructors are
ordinary receiver heads. They do
not require a separate declaration syntax. A colon only builds the right-nested
form; it is not a named semantic node.

For example:

    const: char: (): s "hello" "world!"
    const: char: []: s "hello" "world!"
    const: String: (): s "hello" "world!"
    const: String: []: s "hello" "world!"

These are parsed as nested forms:

    const(char(()(s, "hello", "world!")))
    const(char([](s, "hello", "world!")))
    const(String(()(s, "hello", "world!")))
    const(String([](s, "hello", "world!")))

The heads `const`, `char`, `String`, `()` and `[]` are ordinary visible names
resolved by the active semantic profile. In this profile:

    const       qualifies the introduced storage/value as immutable
    char        selects the character primitive/element descriptor
    String      selects the Lingvamyxa immutable string value/reference model
    ()          introduces a named arena-owned Structure reference by default
    []          introduces a named arena-owned Array reference

Therefore the examples mean:

    const: char: (): s "hello" "world!"
        `s` is an immutable Structure reference whose fields contain character
        or text values according to the visible descriptor.

    const: char: []: s "hello" "world!"
        `s` is an immutable Array reference whose elements are character/text
        values. The array and its backing storage belong to the selected arena.

    const: String: (): s "hello" "world!"
        `s` is a reference to an immutable arena-owned Structure whose fields
        are references to immutable Lingvamyxa String values.

    const: String: []: s "hello" "world!"
        `s` is an immutable array of references to immutable Lingvamyxa String
        values.

`char` and `String` are intentionally different descriptors. `char` is a
primitive/element descriptor. `String` has higher-level immutable text behavior
and must not be treated as merely a spelling of `char *`.

---ANSI C---
A hosted ANSI C backend may represent a known String as a pointer-and-length
object and may represent a known array with contiguous backing storage. It must
obtain every ordinary Structure and Array object from the selected Lingvamyxa
arena. A generated `typedef struct`, flat element block or direct field access
is an optimization; C automatic or static storage duration is not observable
source semantics. The concrete field names, descriptor representation, length
type and string interning are implementation details.
---ANSI C---

9.2. Type Constructors

A form such as:

    int: i

or:

    PlantBed: bed

is meaningful only if `int` or `PlantBed` resolves in the active namespace as a
primitive constructor or as a binding that the active profile exposes as a
Structure constructor. Merely exposing first-level names for `implements`, or
supplying backend layout information, does not by itself make a binding a
constructor. `PlantBed: bed` materializes a `PlantBed` Structure in the selected
arena and explicitly introduces only the binding `bed`; it does not declare or
modify `PlantBed`.

A receiver such as `fn`, `sub`, `autoModel` or `args` may consume these Structures as typed declarations.

10. Conditional Structures: L3 Core Extract

10.1. L3 `if`

L3 `if` is a computational control-flow statement receiver. In the current
core form it consumes:

    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    body      -> statement Structure consumed by `if`

`else` is a separate sibling statement receiver. It may immediately follow an
`if` at the same statement level and consumes:

    body      -> statement Structure consumed by `else`

At interpretation time, the `if` condition is evaluated once. If the condition
is true, the `if` body is interpreted and the following sibling `else` body is
skipped. If the condition is false, the `if` body is skipped and the following
sibling `else` body, when present, is interpreted.

At translation time, the two receivers lower to adjacent target control-flow
forms such as `if (...) { ... } else { ... }`. If another statement separates
`if` and `else`, the pair is no longer adjacent and the target/profile
validator may reject the resulting control flow.

10.2. Conditional Forms

Short positional conditional form:

if: mode = debug
    build: debugTarget

else:
    build: releaseTarget

Normal receiver sequence:

    if(
      <expression Structure for `mode = debug`>,
      (build(debugTarget))
    )
    else(
      (build(releaseTarget))
    )

A profile may also define an independent branch-like receiver with explicit
named branch Structures:

branch: mode = debug
    first:
        build: debugTarget
    second:
        build: releaseTarget
end: branch

This builds:

    branch(
      <expression Structure for `mode = debug`>,
      first(build(debugTarget)),
      second(build(releaseTarget))
    )

The named vertical form is an ordinary receiver-signature pattern. It is not
required for the current core `if`/`else` statement pair.

11. L2 Addresses

11.0. L2 Addresses, References and Callable References

This section separates ordinary arena-owned aggregate references from the
explicit typed-address and machine-storage primitives available only at L2.

11.1. L3 Passing Model

At L3, explicit address syntax is unavailable. Every non-primitive value is a
StructureReference or ArrayReference. A checked "handle" exposed by an L3
profile must therefore be either an admitted primitive token or a wrapper
StructureReference; it is not a fourth value category.

At L3, ordinary names denote primitives, StructureReferences or ArrayReferences
according to the namespace binding. They are not raw L2 addresses.

11.2. L2 Address Values and Storage/Reference Declarations

At L2, primitive values and typed address values coexist with references to
arena-owned Structures and Arrays. A descriptor such as `PlantBed`, explicitly
bound in the namespace, describes an ordinary Structure; construction allocates
it in the current arena and binds its StructureReference. Ordinary `[]:` likewise
constructs an arena-owned ArrayReference. Explicit address slots and explicit
low-level machine-storage forms separately expose raw storage.

Bare L2 value reading is intentionally narrow. A primitive/scalar slot such as
`u32`, `int`, `size_t`, an enum-like scalar or a typed-address primitive may be
read directly by naming it. Raw machine aggregates, when a profile exposes
them, are accessed through typed addresses/low-level operations and are not
StructureReferences or ArrayReferences.

Examples:

PlantBed: branch
PlantBed: tree
PlantBed: root
int: count 5
[]: leafs count

int: x 5
@: int p
@@: int pp
@@@: int ppp

p: @x
pp: @p
ppp: @pp

Meanings:

    branch  arena-owned `PlantBed` Structure reference
    tree    arena-owned `PlantBed` Structure reference
    root    arena-owned `PlantBed` Structure reference
    count   primitive value/storage initialized to `5`
    leafs   arena-owned Array reference with `count` elements
    x       primitive storage/value initialized to `5`
    p       address-depth-1 slot whose base type is `int`
    pp      address-depth-2 slot whose base type is `int`
    ppp     address-depth-3 slot whose base type is `int`
    p: @x   stores the address of storage location `x` into `p`
    pp: @p  stores the address of storage location `p` into `pp`
    ppp: @pp stores the address of storage location `pp` into `ppp`

Structural descriptors such as `PlantBed`, primitive constructors such as
`int`, and Array constructors such as `[]` are namespace-visible bindings. They
consume a raw result name and explicitly introduce that result binding; they do
not register the descriptor or generate Table rows.

An L2 address value is not necessarily a source-level integer. It may carry, or be statically associated with, layout/type metadata. Receivers and L1 lowering may use that metadata to treat an address as a typed reference rather than as a printable numeric address.

The core L2 syntax does not use a separate `*` or `^` marker for address types.
Address-ness is introduced by address-slot declarations, the reserved
address-depth operator family `@ⁿ`, address-producing expressions and explicit
raw field/index operations. Ordinary Arrays do not introduce raw addresses.

Minimal L2 field-follow is address-oriented. If the binding is a typed address,
`value\field` is the direct Lingvamyxa field-follow form. A direct ANSI C
value-structure operation is written through the explicit C door:
`c.value.field`, which lowers to `value.field`. This keeps C value-member access
visible as C-surface syntax while leaving ordinary Lingvamyxa Structures and
Arrays as arena-managed references.

11.2.1. Reserved Address-Depth Operator Family `@ⁿ`

The token sequences:

    @
    @@
    @@@

form the reserved L2 address-depth operator family `@ⁿ`. They are reserved operator heads, not ordinary user-defined identifiers. Ordinary source cannot bind procedures, functions, receivers or variables named `@`, `@@`, `@@@` and so on, unless a privileged core/prelude profile explicitly defines the reserved operator metadata.

In head position before `:`, `@ⁿ:` declares an address/reference slot of depth n:

    @: BaseType name
    @@: BaseType name
    @@@: BaseType name

Examples:

    @: int p
    @@: int pp
    @@@: int ppp

Meanings:

    p    address-depth-1 slot whose base type is `int`
    pp   address-depth-2 slot whose base type is `int`
    ppp  address-depth-3 slot whose base type is `int`

Whitespace separates the base type and the binding name under the ordinary short-tail rule. Therefore:

    @@@: int ppp

has two fields, `int` and `ppp`, while:

    @@@: int; ppp

terminates the `@@@:` short form after `int`. The following `ppp` is not the binding-name field of that declaration; it is the next source item at the current level.

The prefix expression form `@x` remains the depth-1 address-of operation. Higher address depth is expressed by declaring deeper address slots and storing ordinary depth-1 addresses of those slots:

    int: x 5
    @: int p
    @@: int pp
    @@@: int ppp

    p: @x
    pp: @p
    ppp: @pp

This keeps prefix address-of simple while preserving arbitrary address depth in declarations.

The implementation of `@ⁿ` may be represented by reserved operator descriptors in the L2 operator table. The source semantics only require that `@ⁿ` is parsed as a reserved operator family and that its declaration form produces typed address slots compatible with the active L2 memory profile.

Address-depth projection composes with the base type category. A primitive base
type starts at address depth 0. External scalar L1 base types imported by a
projection profile, such as `size_t` in the hosted C99 profile, also start at
address depth 0. This remains true when a primitive exposes first-level names
and a physical aggregate spelling: those names participate in `implements`, but
do not turn the primitive value into a reference. A Structure/opaque-reference descriptor denotes a source-level reference
by its bare name and therefore starts at address depth 1 in an ANSI C pointer
projection. This includes ordinary Lingvamyxa descriptors such as `User`
and opaque imported L1 object/layout types such as ANSI C `FILE`. An opaque
reference descriptor may be stored, passed and addressed by LMX code. Its fields are
not visible in the hosted C99 profile because the ANSI C/C99 substrate does not
publish a portable field layout for that object type; this is not a separate
LMX restriction. A profile/import that explicitly declares layout metadata for
an opaque object type may make typed field-follow available for that imported
layout. Explicit `@ⁿ` address-depth receivers add further address layers on top
of that base category. Ordinary Array construction does not: it produces an
ArrayReference whose backend representation includes its bounds/lifetime
metadata as well as any element-storage address.

Primitive/scalar descriptors are explicit namespace bindings. A visible
descriptor may refer to range, contextual-literal, projection or serialization
information through ordinary Structure fields. An explicit table may organize
the same information for a library/profile, but table membership does not
declare the primitive or alter the value ontology.

Examples in the hosted ANSI C profile:

    char          -> char
    @: char       -> char *
    []: char      -> ArrayReference
                     # backing storage may internally contain `char *`

    FILE          -> FILE *      # opaque imported reference descriptor
    @: FILE       -> FILE **     # address of a `FILE` reference slot

    User          -> User *      # possible projection of a StructureReference
    @: User       -> User **     # address of a `User` reference slot
    []: User      -> ArrayReference
                     # element backing may internally contain `User **`

The arrows in this table illustrate one hosted ANSI C projection. In
particular, `char *` or `User **` may be a component of an ArrayReference
representation, but is not the complete source value produced by `[]:`.

The hosted C99 profile distinguishes three related, but not identical,
reference/layout cases:

1. A Lingvamyxa Structure descriptor is explicitly bound in the namespace and
   describes visible fields and their descriptors. Typed field-follow uses
   that descriptor to determine the result of `user\age`.

2. An opaque imported reference descriptor, such as `FILE`, may be visible for
   storage and call signatures without exposing fields. Therefore `FILE: file`
   may lower as `FILE *file`, and `fopen(...)` may return `FILE`, but field
   follow is unavailable unless an explicit import binds a descriptor that
   exposes the required field information.

3. A projection-only pointer-follow may lower `a\b` to the selected L1 pointer
   member spelling, for example `a->b` in the hosted C99 profile, without
   requiring the LMX translator to prove the field layout. In this case the
   selected L1 toolchain validates the projected spelling. For an opaque C99
   type such as `FILE`, an attempted `file\some_field` in such a projection
   path is accepted or rejected by the C compiler according to the platform's
   visible C declarations.

Typed lookup is required when the translator needs the result descriptor before
L1 compilation. Examples include a field-chain used as a callable head, such as
`binding\handler(...)`, forwarding a named Structure reference through
`return: user`, and fields whose descriptor is derived from an existing binding.
In those cases the translator consults only the bindings and descriptors visible
in the namespace. The lookup does not authorize a return-time copy: the same
reference is returned.

The namespace used to choose the hosted representation is the same namespace
that resolved the source type. A visible descriptor with a present
`structuralFields` view determines only the first-level semantic shape; it does
not by itself select Primitive versus StructureReference and does not contribute
an implicit reference layer. The base value category is resolved separately.
In the hosted profile a function-pointer or explicitly declared Primitive starts
at address depth 0, while an explicit reference-base, opaque reference or
ordinary named-Structure constructor binding starts at address depth 1;
explicit `@ⁿ` depth is then added. Explicitly imported ABI layout/projection
metadata may supply the physical spelling or the compatibility fallback when no
source category has been declared, but it does not replace the descriptor's
semantic field view. A global Registry classification must not override a
source category explicitly visible at the use site.

During bootstrap that compatibility fallback may read explicitly authored
`class.reference-base`, `class.kind = opaqueReference`,
`class.semantic = opaqueHandle` or `class.c.projection` metadata. These are
projection inputs for imported ABI names, not facts created by declaring or
constructing a Lingvamyxa Structure.

The temporary hosted fallback for bindings that do not yet expose a
structural-field view is documented in section 19.20.4. It is a bootstrap
migration mechanism, not an additional source-semantic lookup environment.

11.3. Address-of, Raw Load and Callable References

The prefix form:

    @x

returns the address of the storage location `x`.

If `x` is already an address binding, `@x` is the address of the binding that stores that address. It is address-of-address, not the address of the target object. This is the expression-side companion of the declaration-side `@ⁿ:` family: `@@: int pp` declares an address-depth-2 slot, while `pp: @p` fills that slot with the address of the depth-1 slot `p`.

The prefix form:

    \a

performs a raw L2 load at address expression `a`. It reads the primitive cell, word or value defined by the active L2 memory profile. It does not perform typed structural rendering by itself.

The field-follow form:

    a\field

follows typed address/reference `a` to the named field `field`. It uses the known layout/type metadata of `a`, reads or writes the field slot, and returns the field content as either a primitive value or another typed address/reference.

When more than one field has the same exact name, `a\field` selects the newest
occurrence. The occurrence-qualified form `a\[n]field` selects occurrence `n`
among exact-name matches in zero-based logical source/ordered order. Both forms
designate the selected field slot and may therefore participate in a read,
field/index chain or addressable update.

C-profile mnemonic table:

    @: char c        # C declarator role: char *c
    const: @: T p    # C declarator role: const T *p
    @c               # C expression role: &c

    parent\child     # C expression role: parent->child
    c.parent.child   # C expression role: parent.child
    \parent\child    # parsed as \(parent\child); C expression role: *(parent->child)
    (\parent)\child  # explicit grouping: raw-load parent first, then follow child
    \slot: value     # C update role: *(slot) = value
    \\slot: value    # C update role: **(slot) = value

In expression position, field-follow and index chains are assembled before
prefix `@` and prefix `\`; parentheses override this order.

The last spelling is not the default parse. It is the explicit grouped form for a profile that needs the meaning "first raw-load `parent`, then field-follow `child` from the loaded typed address/reference".

In update/statement head position, the same leading prefix `\` may be used on
the left-hand target before `:`. The leading backslash run applies to the whole
following field-follow/index target and writes through the addressed cell:

    \slot: value
        Store `value` into the cell addressed by `slot`; C-profile role `*(slot) = value`.

    \\slot: value
        Store `value` through two address levels; C-profile role `**(slot) = value`.

This is still receiver/update semantics, not C assignment syntax. The colon
continues to introduce the Structure consumed by the update target. The active
L2 profile is responsible for type/address-depth validation.

Examples:

print: @count
print: leafs[0]
print: leafs[3]
print: \p
tree\branch: branch
root\branch: tree
print: tree\branch
print: @tree\branch
print: root\branch\branch

Meanings under the declarations in section 11.2:

    print: @count
        Print the address of the primitive storage location `count`.
        To print the primitive value `5`, write `print: count`.

    print: leafs[0]
        Perform ordinary checked ArrayReference indexing and print the first
        element. The ArrayReference is not converted to a raw address.

    print: leafs[3]
        Perform checked ArrayReference indexing and print element `3`.

    print: \p
        Raw-load the primitive cell addressed by the explicit typed-address
        primitive `p`. This operation does not apply to an ArrayReference.

    tree\branch: branch
        Store the `PlantBed` Structure reference `branch` in field `branch` of
        the Structure referenced by `tree`.

    root\branch: tree
        Store the `PlantBed` Structure reference `tree` in field `branch` of
        the Structure referenced by `root`.

    print: tree\branch
        Read the field `branch` through Structure reference `tree`.
        If that field contains a typed `PlantBed` address, `print` may render the whole object/tree addressed by that field.

    print: @tree\branch
        Print the address of the field slot `branch` inside the storage addressed by `tree`.
        This is parsed as `print: @(tree\branch)`.

    print: root\branch\branch
        Follow `root\branch` to the typed address stored there, then follow its `branch` field.
        If the final result is a typed `PlantBed` address, `print` may render that whole nested object/tree.

    \p: 9
        Store `9` into the primitive cell addressed by the explicit typed
        address `p`.

    \\pp: 10
        Store `10` into the primitive cell reached by two address levels from `pp`.

Field-follow and index chains bind tighter than prefix `@` and prefix `\`.

Therefore:

    @tree\branch

is parsed as:

    @(tree\branch)

and means the address of the field slot `branch` inside the storage addressed by `tree`.

Prefix raw-load is valid only when its operand is an explicit typed-address
primitive. It does not reinterpret a StructureReference or ArrayReference as
an address. A profile that exposes an Array's backing address must do so through
a separate explicit L2 adapter; only the adapter result may be consumed by
prefix `\` or raw address arithmetic.

At L1 lowering time, a receiver such as `print` receives primitives,
StructureReferences or ArrayReferences according to the resolved source
descriptor. For example, `leafs` may be printed as a whole array/string/buffer,
and `tree\branch` may yield another StructureReference for structural
rendering. The prefix raw-load form `\expr` instead consumes an explicit typed
address and produces the primitive cell/value loaded from it.

Function/subroutine address forms are strictly L2. A statically resolved L2
executable binding may be addressable through `@name` if the active L2 profile
exposes explicit callable-address metadata.

At L3, calls are receiver Frames resolved through receiver/operator bindings in
the current namespace, not raw callable addresses. Table entries are never
automatically callable bindings or L2 addresses.

The exact L2 memory profile must define primitive cell size, address arithmetic,
alignment, raw storage duration, allocation failure, callable-address
representation and which bindings are addressable through `@`. These choices
apply to explicit L2 machine storage such as `c.struct:`, raw indexed storage
and raw addresses. Ordinary Lingvamyxa Structures and Arrays are arena-owned
referents governed by section 19.29, even when their representation is projected
to ANSI C behind the L1/L2 boundary.

variant 1:
Treat `@ⁿ`, prefix `@`, prefix `\`, field-follow `\field` and the postfix
index suffix `target[...]` as built-in L2 parser forms. The ordinary `[]:`
Array constructor is a namespace-resolved L2/L3 receiver and is not part of
this L2-only list.

variant 2:
Treat them as bootstrap-registered L2 operators with parser support only for tokenization and precedence.

variant 3:
Allow both forms: parser-level recognition for core operators and receiver-level extension for specialized memory models.


12. Arrays, Text and Indexing

12.1. Logical Model

At L2 and L3, an ordinary array is an `ArrayReference`, distinct from a `StructureReference`.
The Array object/header and its owned element storage are allocated in the
selected arena. The logical array model includes at least length and element
storage and may include capacity, shape, rank, encoding, element descriptor and
stride information. Text is represented by an immutable Structure or Array
reference according to its namespace-visible descriptor.

12.2. L2 Lowering

At L2, ArrayReferences and text may lower to header pointers,
length-plus-address records or other layout-specific representations. This is a
backend projection, not a raw typed-address source value or a new category.

Example conceptual lowering for an array:

    (len, addr)

Lowering does not change source semantics.

12.3. Indexing

Operator-token consumption rules are specified by level and profile. Indexing is treated as a level-dependent operator/form.

At both levels, indexing an ArrayReference follows Array semantics and the
selected checked-bounds contract.

In a chain such as `value\field[index]`, unqualified newest-occurrence
field-follow is performed first and the postfix index is applied to the value
returned by that field-follow. The postfix index is not an ordinal selector for
repeated fields named `field`. Such an ordinal precedes the field name instead:

    value\[occurrence]field

Thus `value\[occurrence]field[index]` selects the occurrence first and indexes
the selected field value second.

Example conceptual Array meaning:

    beds[i] means index(beds, i)

The operation receives the ArrayReference, checks the admitted coordinates
against its metadata and returns the element or a bounds diagnostic.

L2 may separately index an explicit raw typed address. That is a low-level
address operation and is not ordinary ArrayReference indexing.

Example conceptual checked meaning:

    beds[i] means index(beds, i)

where `index` checks `0 <= i < length(beds)`.

?
The final array/text specification must define exact index result semantics, slicing, negative indexes if any, multidimensional arrays, text encoding and the boundary between parser form and receiver/operator form.
?

---ANSI C---
Possible array-lowering variants:

variant 1:
ArrayReference access lowers to computed-address access with generated bounds
validation.

variant 2:
Explicit L2 raw-address access lowers without Array bounds metadata.

variant 3:
Dynamic arrays lower to runtime handles with accessor calls when layout is not statically known.
---ANSI C---


13. Typed Field Follow

13.1. Backslash Structural Traversal Form

Structural traversal / dereference-like access uses backslash syntax:

    result\harvest

The backslash form is a built-in parser form. It exists so the translator can
generate direct structural traversal through resolved StructureReferences,
ArrayReferences where the descriptor admits a field-like view, and explicit L2
typed addresses. It is not a textual namespace separator and it is not
equivalent to the L2 foreign/substrate qualified-symbol form such as `c.printf`.

At L2, the left side may be a typed-address Primitive or an ordinary aggregate
reference. At L3, it is a StructureReference or an admitted ArrayReference. A
checked handle is either a Primitive accepted by a descriptor-specific
operation or a wrapper StructureReference; it is not another Value kind. In all
cases, `\` implies an actual traversable source-semantic referent, not a dotted
or slash-separated external name.

When the referent contains repeated exact field names, an unqualified
`value\field` selects the newest, last matching occurrence. A particular
occurrence is selected with:

    value\[occurrence]field

The occurrence expression is evaluated once and must produce a non-negative
integer Primitive. Exact-name matches are counted from zero in the Structure's
logical source/ordered order. A missing exact name or an ordinal outside the
matching occurrence count is a field-access error. It is diagnosed during
translation when statically known and by the checked runtime access otherwise.
The bracket before `field` is part of field-follow; a bracket after `field`
remains postfix value indexing.

The same `\` character also has a prefix L2 raw-load form, described in section 11.3. These forms are distinguished by position:

    \address      raw L2 load from an address expression
    value\field   typed field follow/access

Possible normal form:

    field(result, harvest)

or an internal field-follow expression Structure equivalent to it.

13.2. Lowering

If the model/layout of `result` is known, the translator may lower:

    result\harvest

into direct structure access, typed-address field-follow access or generated accessor code.

If the model/layout is dynamic, the receiver/runtime may perform a checked lookup.

The language permits the occurrence selector to be an arbitrary expression and
therefore permits runtime occurrence selection. The current hosted C bootstrap
projection implements occurrence-qualified field-follow only when the selector
is a compile-time-known non-negative decimal integer. If it cannot prove such
an ordinal, it must reject only that projection with a diagnostic such as:

    trans hosted C backend limitation: occurrence-qualified field follow requires a compile-time non-negative decimal ordinal

This is a hosted-backend limitation, not a restriction on Lingvamyxa source
semantics. A hosted projection must also reject a statically out-of-range
ordinal as a field-access error; it must not silently emit access to another
field.

!!!
Apart from the fixed missing-name and out-of-range behavior of the occurrence
selector above, some field-follow failure semantics are not fixed. A final
source-semantic rule must define behavior for unknown models, dynamic
Structures and nullable handles, including which failures are runtime errors or
receiver-defined diagnostics.
!!!

---ANSI C---
Possible field-follow lowering variants:

variant 1:
Compile-time-known descriptor shapes may lower to direct C struct-field access.

variant 2:
Known typed-address layouts lower to direct pointer-field access in ANSI C.

variant 3:
Dynamic Structures lower to checked runtime lookup or generated accessor calls.
---ANSI C---


14. Update, Return Structures and Destructuring

14.1. Plain-Name Rebind and Addressable Update Instead of `=` Assignment

The native structural core does not use `=` for assignment.

A declaration with initialization is written through a declaration-context constructor:

    int: a 3

A later plain-name rebind or an update of an addressable field/index location is
written by placing that target in head/update position:

    a: 4

Example:

    int: a 3
    print: a
    a: 4
    print: a

The first `print` observes `3`. The second `print` observes `4`.

An occurrence-qualified field-follow is an addressable field location:

    value\[0]field: replacement

This updates only occurrence 0 of the exact name `field`. The unqualified
`value\field: replacement` updates the newest occurrence. Neither form appends
a new field occurrence.

When a plain name such as `a` resolves as value rebinding, the old visible
descriptor and the pending value descriptor are checked with
`implements(old, new)`. Success appends a new binding occurrence named `a` and
makes it the result of ordinary lookup; failure changes nothing. This is not an
in-place mutation of the preceding namespace occurrence.

When the head is an addressable field/index location, the selected receiver may
instead mutate that storage location. Such mutation does not append a lexical
namespace occurrence. A visible function, subroutine or receiver may consume
the same surface form as a call. These cases are selected by ordinary namespace
and receiver resolution, not by a second assignment grammar.

For example, `i: 5` is parsed as a Frame with head `i` and argument `5`. A
translator resolves it while lowering statically known source; an interpreter
performs the same resolution against its current namespace state.

A bounded Structure or compact Frame in an argument/update position remains one
Structure after parsing. A nested compact Frame is lowered as a call only if its
head resolves in the ordinary namespace as a function, subroutine or registered
receiver. For example, in `print(1, 2, get(1,2))`, `get(1,2)` is just the third
field for P0; it is generated as a call only after `get` resolves as callable
during lowering.

This is declared head consumption, not automatic execution of every Structure in
an argument list.

At L2, an addressable mutation may lower to a direct storage write according to
the L2 memory model. At L3, addressable mutation is receiver-defined over
fields, handles and Structures. A plain-name rebind may be optimized to target
storage only when the observable ordered-occurrence semantics is preserved.

?
Initialization and addressable-mutation rules are not specified in full in this
document. Namespace redeclaration/rebinding and scope visibility are defined by
sections 7.3.1, 7.3.2 and this section; a future execution-model specification
may refine storage mutation without replacing those rules.
?

14.2. Return Structures

A function return value is one logical source-level value: Primitive,
StructureReference or ArrayReference. A structural value is an arena-owned
reference. If the return is an `fm` Structure, updating the fields of its existing
return Structure and returning one Structure reference whose descriptor
implements the declared return descriptor are source-semantically equivalent.

A function may return a Structure with ordered and/or semantic named fields.

Example return signature:

    (f32 harvest, f32 redApples, f32 greenApples)

Canonical positional structural return into an existing `fm` result Structure:

    return: bed\area 0 0

Named return variables may also be updated before an explicit terminal value return:

    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
    return: harvest redApples greenApples

The canonical return mechanism for an `fn` is `return: value`. In a valid
terminal trailer position, `return: value` also closes the `fn` source frame.
Named return variables are typed fields/bindings of an already existing result
Structure. The field updates occur before the return control exit; return itself
only forwards the result reference.

14.3. Destructuring

Destructuring syntax using `=` is not part of the current native structural core.

The semantic operation still exists: a produced Structure may be assigned or bound as one value, stored in a temporary service binding, or used to update target bindings from its fields by name or by position.

Possible explicit receiver form:

unpack: toEat(myFilter, Monday)
    harvest
    redApples
    greenApples
end: unpack

Conceptual meaning:

    tmp receives toEat(myFilter, Monday)
    harvest receives tmp\harvest
    redApples receives tmp\redApples
    greenApples receives tmp\greenApples

If the produced Structure has no field names, a receiver-defined positional unpack form may map fields by order.

?
The canonical destructuring/update syntax is not specified. A final execution-model specification must define whether destructuring is written through an `unpack` receiver, through target-list syntax without `=`, or through another explicit structural form.
?

---ANSI C---
A translator may implement destructuring by assigning the produced Structure or handle to a temporary service variable and then generating field updates one by one.

variant 1:
For compile-time-known descriptor shapes, generated code may read fields through
direct projected accessors from the temporary Structure reference.

variant 2:
For handle-based Structures, generated code reads fields through generated accessors or runtime lookup.
---ANSI C---

15. Structural Grammar Sketch

This grammar sketch describes the structural parser only. It does not define final receiver semantics.

15.0. P0 Source-Event Automaton

This subsection is normative for P0 structure recognition. The grammar below is
only a compact readable projection of the same machine.

P0 is one recursive source-event automaton over source positions:

    P0 = (S, E, delta, O)

where:

    S  is the parser state;
    E  is the stream of source events produced by the source scanner;
    delta consumes one event and updates the state;
    O  is the produced parser tree with source spans and node flags.

There is no separate line parser, brace parser, bracket parser or expression
parser at P0. Physical lines are input fragments, not containers in the
abstract tree. Parentheses, index brackets and MIX braces all enter nested P0
contexts; their difference is the channel where the resulting Structure is
used.

State:

    S.position
        current source position and absolute span base.

    S.lexical_mode
        SOURCE
        C_STRING
        EXACT_IDENTIFIER
        PYTHON_RAW_QUOTE
        BLOCK_STRING
        RAW_COMMENT
        LINE_COMMENT

    S.level_state
        the normalized source level state. Canonical dotted spelling maps
        leading dots to integer source levels. Relaxed indentation maps
        indentation columns to the same integer source levels through the
        indentation-stack rule.

    S.form_stack
        ROOT(level 0)
        VERTICAL(level K)
        SHORT(head at level N, implicit argument level N + 1)
        BOUNDED_PAREN(base level B)
        BOUNDED_INDEX(base level B)
        BOUNDED_MIX(local base level 0)

    S.output_cursor
        the current ordinary Structure insertion point, plus the current
        source span being attached to the next node.

Events:

    LEVEL(K)
        next source item is at normalized source level K.

    ATOM(value)
        ordinary token/value after operator splitting and payload recognition.

    HEAD(value)
        head token before `:`.

    COLON
        opens a short form for the preceding HEAD.

    NEWLINE
        physical newline separator.

    COMMA_SEPARATOR
        comma separator at the current scan depth. It separates fields like
        whitespace, but it has no indentation/source-level effect.

    SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR
        semicolon separator/trailer at the current scan depth. It may terminate
        the current open short form.

    OPEN_PAREN / CLOSE_PAREN
        `(` and matching `)` bounded Structure delimiters.

    OPEN_INDEX / CLOSE_INDEX
        `[` and matching `]` bounded index/access delimiters.

    OPEN_MIX / CLOSE_MIX
        `{` and matching `}` MIX bounded form delimiters.

    TAIL_CUTTER(candidate)
        a candidate visible close/cut form, such as `end`, `return`, `until`
        or a dash-fence delimiter. Candidate status is later validated by the
        current receiver/profile context.

    EOF
        end of source.

Lexical shielding:

    If S.lexical_mode is not SOURCE, braces, parentheses, brackets, commas,
    semicolons, colons, dots, indentation and tail-cutter words are payload
    characters for that lexical mode. They do not produce structural events
    until that lexical mode closes.

    C_STRING closes by its C string delimiter rules.
    EXACT_IDENTIFIER closes by its exact identifier delimiter rules.
    PYTHON_RAW_QUOTE closes by its matching raw quote run.
    BLOCK_STRING closes by its matching column-0 `===...` fence.
    RAW_COMMENT closes by its matching column-0 `***...` fence.
    LINE_COMMENT closes at physical newline.

    Therefore a `}` inside a string, exact identifier, raw string, block string
    or comment inside a MIX bounded form does not close that MIX bounded form.

Level transitions:

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K + 1))
        opens one Structure field under the current source parent. Portable P0
        source-level syntax opens block structure by one atomic level increase.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K))
        produces a sibling item at the current source level, unless the current
        scan depth is controlled by an open short/bounded form.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K - 1))
        closes the current visible vertical Structure by the ordinary one-step
        source-level cut.

    delta(LEVEL(K -> K - M), M > 1)
        is not ordinary block syntax. It is admitted only when the current
        source event is consumed by a valid tail-cutter layer for that context.

    An empty physical line is a level-0 boundary event. It may close an open
    level-1 vertical tail by the ordinary level decrease, but it is not a
    same-level delimiter below level 1.

Short-form transitions:

    HEAD COLON at source level N creates:

        Frame(head, BodyStructure at implicit argument level N + 1)

    Inline tail fields after `:` are appended to that BodyStructure at the
    implicit argument level N + 1.

    NEWLINE inside an open short form performs the synthetic short-form level
    step from N + 1 back to N. This step is only a separator step. It is not by
    itself the trailer of the short form.

    After that newline:

        if the next source item appears at level N + 1, the atomic level
        increase N -> N + 1 opens a new Structure field in the same short-form
        argument sequence;

        if the next source item appears at level N or smaller, the short form
        is complete and the next item is handled by ordinary sibling, cut or
        receiver-trailer rules.

    COMMA at the current scan depth separates fields and does not terminate an
    open short form. A single comma does not append an empty field. Repeated
    explicit separators may materialize positional skips where the consuming
    profile admits them.

    SEMICOLON at the current scan depth terminates the current open short form.
    It does not append a field to that short form. After terminating the short
    form, it separates the next source item/argument segment at the same scan
    depth. If no short form is open at that scan depth, it is an explicit
    source-item/argument-segment separator or a cosmetic trailing marker
    according to the surrounding context. Repeated explicit separators may
    materialize positional skips where the consuming profile admits them.

Bounded-form transitions:

    OPEN_PAREN starts a nested BOUNDED_PAREN context. Its contents are parsed by
    the same P0 source/token rules, with source-level composition measured
    relative to the bounded opener. CLOSE_PAREN closes that context. If a short
    form is still open inside the bounded context, it is completed as part of
    closing the bounded context.

    OPEN_INDEX after a target starts a nested BOUNDED_INDEX context. Its
    contents are parsed by the same P0 source/token rules. CLOSE_INDEX closes
    that context and attaches the resulting Structure as an index/access suffix
    to the preceding target or to a receiver form that admits bracket syntax.

    OPEN_MIX starts a nested BOUNDED_MIX context for a MIX bounded form `{...}`.
    Its contents are parsed by
    the same P0 source/token rules in a local level-0 context. CLOSE_MIX closes
    that context. The resulting Structure is stored as a MIX-flagged parser
    node with its source position/span. Ordinary structural consumers may skip
    MIX-flagged nodes; document/MIX consumers project them into active overlay
    intervals and marks.

    MIX bounded forms are then anchored to the surrounding source stream.
    Inline/tail MIX is recorded in the current field sequence. A leading MIX
    prefix before an ordinary item is recorded at that following item's source
    level before the item itself. A column-0 MIX-only physical line is queued
    as a margin MIX event and attached to the next ordinary LMX source item;
    several such lines keep their order. A non-margin standalone MIX line uses
    the ordinary source level of that line.

    A matching closing delimiter line may return to the opener level. The
    closer token itself is the bounded-form close event, not an ordinary
    sibling source item.

Tail-cutter transitions:

    Tail-cutters are not magic words in P0 by spelling alone. A token such as
    `return` or `until` becomes a tail-cutter only when it appears at a source
    event where a valid receiver/profile context admits that close/cut role.
    Otherwise it remains an ordinary item/operator/receiver token for later
    semantic processing.

    `end: name` is a visible structural close form. Its argument is not
    evaluated as a runtime expression. It is compared with the admitted close
    targets supplied by the receiver being closed: normally the privileged
    `name` argument as the canonical close target, and the receiver/head key
    as an admitted structural alternative.

    `return`, `return: value`, `until` and dash-fence delimiter forms are
    members of the same tail-cutter family, but each is admitted only in the
    receiver/profile positions defined for it.

EOF transition:

    EOF closes the root context after all open structures that may be closed by
    EOF have been completed. Any remaining open bounded form or lexical mode is
    a syntax error.

Output invariants:

    P0 constructs Structure, Frame, Atom/value and MIX-flagged nodes with source
    spans. It does not execute Structures, evaluate expressions, resolve
    functions, declare variables or decide type semantics.

    A named Structure is translated or interpreted as a call/function/subroutine
    only when a later semantic layer resolves its head through the active
    namespace. An anonymous Structure remains Structure data until a receiver
    consumes it under a receiver-defined rule.

    Any search for a matching `)`, `]` or `}` must use the same lexical modes as
    P0 itself. There is no separate opaque boundary walker that merely counts
    characters.

    program             ::= { empty_line | physical_item }

    physical_item       ::= raw_file_comment_block
                          | block_string_literal
                          | logical_line

    raw_file_comment_block
                        ::= raw_comment_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                              raw_ignored_line*
                            raw_comment_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            where 3 <= N <= 80

    raw_comment_fence(N)
                        ::= exactly N "*" characters at column 0

    block_string_literal
                        ::= block_string_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                              block_string_content_line*
                            block_string_fence(N) trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            where 3 <= N <= 80

    block_string_fence(N)
                        ::= exactly N "=" characters at column 0

    trailing_horizontal_space
                        ::= { " " | tab }

    horizontal_space    ::= " " | tab

    logical_line        ::= source_level line_content newline

    indentation         ::= { "." | " " | tab }

    source_level        ::= integer source level assigned by the
                            source-level normalization prepass

    source-level normalization prepass:
                            maps the physical source spelling to source-level
                            item/level events
                            before the structural grammar consumes it;
                            in canonical dotted notation, source_level is the
                            count of leading dot characters;
                            in relaxed indentation notation, source_level is
                            computed by the indentation-stack rule.

    empty_line          ::= trailing_horizontal_space newline
                            level-0 structural boundary event;
                            closes an open level-1 vertical tail by ordinary
                            source-level decrease, but is not a same-level
                            delimiter below level 1

    line_content        ::= dot_only_marker [ comment_body ]
                          | comment_body
                          | disabled_body [ comment_body ]
                          | line_item_sequence [ comment_body ]

    dot_only_marker     ::= empty content after indentation
                            valid only if source_level > 0;
                            may delimit/open an anonymous Structure at that level

    comment_body        ::= "#" ignored_until_newline

    disabled_body       ::= "%" enabled_line_body

    enabled_line_body   ::= line_item_sequence

    compact_frame       ::= name "(" [ argument_list ] ")"

    line_frame          ::= source_level line_item_sequence newline

    line_item_sequence  ::= source_item
                            { semicolon_segment_separator source_item }
                            [ semicolon_segment_separator ]

    source_item         ::= colon_frame_body
                          | nullary_line_body
                          | structural_value

    colon_frame_body    ::= head ":" [ line_arguments ]

    semicolon_segment_separator
                         ::= ";"

    // Semicolon terminates the current open short form/source item at the
    // current scan depth and separates the next source item or argument
    // segment. Comma is an ordinary field separator and never terminates the
    // current open short form.

    head                ::= bare_identifier
                          | exact_identifier
                          | symbolic_head

    nullary_line_body   ::= name
                            valid in executable statement position only when
                            `name` resolves to a zero-argument consumable binding:
                            registered nullary operator/receiver or visible zero-argument fn/sub

    line_arguments      ::= short_tail

    short_tail          ::= short_field
                            { short_tail_field_separator short_field }
                            [ "," ]

    short_tail_field_separator
                         ::= horizontal_space+
                          | ","

    short_field         ::= colon_frame_body
                          | structural_value

    // In ordinary physical short-line form, comma separates fields in the
    // current short form. Semicolon is not a short-tail field separator: it
    // terminates the current short form and exposes the following segment at
    // the surrounding scan depth.
    // Physical newline is not a short-form trailer; after newline, the next
    // source-level event decides whether the open frame receives more fields
    // or is completed by level rules.
    // Operator-token consumption is deferred to a later consumer.

    argument_list       ::= bounded_item_sequence

    bounded_item_sequence
                         ::= bounded_item { bounded_item_separator bounded_item }
                             [ bounded_item_separator ]

    bounded_item_separator
                         ::= horizontal_space+
                          | comma_field_separator
                          | semicolon_segment_separator
                          | newline/source-level composition

    comma_field_separator
                         ::= ","

    bounded_item        ::= colon_frame_body
                          | nullary_line_body
                          | structural_value

    // Inside bounded Structures, short colon-forms may appear inline.
    // Horizontal whitespace separates simple bounded fields only when no
    // short colon-form is open. Inside an open short colon-form it remains
    // the short-tail field separator. Comma is also a short-tail field
    // separator and never closes the short form. Semicolon terminates an open
    // short form at any scan depth and then separates the following bounded
    // item/argument segment.
    // Newline/source-level composition is not a short-form
    // trailer by itself; the following source level determines continuation
    // or closure. Inside a bounded form that continues across a physical
    // newline, the next physical line must remain inside that bounded form:
    // it must be at the corresponding deeper dotted source level or, in
    // relaxed notation, at an indentation column deeper than the line that
    // opened the bounded form. A line whose next source token is the matching
    // RPAREN/RBRACKET is the closing line and may return to the opener level.
    // The matching RPAREN/RBRACKET closes the bounded Structure; if a short
    // form is still open inside it, that short form is completed as part of
    // closing the bounded context.

    bounded_structure   ::= "(" [ argument_list | vertical_argument_list ] ")"

    source_fragment     ::= P0 source parsed in a local level-0 context

    mix_structure       ::= "{" source_fragment "}"
                            parsed by the same P0 source/token rules in a
                            local level-0 context and stored as a MIX-flagged
                            Structure node

    index_access_suffix ::= "[" [ argument_list | vertical_argument_list ] "]"
                            attached to a preceding target by an expression,
                            update or receiver profile that admits index/access
                            syntax

    vertical_argument_list
                         ::= newline source_line { source_line }
                            where each contained source_line follows the same
                            source-level rules as ordinary vertical composition

    structural_value
                         ::= compact_frame
                          | bounded_structure
                          | mix_structure
                          | atom_or_value

Square brackets in the portable core are not a free-standing replacement for
parentheses. `target[...]` is bounded index/access syntax attached to a preceding
target or to a receiver form that explicitly admits bracket syntax. `[]:` in
head position is a symbolic receiver spelling, such as an Array
constructor, not the same thing as an index suffix. A standalone `[a b]` has no
portable core meaning unless a profile explicitly admits it.

The vertical Structure tree is produced by source levels and explicit visible delimiters: following source items at one-step smaller source level as level delimiters, canonical dotted delimiter lines, relaxed dash-fence lines, and admitted source-level tail-cutter forms such as `end:` or terminal return where valid. The physical newline separator is not itself the trailer; it only helps expose the next source item and its level.

For a source item at level N, vertical child arguments are written at level N + 1. A following source item at level N + 1 is one positional child item of that frame/Structure. In a short colon-form, the inline tail fields are also treated as fields at the implicit argument level N + 1. A following source item deeper than N + 1 belongs to the immediately preceding child item only if that child item opens a Structure. Scalar literals and identifier values do not become headers merely because a deeper item follows.

A canonical dotted delimiter line or relaxed dash-fence line at level K may delimit an anonymous positional Structure at level K. If it is followed by source items at level K + 1, it opens such an anonymous Structure. The next delimiter at level K closes the previous anonymous Structure and may open another one when followed by deeper items. A following source item at level K - 1 closes that anonymous Structure by the ordinary one-step cut. A larger cut is not ordinary P0 syntax and requires an admitted tail-cutter layer.

An empty colon Frame is not rejected by P0 merely because its body has zero
fields. It is still a structural item. The active receiver, validator,
translator or profile decides whether a nullary colon Frame is meaningful in
that position. For example, a profile may reject `level0:` followed by sibling
`level1:` when `level0` requires a body, or reject `level0(level1:)` when the
inner empty Frame is not an admitted argument. Those are receiver/profile
diagnostics, not a separate P0 grammar category.

This grammar is intentionally incomplete around full update semantics, full indexing, full field-follow access, type constructors, literals and relation forms because those areas require final design decisions. Native structural grammar does not include `=` assignment, `=` named-field syntax, bare `end`, `end <target>`, bare non-nullary receiver/callable calls or a built-in `case` form. The standalone dash-fence line is part of vertical structure syntax as a nameless delimiter: it may open an anonymous positional Structure when followed by a deeper item, separate sibling anonymous Structures, or close/cut an anonymous/implicit section.

Ordinary line arguments are whitespace-separated short-tail fields. A parser must not use receiver signatures to insert hidden nesting while parsing `line_arguments`.

Comma and semicolon have different P0 roles at the current scan depth. Comma is an ordinary explicit field separator, like whitespace without indentation/source-level effects; it may add another field to an open short tail. Semicolon is the explicit separator/trailer: for a short form, it terminates that short form and never adds another field to the open short tail. When no short form is open at the current scan depth, comma is optional conventional separator notation and semicolon may act as an explicit source-item separator or trailing marker. A trailing comma or semicolon with no following item is accepted as a cosmetic trailing marker at any block level, including file/root level 0. A comma or semicolon inside a deeper bounded Structure or compact Frame belongs to that deeper form.

When a semicolon terminates a short form, it terminates/materializes the current
headless argument segment at that scan depth. If the neighboring segment has no
explicit head of its own, P0 may expose it as a separate headless Structure.
This preserves the source-item boundary for higher profiles. A
declaration/profile layer may then consume those headless Structures as repeats
of a preceding repeatable head, while an expression layer may reject them or
treat them as ordinary neighboring Structures. P0 does not silently append the
headless item to the terminated short tail and does not use namespace lookup to
decide whether the item is a call or a repeated declaration.

Repeated explicit separators at the same scan depth may materialize an empty
positional field. This is not specific to semicolon: `a,,c`, `a;;c`, `a,;c`
and `a;,c` all contain an explicit positional skip between `a` and `c` when the
current bounded/profile context admits empty positional fields. A leading
separator may similarly skip the first positional field. A single trailing
separator remains a cosmetic trailing marker. The parser records such a skip as
a positional-skip field; a receiver, validator or translator may consume it
only if the corresponding positional argument has an admitted default form.
Otherwise it must report an error rather than silently compacting the argument
list.

Structural grammar has no separate anonymous-function syntax. A bounded
Structure, compact Frame or vertical anonymous Structure remains Structure data.
The parser never marks a Structure as executable merely because it is nested
inside arguments. Translation or interpretation of that Structure happens only
when the active receiver consumes it under a receiver-defined rule, or when a
compact Frame head resolves through the namespace as a function, subroutine or
registered receiver.

Labels are not a separate structural grammar category. A source line such as `outerLoop:` is a colon frame with an empty inline tail and a required following vertical block. The active receiver/profile decides whether that frame introduces a local nullary executable binding, a control label role, both, or an error. A later bare line such as `outerLoop` is also not special label grammar; in executable statement position it may resolve through the ordinary namespace to that zero-argument label-block binding.

15.1. Structural Semantics in EBNF Form

The following EBNF describes the structural-semantic normal form produced by the
parser. It is not a separate surface syntax and it does not assign binding,
variable, type, field, atom, descriptor, operation or execution semantics. Those meanings
are assigned later by receiver consumption, explicit descriptors and the active
level/profile.

The notation uses `Structure(...)` and `Frame(...)` as semantic constructors:

    SourceFile
        ::= RootBlock

    RootBlock
        ::= Block(0)

    Block(K)
        ::= Structure({ BlockElement(K) })

    BlockElement(K)
        ::= FrameElement(K)
         |  ValueElement(K)
         |  ExpressionElement(K)
         |  AnonymousStructure(K)

    FrameElement(K)
        ::= Frame(Head, BodyStructure, FrameTrailer)

    Head
        ::= BareIdentifier
         |  ExactIdentifier
         |  SymbolicHead
         |  ProfileHeadPath

    BodyStructure
        ::= Structure({ Field })

    Field
        ::= FrameElement(current)
         |  ValueElement(current)
         |  ExpressionElement(current)
         |  BoundedStructure
         |  CompactFrame
         |  AnonymousStructure(current)

    ValueElement(K)
        ::= NumericLiteral
         |  StringLiteral
         |  BareIdentifier
         |  ExactIdentifier

    ExpressionElement(K)
        ::= ExpressionStructure

    CompactFrame
        ::= Frame(Head, BoundedBody, RPAREN_BOUNDED)

    BoundedStructure
        ::= Structure({ BoundedElement }, RPAREN_BOUNDED | RBRACKET_BOUNDED)

    BoundedElement
        ::= FrameElement(bounded)
         |  ValueElement(bounded)
         |  ExpressionElement(bounded)
         |  CompactFrame
         |  BoundedStructure

    AnonymousStructure(K)
        ::= Structure({ BlockElement(K + 1) })

This semantic EBNF is read with these structural rules:

    ShortTail
        ::= Field { (HorizontalSpace+ | COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR) Field }
            [ COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR ]

    BoundedBody
        ::= [ BoundedElement { BoundedSeparator BoundedElement }
             [ BoundedSeparator ] ]

    BoundedSeparator
        ::= HorizontalSpace+
         |  COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR
         |  SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR
         |  NewlineSourceComposition

    COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR
        ::= COMMA_SEPARATOR

    SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR
        ::= SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR

    SEMICOLON_SHORT
        ::= SEMICOLON_SEPARATOR

    FrameTrailer
        ::= SEMICOLON_SHORT
         |  RPAREN_BOUNDED
         |  RBRACKET_BOUNDED
         |  LEVEL_DROP
         |  DELIM_NAMELESS
         |  RECEIVER_TRAILER
         |  EOF_TRAILER

    RECEIVER_TRAILER
        ::= END_NAMED
         |  UNTIL_CLOSE
         |  TERMINAL_RETURN
         |  ProfileTrailer

Comma and semicolon semantics:

    COMMA_FIELD_SEPARATOR appends/separates fields in the open ShortTail. It
    never terminates the current open short form.

    SEMICOLON_SEGMENT_SEPARATOR first acts as SEMICOLON_SHORT if a short form
    is open at the current scan depth. It never appends a field to that
    ShortTail. After the short form is materialized, the same semicolon
    separates the following source item or argument segment.

    When no short form is open at the current scan depth, comma may act as
    ordinary separator notation and semicolon may act as an explicit source-item
    separator or cosmetic trailing marker.

    Therefore:

        call(cast: Type value, other)

    has one semantic argument segment for `call`, because `other` is still a
    field of the open `cast` short form. To end `cast` and supply `other` as the
    next argument segment, use semicolon:

        call(cast: Type value; other)

Physical newline semantics:

    Physical newline is not a short-form trailer. It ends the physical line.
    If the next source item is at the open short form's implicit argument
    level, that item continues the open short form. If the next source item is
    at the short form's own source level or smaller, the short form is complete
    and ordinary sibling/level-close rules apply.

Bounded delimiter semantics:

    RPAREN_BOUNDED closes the current parenthesized BoundedStructure or
    CompactFrame. RBRACKET_BOUNDED closes the current bracketed
