    BoundedStructure. If a short form is open inside that bounded form, that
    short form is completed as part of closing the bounded context, and then
    the bounded form itself is closed.

Source-level semantics:

    A line at source level K + 1 is a child field of the current item at level K.
    A line at source level K - 1 closes the current open Structure by the
    ordinary one-step cut. Larger decreases require an admitted tail-cutter
    layer.
    A delimiter at level K may open an AnonymousStructure(K) when followed by
    items at K + 1, or close/separate the current anonymous or implicit section
    at level K.

Receiver semantics:

    Frame(Head, BodyStructure, FrameTrailer) is still only data after parsing.
    The receiver named by Head consumes BodyStructure and assigns semantic roles
    to positional fields, anonymous Structures, named child Frames, labels,
    operators, declarations, model descriptors, message/table descriptors and control
    trailers. The parser does not infer those meanings from spelling alone.

16. Canonical Examples

16.1. Hello World

Source:

print: "Hello World"

Full form:

L3:
    print: "Hello World"
end: L3

Normal form:

L3(
    print("Hello World")
)

Shortest form:

print: Hello World

`Hello` and `World` are accepted only if the active L3 `print` receiver and lexical rules allow bare atoms/symbols in that position.

16.2. Function with Structural Return

Source:

fm: estimateHarvest (PlantBed(bed)) (f32(harvest), f32(redApples), f32(greenApples))
    if: bed\crop = Apples
        return: bed\area * 1.7 bed\area * 1.7 bed\area * 1.1
return: bed\area 0 0

Semantic outline:

    `fn` consumes a definition Structure.
    `PlantBed: bed` is a constructor Structure if `PlantBed` resolves to an
        admitted structural descriptor in the namespace.
    The return Structure has three named fields.
    `if` consumes condition and body Structures.
    The inner `return:` exits with the Apple-specific structural result.
    The final `return:` is terminal return. It supplies the default structural result and closes the function definition.

16.3. Explicit Return with Named Return Variables

Source:

fm: estimateHarvest (PlantBed(bed)) (f32(harvest), f32(redApples), f32(greenApples))
    harvest: bed\area
    redApples: 0
    greenApples: 0
return: harvest redApples greenApples

Semantic outline:

    Named return fields are local return variables.
    Head/update position updates existing bindings.
    Terminal `return:` exits, returns their current values as one return Structure and closes the function definition.

16.3.1. Recursive Function with Terminal Return

Source:

fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)
    if: n <= 1
        return: 1
return: n * factorial(n - 1)

Semantic outline:

    `return: 1` is a runtime return inside the `if` branch.
    In expression position, `factorial(n - 1)` is a recursive call.
    The final `return:` supplies the recursive result and closes the function definition.

17. Summary Table

    f(a, b)                    compact Frame named f
    f: a b                     line command Frame named f with whitespace-separated short-tail fields
    f: a, b                    f receives a and b; comma is an ordinary explicit field separator
    f: a; b                    f receives only a; semicolon terminates the short form, so b is the next source item at the current level
    f: 1 2                     ordinary short-tail fields, equivalent to f(1, 2)
    f: / child lines at . / end: f
                                canonical dotted vertical Frame named f; vertical fields are at source level N + 1
    f: / indented child lines / end: f
                                relaxed indentation vertical Frame named f
    (a b), (a, b)              bounded Structure; comma is optional conventional notation
    ()                         present empty bounded Structure; not a positional skip
    name: value                ordinary nested Structure, often consumed as a setting/semantic field by receiver
    name:                      empty colon Frame; P0 keeps it, receiver/profile decides whether it is valid
    leading dots               canonical visible source level units in the leading indentation zone
    indentation                relaxed source level units before ordinary source items
    dotted delimiter line      canonical anonymous Structure delimiter; may open, separate or close at its source level
    ---...                     dash-fence source-level marker, 3..80 `-` characters; materializes level K without an ordinary receiver item;
                               close/open/separator behavior follows from neighboring source levels
    smaller-level source line  ordinary one-step source-level cut/trailer; larger cuts require an admitted tail-cutter layer
    same-parent-level line     ordinary source-level cut for the previous child frame and start of the next sibling item
    empty line                 level-0 structural boundary; closes an open level-1 vertical tail
    #                          line comment marker; ignores text to the end of the physical line
    %                          disabled item/block marker; syntax is still parsed and checked
    {...}                      MIX bounded form; inline/tail belongs to the current item, leading prefix belongs to the following item,
                               column-0 MIX-only lines anchor to the next ordinary LMX source item
    ***...                     column-0 raw file comment fence, 3..80 `*` characters; closing fence must have the same length; syntax inside is ignored
    ===...                     column-0 block string fence, 3..80 `=` characters; closing fence must have the same length
    end: name                  explicit named tail-cutter for a visible frame/entity/label block; not the only close mechanism
    L2/L3                      the only level receiver frames
    sub                        receiver that explicitly binds subroutine definitions in namespace
    fn                         receiver that explicitly binds function/callable descriptors in namespace
    return                     executable-body return receiver/operator; also terminal executable close at definition level
    descriptor                 proposed explicit namespace declaration; no standalone `descriptor:` receiver is registered by the current bootstrap
    operator                   proposed explicit operator-descriptor declaration; current bootstrap derives initial bindings from legacy Tables
    if                         registered receiver, semantics depend on level
    match                      proposed receiver; not registered by the current bootstrap; no built-in `case` syntax
    merge                      registered head; current handler declares a composed namespace descriptor and emits only its hosted type/layout projection; it does not construct an instance and is not yet generic high-level Structure merge
    join                       registered Table receiver; normatively consumes explicit Tables and returns an arena-owned Table
    while                      registered receiver, semantics depend on level
    operator/infix tokens       structural operator fields consumed into expression Structures
    (): result                construct one arena-owned Structure and bind its StructureReference with a descriptor attached directly to that result binding
    []                         head receiver spelling for arena-owned Array forms; postfix `target[...]` is index/access
    @                          L2 address-of storage location
    \                          L2 prefix raw load, and typed field-follow separator in `a\field`
    table                      registered receiver constructing an ordinary Table value; legacy C/Registry projections remain implementation debt
    relation/rule/query        ordinary descriptor/Table vocabulary; no current generic receiver heads
    c.union                    registered L2 physical-layout receiver; generic union is not registered
    c.array                    registered L2 raw C-array storage receiver; ordinary [] constructs an ArrayReference
    method/requires.method     legacy bootstrap Tables; not normative implements input
    implements                 namespace-only structural compatibility receiver; translated or interpreted with the same predicate
    message/operation          ordinary Structures/descriptors in L2/L3; no current generic receiver heads


18. Remaining Open Points

This section keeps only the remaining open points that affect common syntax,
L2/L3 execution, expressions, arrays, text, field-follow, closure and ANSI C
projection variants.

18.1. Source-Semantic or Syntax Uncertainties

!!!
Field-follow traversal policy for unknown models, dynamic Structures and
nullable handles is not fixed. Missing exact names and out-of-range occurrence
ordinals already use the checked field-access rule above: they are diagnosed at
translation time when statically known and otherwise by checked runtime access.
!!!

18.2. External or Profile-Level Specification Areas

The exact operator token set and backend lowering rules may be extended by
profiles, but the ANSI C projection deliberately keeps C precedence and
associativity for primitive arithmetic, comparison and boolean expressions.

?
The L2 memory/operator specification must define exact semantics for `@`,
prefix `\`, field-follow `\field`, low-level addressable mutation, raw loads,
raw indexing and address arithmetic.
?

?
The array/text specification must define indexing, slicing, bounds errors,
multidimensional arrays, text encoding and dynamic storage behavior.
?

?
The execution-model specification must finish initialization, addressable
mutation, scope escape and outer-binding mutation rules without replacing the
plain-name rebind semantics defined in sections 7.3 and 14.1.
?

?
Closure representation for nested `fn` and `sub`, explicit capture, mutation of
outer bindings and escaping nested executables are not specified.
?

?
The general error/status system is not specified. Until specified, erroneous
results may be represented as ordinary values allowed by the declared return
type or by receiver-defined diagnostics.
?
18.3. ANSI C Implementation Variants

---ANSI C---
These variants are implementation notes for a possible ANSI C based translator.
They are not part of Lingvamyxa source semantics.

Call lowering:

variant 1:
Pass a small number of primitive/reference fields directly in internal generated C functions.

variant 2:
Pass an args Structure address/handle when the logical argument Structure is large or dynamic.

variant 3:
Generate specialized adapters, inline calls or eliminate physical args Structures when observable source semantics are preserved.

Return lowering:

variant 1:
Return a single primitive as a direct C primitive value.

variant 2:
Return an ordinary Structure or Array as the exact existing C reference/handle
representation that denotes it.

variant 3:
Return multi-field `fm` results through an arena-owned result StructureReference
created before function execution.

variant 4:
Create an `fm` result once in the callee prologue before body execution and
return that exact arena-owned reference from every return path.

variant 5:
Elide unobservable result-field traffic while preserving Structure identity and
the reference-only result contract.

No variant may allocate, copy, materialize or promote an ordinary Structure or
Array in the `return` path, and no ordinary aggregate may be returned as a C
aggregate value.

Destructuring lowering:

variant 1:
Store the returned Structure or handle in a temporary service variable and update target variables from its fields.

variant 2:
For known layouts, generate direct field-follow access.

variant 3:
For dynamic layouts, generate checked accessors or runtime lookup.

Field and array lowering:

variant 1:
Known field-follow access lowers to direct C struct or pointer access.

variant 2:
L2 indexing lowers to computed-address access under the L2 memory model.

variant 3:
L3 indexing lowers to checked access through array metadata such as `len`.

The language does not define C slot counting, hidden runtime addresses, hidden output addresses or C storage ownership rules. Those are translator/runtime design choices.
---ANSI C---

19. L2 & L3 Operators and Receiver Families

19.0. Scope of This Section

This section defines the L2/L3 source-level receiver/operator families for
executable, descriptor, data/query, message and service bodies. The first
entries are control-flow receivers; later subsections retain Registry/Table
facilities only as explicit ordinary values and operations.
`merge` is the intentionally broader structural receiver in this list. L2 and
L3 use the `merge` spelling for ordered Structure construction. Table profiles
use `join` to combine explicitly named Table values and produce an ordinary
result Table. A minimal bootstrap L2 profile may expose only the subset
listed in section 20.5, while richer L2 profiles and ordinary L3 profiles may
admit the full set or profile-specific extensions.

This shared list does not mean that L3 contains all L2 operators. L2 has an
additional low-level machine operator/parser-form surface in section 20. L3 is
the high-level subset and cannot use that machine surface. This is an
abstraction boundary, not a sandbox or authority boundary.

The control operators in this section are not raw low-level jumps. A translator
may lower them to branches, labels, hidden control-result Structures,
cleanup-stack operations or other implementation artifacts. Structural
operators such as `merge` may lower to compile-time Structure construction,
descriptor normalization or runtime Structure construction depending on the
active profile.

Unless stated otherwise, these operators have the same source semantics in L2
and L3 when the active profile admits them. Differences belong to profile
constraints, available value types, memory model and lowering policy, not to a
separate parser syntax.

Operator summary:

The left column below is a historical item label, not a subsection number.
Status words refer to the current bootstrap:

    IMPLEMENTED       the exact spelling and stated semantic contract exist;
    REGISTERED ONLY   the spelling resolves, but the stated contract is absent
                      or has different semantics;
    TABLE/DATA        ordinary Structure/Array-based data, not an operator or
                      a fourth Value category;
    NOT IMPLEMENTED   the following text proposes a concrete source contract.

Later subsection headings describe the full design families. They do not imply
that a source receiver exists; registration and contract status are stated
explicitly below.

    0.  if / else
        IMPLEMENTED statement receivers; L2/L3.

    1.  match / default-pattern receivers
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register `match:` as a statement/expression
        receiver consuming ordered pattern/body pairs; `default` remains an
        ordinary pattern atom.

    2.  while
        IMPLEMENTED statement receiver; L2/L3.

    3.  until
        REGISTERED ONLY as a generic trailer. The post-condition loop contract
        in section 19.4 is NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: give the trailer a
        loop-closing lowering that re-enters the closed frame while false.

    4.  for
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register a statement receiver consuming
        initialization, condition, step and body Structures.

    5.  each
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register a statement receiver over an array
        or iterator descriptor; L3 array traversal uses checked access.

    6.  return
        IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 executable-body receiver; terminal executable close
        is also implemented at definition level.

    7.  break
        IMPLEMENTED for the nearest loop frame; L2/L3.

    8.  continue
        IMPLEMENTED for the nearest loop frame; L2/L3.

    9.  finally
        IMPLEMENTED executable/scope cleanup receiver; L2/L3.

    10. guard / fail / throws / failure labels / handlers
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as recoverable failure flow. The registered `guard:` is
        an unrelated module/prelude guard. Proposed: register distinct
        failure-flow `guard:`, `fail:`, `throws:` and handler receivers producing
        and consuming a Failure Structure; do not overload the module guard.

    11. retry / retryable
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register retry-frame and retry-transfer
        statement receivers with explicit cleanup semantics.

    12. assert
        IMPLEMENTED diagnostic statement receiver; L2/L3.

    12a. log / error
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source receivers. Proposed: explicitly bind
        observation statement receivers whose descriptors select their
        handlers/policies. A descriptor may explicitly reference an observation
        Table, but no ambient table participates.

    13. redo
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register a loop-frame transfer that repeats
        the current body without advancing its source.

    14. yield
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: register a function-body receiver that emits
        an iterator value and persists the resume state in the iterator Structure.

    15. labelled break / labelled continue
        NOT IMPLEMENTED; current `break` and `continue` are unlabelled. Proposed:
        introduce the label by an explicit lexical declaration and resolve the
        optional target in the current namespace. Any backend label index is
        only an implementation of that namespace state.

    16. merge / join
        `merge:` is REGISTERED ONLY for the contract below: its current handler
        composes and binds a named structural descriptor and emits its hosted C
        type/layout projection. It emits no result-object storage and constructs
        no Structure instance. The ordered high-level Structure merge specified
        in section 19.17 is NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: keep descriptor/Structure
        merge (L2/L3) separate from optional physical layout projection (L2-only).
        `join:` currently has legacy source-row behavior; normative `join:`
        consumes explicitly resolved Tables and produces an ordinary arena-owned
        Table reference.

    17. table / source / import / predef
        `table:` constructs an ordinary arena-owned Table and may explicitly
        bind its result. `source` is a table-body projection/lifetime marker, not
        a receiver and not a RegistryView selector. Current static C and
        Registry-row projections are legacy implementation paths.
        `import:` is an IMPLEMENTED ordinary module import. `predef:` is an
        IMPLEMENTED hoisted import, but the current bootstrap excludes it from
        L3. Proposed: admit `predef:` on L3 surfaces too; a selected dependency
        retains its own level and exports only explicit namespace declarations;
        Table rows/cells are never imported as names.

    18. cast / implements
        `implements(CandidateName, RequiredName)` is IMPLEMENTED by the hosted
        translator for the minimal namespace-only predicate. Translation and
        interpretation use the same source-semantic algorithm: resolve both
        names in the namespace at the source point, read the descriptors carried
        by those bindings, and check that every first-level name exposed by the
        requirement is exposed by the candidate. Unresolved names or bindings
        with missing/malformed descriptors are diagnostics; field
        values, nested contents, `requires.method`, `method`, ordinary rows and
        `default` are not consulted.
        See section 19.21.3.
        `cast:` is REGISTERED ONLY for high-level conversion semantics: its
        current emitter lowers directly to a target-language C cast. Proposed:
        add method/codec-selected high-level conversion for L2/L3 and restrict
        raw representation/address casts to L2; the current L3 gate is missing.

    19. class / relation / rule / query
        `class` has no privileged core table meaning. A Table named `class` is
        ordinary data. `relation` and `rule` may be ordinary descriptor/data
        conventions. An expression `query(Table, coordinates...)` is NOT
        IMPLEMENTED generically; if added, it explicitly receives its Table and
        returns matching data, empty, or ambiguity.

    20. message / validate / execute / emit
        A message is TABLE/DATA: one LMX document whose root is the standard
        message envelope and whose `expression` is ordinary LMX. Its address is
        `endpoint + route`; the envelope fields are defined in section 19.19.1.
        The `send:` statement in item 27 is IMPLEMENTED for transporting these
        bytes, but it does not itself parse or validate the document.
        `validate`, `execute` and `emit` are NOT IMPLEMENTED as source
        operators. Proposed: register expression receivers
        `validate(Message, NamespaceSnapshot, ExplicitPolicies) -> ExecutionPlan | Failure` and
        `execute(ExecutionPlan) -> Result | Failure`, plus an `emit:` statement
        receiver that appends a proposed message to the current Message Thread
        outbox. These operations belong to L2/L3. The lower-level hosted runtime
        now has FIFO inbox/outbox byte queues and local route delivery, but that
        ABI does not itself parse, validate or execute the LMX document and does
        not make these source operators implemented.

    21. model / descriptor / union / c.union
        `model` is ordinary Structure data. A standalone `descriptor:`
        declaration receiver is NOT REGISTERED by the current bootstrap. Named
        Structure descriptor declarations and the current physical `merge:`
        path do attach normalized first-level descriptor views to their explicit
        namespace bindings and emit hosted type/layout projections without
        emitting Registry field rows or implicit instance storage. Broader
        descriptor content remains ordinary Structure/Array data interpreted by
        selected consumers.
        High-level `union:` is NOT IMPLEMENTED. A future variant
        declaration must create an explicit descriptor without choosing physical storage. The
        physical `c.union:` machine-layout receiver is IMPLEMENTED, but its
        required L3 exclusion is not yet enforced. `field.union` is a TABLE/DATA
        layout relation.

    22. operator / receiver / method
        There are no `operator:`, `receiver:` or `method:` declaration receivers.
        The current bootstrap derives initial bindings from `operator.*`,
        `namespace`, `receiver.*`, `method` and `requires.method` Tables. This is
        implementation machinery. A future declarative receiver must explicitly
        create descriptor bindings; it must not insert ambient rows as its
        language meaning.

    23. evidence / validator
        ordinary Structure/Array data, not source operators. Legacy
        `profile.validator` Table lookup exists, but
        the generic validation pipeline is NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: the
        `validate` operation from item 20 returns an ExecutionPlan containing
        validation/authority evidence, or Failure; no separate `evidence:`
        receiver is required.

    24. entry / provides / satisfies / conflict
        ordinary descriptor/data fields, not registered receivers. A program may
        cache `provides`/`satisfies` results in a Table, but `implements` computes
        compatibility from namespace descriptors. Namespace conflicts and Table
        merge conflicts remain distinct.

    25. procedural / object / functional / logical
        Consumption modes, not operator spellings. Proposed representation:
        an explicit receiver-descriptor field such as `consumptionMode`.
        Concrete actions remain ordinary resolved calls or explicit `validate`,
        `execute`, `query` and `derive` operations.

    26. provider / codec / toLmx / fromLmx
        provider/codec descriptors are ordinary Structures and generic provider
        selection is not yet implemented. They enter resolution only through
        explicit namespace bindings; an operation may use an explicit policy
        Table internally. Legacy `serialization.codec` lookup is IMPLEMENTED.
        `toLmx` and `fromLmx` are
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source operations; only internal table serialization
        helpers currently exist. Proposed generic methods:
        `toLmx(Value) -> Text | Failure` and
        `fromLmx(TargetDescriptor, Text) -> Value | Failure`, selected through
        visible callable/codec descriptors rather than parser primitives.

    27. stop / exit / send / reply / await / schedule / thread
        `stop:` is an IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 statement receiver. Its admitted forms
        are `stop:` and `stop: Status`; the optional expression is evaluated at
        the call site and converted to `int`, with omitted status meaning zero.
        It requests cooperative termination of the explicitly executing Message
        Thread only: RUNNING becomes STOPPING, but `stop:` does not itself leave
        the current fn/sub/block or skip its cleanup. The first accepted ordinary
        stop request retains its status; a later fatal diagnostic/runtime failure
        may override that ordinary stop status. The final turn runs local
        collection and then becomes STOPPED.

        `thread:` is an IMPLEMENTED top-level L2/L3 source receiver. Its current
        form is:

            thread: HandlerName (IncomingMessage: message)
                route: "/absolute/local/route"
                handler statements
            end: HandlerName

        The handler has exactly one named parameter with exact descriptor
        `IncomingMessage`; direct source calls of the handler are rejected.
        `route:` is required compile-time metadata, not an executable statement.
        The route is a double-quoted printable-ASCII absolute path beginning
        with `/` and containing no backslash, query or fragment. Duplicate
        routes in one application translation are rejected. A handler may be
        declared directly or supplied by ordinary `import:`. Executable thread
        declarations in `predef:` modules and conditional `os`/`ifdef`/`guard`
        branches are rejected by the current startup profile.

        Each declared handler is materialized as one mailbox Message Thread.
        One invocation handles the one LMX document dequeued for that turn.
        `IncomingMessage.lmx` is a borrowed `const char *` view and
        `IncomingMessage.length` is its byte length; both remain valid only for
        the handler turn. The generated application entry always constructs a
        runtime and attaches its root; when at least one handler is declared it
        also constructs a pool, starts every handler and binds its route.
        Teardown drains cascaded committed work before stopping and joining the
        handlers.

        `send:` is an IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 statement receiver in these bootstrap
        forms:

            send: Route LmxBytes Length
            send: Endpoint Route LmxBytes Length

        The arguments are evaluated exactly once, from left to right. `Route`,
        `Endpoint` and `LmxBytes` lower to byte-addressable C strings in the
        current hosted profile; `Length` is converted to `size_t`. The first
        form uses the current application endpoint. The second admits only an
        absolute HTTP(S) endpoint. A send copies the complete LMX document into
        the current Message Thread's FIFO outbox. It is committed at the common
        end-turn boundary and does not perform a hidden return. An immediate
        address error or a commit-time delivery error requests failure of the
        sending Message Thread.

        The explicit address operands are temporary bootstrap routing
        projections of the envelope's `to.endpoint` and `to.route`; they are not
        a second address model. `LmxBytes` must still contain the complete
        standard message envelope. Until envelope parsing is inserted, the
        caller is responsible for making the explicit projection agree with
        `to`. The validation stage must eventually reject a mismatch.

        Local sends resolve `Route` in the runtime route table. A missing route
        fails with `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_ROUTE_NOT_FOUND` (64). An HTTP(S) send is
        retained as a remote address but, until the REST/LMX provider is
        configured, fails with
        `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_TRANSPORT_PROVIDER_NOT_CONFIGURED` (65). Invalid
        address syntax fails with `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_INVALID_ADDRESS` (66).
        A configured provider reports a native transport failure as
        `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_TRANSPORT_FAILED` (67), a syntactically valid HTTP
        response which does not admit the message as
        `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_HTTP_REJECTED` (68), and an invalid provider result
        as `LM_MESSAGE_STATUS_TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_ERROR` (69). HTTP 2xx admits
        the transport delivery; HTTP 404 maps to the same route-not-found
        status 64 as local lookup. Raw HTTP status numbers never become actor or
        process exit statuses.

        `exit:` is an IMPLEMENTED L2/L3 application receiver:

            exit:
            exit: StatusExpression

        The omitted status is zero; the optional expression is evaluated
        exactly once and converted to `int`. The first accepted application
        exit request selects the ordinary exit-request status. `exit:` is
        cooperative and does not perform a hidden return, jump or lexical unwind: the
        current fn/sub/handler continues through its visible source control flow
        to the common turn boundary. It is available wherever the current
        MessageThread context is explicitly propagated, including managed
        nested functions, but is rejected in an unmanaged external function.

        Request and shutdown are separate phases so an actor never joins
        itself. The requesting Message Thread becomes STOPPING, finishes its
        current outbox and collection boundary, and only then publishes the
        application exit as ready. A delivery or collector failure in that
        requester replaces the requested ordinary status with the requester's
        effective failure status. Once ready, the application controller stops
        the pool and root, waits for declared actors/workers, releases the pool,
        detaches/releases the root and deletes the runtime before returning to
        the host. An actor callback only requests this sequence; it never runs
        join/delete itself.

        Host-process status keeps the existing failure-preserving priority:
        a nonzero effective root status wins; otherwise a nonzero selected
        application exit status wins; otherwise the first nonzero declared
        actor status wins; cleanup failure supplies `1` only if all earlier
        choices were zero. Thus `stop: 3` followed in the same root turn by the
        first `exit: 5` records exit status `5` without rewriting the root's
        already selected status `3`, and the host returns `3`.

        The requester outbox is committed before the shutdown cut. Deliveries
        from other turns are linearized by the runtime mutex: entries admitted
        before the cut follow normal FIFO delivery, while entries that reach
        transport after the cut are cancelled as shutdown work without creating
        a spurious route/transport failure. The current generated application
        has one runtime and at most one application pool. Coordination of
        several independently constructed low-level runtimes/pools remains the
        responsibility of their explicit host controller.

        `reply`, `await` and `schedule` are NOT IMPLEMENTED as source receivers.
        Proposed contracts: `reply(Value | Failure)`;
        `await(Future) -> Value | Failure`; and `schedule(Message, Time)`.

        The explicit host runtime, worker-pool and actor start/join ABI is
        IMPLEMENTED. Its mailbox subset includes mailbox actor start, local
        route binding, owned-LMX FIFO inbox/outbox delivery and current-message
        access during a handler turn. `thread:` now exposes actor construction,
        binding and one-message-per-turn dispatch. Source `send:` now lowers
        over the same ABI.

    28. copy / move / borrow / own
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source operators; only lower-level ownership/runtime
        helpers exist. Proposed: resolve them through explicitly bound operation
        descriptors whose signatures accept the relevant references/arenas.
        High-level operations may be L2/L3; raw allocator/address operations
        remain L2-only.

    29. watch / derive
        NOT IMPLEMENTED. Proposed: `watch(Query, Handler) -> Subscription` and
        `derive(Query, Rule) -> candidate rows/messages`. `reactive` is a
        profile/mode value, not a separate operator.

    30. service / route / HTTP adapter
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source receivers. Proposed: `service:` materializes a
        service descriptor and binds it explicitly; `route` explicitly updates
        the route-map value it receives. HTTP remains a provider/transport
        adapter selected through a visible descriptor, not a core operator.

    31. authenticate / verify / authorize / ticket
        NOT IMPLEMENTED as source receivers. Proposed expressions:
        `authenticate(Credential, Verifier) -> AuthEvidence | Failure`,
        `verify(Claim, Verifier) -> Evidence | Failure`, and
        `authorize(Evidence, Operation) -> Allowed | Failure`. Ticket creation
        and revocation operate on explicit Ticket Structures; `ticket` itself
        is not an operator.

Current bootstrap receiver/operator summary:

The following list contains exact spellings registered by the current L2
translator. It is an implementation baseline, not a claim that every later
profile family is already a core receiver.

    substrate receivers:   L1
    level receivers:       L2, L3
    definition receivers:  fn, callable, fm, sub
    control statements:    if, else, while, return, stop, break, continue,
                           synchronized, finally, assert
    declarations/values:   descriptor, const, (), [], None, OK, false, true
    module/source:         table, import, predef, join, merge
    machine/build receivers (intended L2-only; current L3 gates incomplete):
                           include, define, ifndef-default, os, ifdef, guard,
                           extern_c, c.struct, c.union, c.enum, c.array
    expression receivers:  cast: TargetDescriptor expression
                           implements(CandidateName, RequiredName)
    modifier/trailers:     external; end, until
    prelude atom consumers:
                           include, define, os, ifdef, fn, layout, constant,
                           alias, forward, prototype, guard, extern_c, unit

The current `merge` handler consumes layout-describing sources, publishes a
composed descriptor under its explicit target name and emits that descriptor's
hosted type/layout projection. It never emits an implicit target object or
materializes a Structure instance; a value must be created separately through
an explicit constructor. It does not yet implement the generic high-level merge
contract. Current `join` still composes legacy source rows; it must become
ordinary Table-to-Table composition. The current bootstrap still lists `predef`
in `receiver.l3.exclude`. Its local-source early prepass scans only implicit or
explicit L2 surfaces, although ordinary declaration can still materialize
table/join on an explicit L3 surface. Hoisted implicit-`.lm3` handling and `.lm3`
predef targets remain implementation work. These are implementation gaps, not
intended level restrictions. The built-in named-layout fallback remains
implementation debt; physical C layout is only a backend projection.
Named-Structure descriptor declarations and physical `merge:` declarations no
longer publish implicit owner or field Registry rows, instances or object
storage.

The exact `c.array` receiver is registered as an L2-only machine-storage
receiver and is rejected on an active L3 surface. Its nested bracket Frame is a
raw C declarator consumed by `c.array`; it is not dispatched as the ordinary
`[]` ArrayReference constructor.

The current expression operator tables register these exact families:

    prefix:   ++, --, +, -, @, \, !, ~
    postfix:  ++, --
    infix:    +, -, *, /, %, =, !=, <, <=, >, >=, &&, ||,
              &, |, ^, <<, >>, \
    index:    [, ]
    atoms:    ., None, OK, false, true

Tables and profile vocabulary are not source operators merely because they have
names. The current bootstrap currently uses the following Tables to construct
part of its initial namespace and backend configuration:

    class / class.kind / class.present
        legacy classification data; not core class semantics;
    method / requires.method
        legacy data relations; the current `implements` receiver does not read
        them;
    namespace / receiver.*
        legacy input used to build explicit receiver bindings;
    operator.prefix / operator.postfix / operator.infix / operator.index
        expression spelling and emitter tables;
    serialization.codec / profile.validator
        codec and validation metadata tables.

Items marked TABLE/DATA above describe ordinary keys, table patterns, result
Structures, runtime roles or proposed profile vocabulary. They do not become
operators merely because they have names. The registered low-level spelling is
`c.union:`, not a generic `union:` receiver. `toLmx` currently appears only in
internal table-serialization helper names and `fromLmx` is absent; neither is a
registered operation-table method yet. A profile makes a proposed term
executable only by explicitly installing an exact receiver binding and its
validation/lowering descriptor.

The current bootstrap implements only unlabelled `break` and `continue`.
Labelled control may be added as a profile extension by registering ordinary
frames such as `outerLoop:` as control targets, but that mechanism is not part
of the implemented summary above.

19.1. `if` and `else`

`if` is a statement receiver. It consumes a condition expression and a statement
Structure for the true branch. `else` is a separate sibling statement receiver.
It may immediately follow an `if` at the same statement level and consumes the
false-branch statement Structure.

Semantic form:

    if(condition, body)
    else(body)?

Compact sibling form:

if: condition
    trueBody

else:
    falseBody

Explicit named-close form, useful for long blocks or diagnostics:

if: condition
    trueBody
end: if
else:
    falseBody
end: else

Nested form:

    if: condition
        trueBody

    else:
        falseBody


Single-line form:

    if: condition doSomething

The condition is evaluated once before branch selection.

Only the selected body is executed.

If no following sibling `else` receiver is provided and the condition is false, `if` completes normally without executing a body.

`else` is not parser syntax and not a reserved P0 word. It is an ordinary
namespace receiver whose registered statement semantics are meaningful when it
appears as the following sibling of an `if` in the selected profile. If another
statement separates `if` and `else`, the pair is not adjacent; the target
projection or profile validator may reject the resulting control flow.

19.2. `match` and default-pattern receivers

`match` selects one body from a set of positional pattern/body pairs or from anonymous branch Structures. `case` is not a structural keyword.

Simple positional example:

match: value
    1
    print: one
    2
    print: two
    default
    print: other
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(value, 1, print(one), 2, print(two), default, print(other))

The `match` receiver may interpret the tail after `value` as repeating `(pattern, branch)` pairs. `default` is an ordinary atom/receiver-defined pattern value, not a parser keyword.

Block branch example:

match: value
    1
    ---
        print: one
        log: matched one
    ---
    2
    ---
        print: two
        log: matched two
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(
      value,
      1,
      (print(one), log(matched one)),
      2,
      (print(two), log(matched two))
    )

Anonymous branch Structure example:

match: value
    ---
        1
        print: one
    ---
        2
        print: two
    ---
        default
        print: other
    ---

Conceptual argument stream:

    match(
      value,
      (1, print(one)),
      (2, print(two)),
      (default, print(other))
    )

There is no automatic fallthrough from one branch to the next unless the active `match` receiver explicitly defines such behavior.

For union values, a `match` may destructure the active variant by ordinary pattern Structures.

Example:

match: shape
    ---
        Circle c
        drawCircle: c
    ---
        Square s
        drawSquare: s
    ---

If the matched value is a closed union and no default pattern is present, an implementation may require exhaustive patterns.

19.3. `while`

`while` is a pre-condition loop.

Semantic lowering shape:

    while(condition, statementStructure)

Example:

while: i < n
    print: i
    i: i + 1


The condition is evaluated before each iteration.

If the condition is false before the first iteration, the body is not executed.

A `while` body is a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

19.4. `until`

`until` is a conditional close for the current executable block. It closes the current block, executes that block as a post-condition loop body, then evaluates its condition.

Semantic form:

    until(currentStatementStructure, condition)

Labelled example:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

In this example, `until:` closes the labelled executable block `readLoop`. The condition is the boolean-producing Structure `endOfInput(record)`. The name `readLoop` is also the local nullary executable binding for the block, as described in section 7.6.

The body is executed first. The condition is evaluated after the body.

The loop repeats until the condition is true. If the condition is false, control transfers to the beginning of the block closed by `until`.

`until` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

19.5. `for`

`for` is a controlled loop receiver for numeric ranges, counters, index ranges or other bounded iteration policies.

Compact counter/triplet form:

for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++
    print: i


The first three arguments are consumed as loop-control fields:

    init      -> initialization Structure
    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    step      -> iteration-step statement Structure

The vertical child Structure is consumed as the loop body.

Single-line compact body form:

    for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++ print(i)

In this form, `print(i)` is the fourth argument. The `for` receiver signature
consumes that fourth argument as its statement tail, so it lowers as the loop
body. The token form `i++` is valid only in profiles that register it as an
operator-token form.

Range-style example:

for: i 0 n
    print: i


Semantic requirement:

    `for` creates one or more loop-local iteration bindings.
    `for` advances those bindings according to the selected control policy.
    `for` executes the body once for each produced iteration state.

`for` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

?
The compact counter/triplet form is specified above. Other source forms for numeric ranges, inclusive/exclusive bounds, step values, reverse iteration, index/value pairs and destructuring of iteration bindings are not specified in this section.
?

variant 1:
Use compact positional forms such as `for(i, 0, n)`.

variant 2:
Use explicit short-tail fields such as `for: i 0 n 1`, or use a future exact-identifier/profile descriptor only when the `for` receiver explicitly defines that descriptor shape.

variant 3:
Treat all range policies as ordinary receiver Structures consumed by `for`.

19.6. `each`

`each` iterates over a container or a Structure whose descriptor implements the
iterator requirement exposed by the resolved `each` receiver.

Array-like example:

each: item in items
    draw: item


Receiver-like example:

each: items item
    draw: item


Semantic requirement:

    `each` obtains a sequence of values from its source.
    For each value, `each` binds the iteration variable or variables.
    `each` executes the body once for that value.

`each` creates a loop frame. `break`, `continue`, `redo` and labelled loop transfer operators may target it.

For arrays or array-like containers, `each` may lower to an index loop.

After that first-level-name check, the `each` receiver separately consumes the
iterator's `next(env)` operation until no value is produced. Failure to consume
that call is an ordinary receiver error, not another compatibility relation.

In the source form:

    each: item in source

the `source` field is consumed by the `each` receiver as an iteration source. If
`source` resolves to a container, `each` iterates over that container. If its
descriptor implements the iterator requirement, `each` consumes the Structure
as iterator data. If `source` resolves to a visible zero-argument iterator
function, `each` may consume the callable binding by creating its iterator
state, equivalent to the explicit compact form `source()`.

Therefore both forms are valid for a zero-argument iterator source:

    each: word in words
    each: word in words()

The first form lets `each` consume the callable binding as an iterator source. The second form explicitly creates the iterator state Structure before `each` consumes it. This is receiver consumption of an already parsed field; it is not hidden receiver-signature grouping.

Iterator consumption by `each` is specified in section 19.15.5.

19.7. `return`

`return` exits the current `fn`, `sub` or executable-like body.

Runtime forms inside an executable body:

    return
    return: value

For an `fn`, `return: value` consumes a Primitive, StructureReference or
ArrayReference whose descriptor implements the declared return descriptor and
exits. For a Structure or Array result it forwards the exact reference under
section 8.8. A bare `return` is not a valid `fn` return in the core profile.

For a `sub`, `return` exits the `sub` body. `return: value` is invalid for `sub` unless a later profile defines status/result subroutines.

At a valid terminal trailer position for the open executable definition, an admitted return form is terminal return. It appends the corresponding runtime return as the final body item and closes the executable definition. For an `fn`, this terminal form is `return: value`; for a `sub` or no-value label/procedure executable, this terminal form may be bare `return`. This is an executable-specific close, not a general replacement for `end: name`.

For a definition opened by `fn: name` or `sub: name` at level N, the source level of the definition is N. An admitted return form at level N is the common terminal structural trailer. The same return form at level N + 1 or deeper is an ordinary runtime return inside the body. A return form at a smaller source level may still be terminal return when the normal source-level cut reaches the visible executable tail and the intervening frames admit that closure. See section 8.3.

Examples:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
return: x * x

fn: factorial (int(n)) (int)
    if: n <= 1
        return: 1
return: n * factorial(n - 1)

Before control leaves the current executable frame, all `finally` cleanups registered for the exited scopes are executed in reverse registration order.

The current hosted bootstrap has one process-entry compatibility rule. A
`return` in the generated external `main` evaluates its status exactly once,
runs the generated cleanups for the active scopes, requests that the root
Message Thread stop with that status, and transfers to the common end-turn
boundary. It therefore ends the root loop without bypassing its final local
collection pass. This special lowering applies only to the external process
entry. A `return` in every nested or ordinary fn/sub retains the semantics
above and does not stop a Message Thread. See section 19.28.2.2.

19.8. `break`

`break` exits the nearest active loop frame.

Example:

while: running
    if: stopRequested
        break
    ---
    step


`break` is valid only inside a loop frame unless a labelled form is used as described in section 19.16.

Before control leaves the exited loop body scopes, all affected `finally` cleanups are executed in reverse registration order.

`break` is not a source-level raw jump. It is a structured loop-frame exit.

19.9. `continue`

`continue` starts the next iteration of the nearest active loop frame.

Example:

each: item in items
    if: skip item
        continue
    ---
    process: item


For `while`, `continue` transfers control to the next condition check.

For `until`, `continue` transfers control to the post-body condition check for the current iteration.

For `for`, `continue` transfers control to the next iteration-state update.

For `each`, `continue` transfers control to the next container or iterator value.

Before control leaves the current loop body scopes, all affected `finally` cleanups are executed in reverse registration order.

19.10. `finally`

`finally` registers cleanup code in the current execution context.

Block form:

finally:
    cleanupBody


Single-line form:

    finally: cleanupCall

`finally` is not part of a `try` block in this specification. It is a standalone cleanup registration operator.

A cleanup registered by `finally` is associated with the current executable/scope frame.

Cleanups are executed when their associated frame or scope is exited by:

    normal completion;
    return;
    break;
    continue;
    labelled break;
    labelled continue;
    fail;
    retry;
    redo;
    assert diagnostic exit;
    thread/task termination in the default runtime profile.

Cleanups are executed in reverse registration order.

A cleanup body is executed outside the registration operation. It is not executed at the point where `finally` is encountered, except when the frame exits immediately after registration.

`finally` cleanups are part of the Message Thread execution-context model in
section 19.28.

!!!
The exact semantics of a cleanup failure are not fixed. A final runtime specification must define what happens if a cleanup itself executes `fail`, `assert`, `return`, `break`, `continue`, `retry`, `redo` or another `finally` registration while cleanup processing is already active.
!!!

19.11. `guard`, `fail`, `throws`, failure labels and handlers

`guard` and `fail` define declared recoverable failure flow.

They are distinct from `assert`.

    guard / fail:
        declared, expected, recoverable failure path

    assert:
        diagnostic invariant violation

19.11.1. `throws`

An executable definition may declare failure labels with `throws`.

Example:

fn: parseInt (Text(text)) (int)
    throws:
        ParseError
    ---

    guard: validInteger text
        fail: ParseError text
    ---

    parseInt: parseInteger text


`throws` lists the failure labels that may leave the executable body as recoverable failures.

A caller must handle, propagate or remap a declared failure.

19.11.2. `guard`

`guard` executes its failure body when its condition is false.

Example:

guard: radius > 0.0
    fail: InvalidRadius radius


Semantic form:

if: not condition
    failureBody


`guard` is a convenience control receiver. It does not itself define the failure transport mechanism. That is defined by `fail`.

19.11.3. `fail`

`fail` exits the current executable body with a declared failure label and optional payload.

Example:

    fail: ParseError text

Semantic effect:

    set failure label to ParseError;
    set failure payload from the remaining arguments;
    execute affected finally cleanups for the current frame exit;
    return a structured failure result to the caller or visible handler.

`fail` is part of the declared `throws` set. It is not a diagnostic abort.

`fail` does not transfer to the thread diagnostic root.

`fail` does not use the `assert` channel.

19.11.4. Failure labels and handlers

Failure labels are ordinary source labels consumed under failure-control semantics. A profile may keep a separate lookup table for diagnostics and control transfer, but the surface grammar is still the same colon-frame grammar.

A handler consumes a failure label and payload.

The exact handler surface syntax is not finalized in this section.

variant 1:
Use label-like handler syntax:

ParseError:
    Text: badText

    print: badText


variant 2:
Use explicit receiver syntax:

handler: ParseError
    Text: badText

    print: badText


variant 3:
Represent handlers as L3 event/message handlers for failure events.

!!!
The exact source syntax, placement, visibility and lifetime of failure handlers are not fixed. A final source specification must define whether handlers are lexical Structures, executable labels, L3 event/message handlers, caller-side receivers or a combination of these forms.
!!!

19.11.5. Failure propagation

If a failure is not handled in the current body, it must be propagated by an enclosing or calling executable whose `throws` declaration includes the failure label.

Failure propagation is structured. It is not a raw jump through arbitrary stack frames in the source semantics.

---ANSI C---
A translator may lower declared failures using:

variant 1:
Return-status wrappers.

variant 2:
Status return plus output parameters.

variant 3:
A hidden control-result channel.

variant 4:
A non-local transfer mechanism for a selected runtime profile, provided that Lingvamyxa `finally` semantics are preserved.

The strict default for this draft is structured failure propagation, not root-level `longjmp`.
---ANSI C---

19.12. `retry` and `retryable`

`retryable` defines a restartable frame.

`retry` restarts the nearest or named retryable frame.

Example:

loadAttempt:
    retryable:
        loadNetworkData
    ---

Possible failure-handler use:

NetworkError:
    reconnect
    retry: loadAttempt


A `retry` transfer executes the affected `finally` cleanups for the abandoned attempt before re-entering the retryable frame.

`retry` does not roll back external side effects. It only repeats the source frame according to the retryable-frame semantics.

Local bindings declared inside the retryable frame are re-created when the frame is re-entered.

Outer bindings keep their current values unless the retryable receiver defines a stronger rollback policy.

`retry` without a label targets the nearest active retryable frame.

`retry: labelName` targets the named retryable frame.

!!!
The exact interaction between `retry`, failure handlers, iterator frames, partially executed cleanups and mutable outer bindings requires a final rule. This draft defines retry as re-execution, not as transactional rollback.
!!!

19.13. `assert`

`assert` checks a diagnostic invariant.

Example:

    assert: index >= 0
    assert: index < values\len

If the condition is true, execution continues normally.

If the condition is false, the current execution/checking phase fails with an `AssertionViolation` diagnostic.

`assert` is not recoverable `fail` control flow.

`assert` is not part of the declared `throws` set.

`assert` is not caught by failure labels or failure handlers.

Default L3 runtime semantics in the Message Thread profile defined in section
19.28:

    record AssertionViolation diagnostic;
    execute all active `finally` cleanups in the executing Message Thread's
        ExecutionContext;
    transfer control to the thread diagnostic root;
    terminate the executing Message Thread/task by default after reporting the
        diagnostic.

Default L2 descriptor/configuration-loading use, if exposed by a receiver profile:

    an assertion violation terminates the current model-loading or translation pass with a diagnostic error.

`assert` is intended for impossible states, broken invariants, corrupted internal assumptions or translator/model errors. Expected input failure must use `guard` and `fail`.

19.13.1. `log` and `error`

`log` and `error` are observation receivers.

Canonical forms:

    log: value
    error: value

The tail may be a constant/literal value or a name visible in the active local
namespace view. The receiver consumes the resolved value and records it through
the active observation profile.

Examples:

    log: "matched one"
    log: matchedValue
    error: "parse failed"
    error: diagnostic

`log` records an ordinary observation, trace, note, audit event, metric payload
or profile-defined informational record.

`error` records an error/diagnostic observation. It is not reserved parser
syntax and it is not automatically equivalent to `fail`, `assert`, `return` or
thread termination. If a profile wants an error observation to also fail,
abort, throw, send a failure message or terminate a task, that behavior must
be defined by that profile or by an explicit surrounding receiver.

Both receivers use ordinary namespace resolution for non-literal tails. A bare
unknown name is not magically logged as text unless the active profile admits
such symbol logging explicitly. Write a string/exact identifier when the source
wants a literal textual record.

19.14. `redo`

`redo` repeats the current loop iteration without advancing the loop source.

For `each`, `redo` repeats the body with the same current element.

For iterator-based `each`, `redo` does not call `next(env)` before repeating the body.

For `for`, `redo` repeats the body with the same loop bindings.

For `while`, `redo` repeats the body without re-evaluating the condition first.

For `until`, `redo` repeats the body without evaluating the post-condition first.

Before the current iteration is restarted, all `finally` cleanups registered inside the abandoned iteration attempt are executed.

`redo` is dangerous because it can create non-progressing loops. It is valid only inside a loop frame.

19.15. `yield`

`yield` is valid only inside an `fn` whose return descriptor defines a yield-protocol.

The standard iterator-like profile is expressed by ordinary namespace names:

    iterator(T)

Here `iterator` and `T` are not parser keywords. They are ordinary identifier
values resolved in the active lexical namespace view by the active L3/profile
receiver set. For example, `Text` or `Point` must be visible descriptor names if
they are used in `iterator(Text)` or `iterator(Point)`.

Calling a function whose return descriptor implements the iterator requirement
does not execute its body to completion. It creates an iterator state Structure.

The translator lowers the iterator body into a `next(env)` implementation.

Each `yield` consumes one produced value. It inspects the current function's return descriptor, verifies that the descriptor defines a yield-protocol, checks the produced value against that protocol, emits one value from `next(env)` and records the resume state in `env`.

The `each` receiver consumes Structures whose descriptors implement its iterator
requirement by repeatedly calling their `next(env)` implementation until no
value is produced.

This is the standard lazy-production protocol for sequences. A call to an
function whose return descriptor implements the iterator requirement creates a
resumable state Structure; it does not
force every later produced value immediately. One consumer step asks for one
value, `next(env)` runs until the next `yield` or termination, then the state is
saved for a later step.

For example:

    fn: words () iterator(Text)
        yield: "Hello"
        yield: "World"
    end: words

is not source-semantically:

    words() -> Array("Hello", "World")

It is:

    words() -> iterator state Structure
    next(env) -> "Hello"
    next(env) -> "World"
    next(env) -> noValue

Therefore `yield` provides demand-driven production:

    one value now;
    state saved;
    next value later;
    later value may never be requested.

This does not make all Lingvamyxa evaluation globally non-strict. Without a
receiver/profile that admits deferred consumption, an expression such as:

    print: expensive()

is ordinary value-producing input to the `print` receiver, not automatically a
thunk. Laziness is attached to descriptors and receivers explicitly visible in
the namespace.
Examples of such profiles may be:

    iterator(T)  zero or more T, forced by next(env);
    stream(T)    zero or more T, possibly asynchronous, forced by next/awaitNext;
    thunk(T)     one deferred T, forced by value(thunk) or an equivalent receiver;
    future(T)    one scheduled/deferred T or failure, forced by await(future);
    continuation(T) profile-defined resumable computation.

The common rule is that Structures are data until a receiver consumes them. A
lazy profile is a receiver/descriptor protocol that decides when to
consume the next step. This is different from Haskell-style global laziness:
Lingvamyxa does not make every argument and every data field lazy by default.
That keeps effects, ownership, validation and message scheduling explicit.

Callable partial/capture descriptor capabilities

Partial application and escaping closure capture are separate descriptor
capabilities. They may use the same backend representation, but they answer
different semantic questions:

    callable.partial
        A callable descriptor may consume a call-shaped argument such as
        `addBase(4)` or an explicit `partial(addBase, 4)` as a residual callable.

    callable.capture
        A callable descriptor may receive a nested callable whose environment
        escapes the defining scope.

    callable.projection
        The backend representation selected for the descriptor. In the first C
        projection profile, `callable.partial` and `callable.capture` require
        `c.closure-struct` or another representation that can store an
        environment.

The hosted bootstrap currently accepts explicit configuration Tables such as:

    table:
        source
        name: `callable.partial`
        columns: class  (value serial)
    end: `callable.partial`

    table:
        source
        name: `callable.capture`
        columns: class  (value serial)
    end: `callable.capture`

    table:
        source
        name: `callable.projection`
        columns: class  (value projection)
        rows:
                 AddX `c.closure-struct`
    end: `callable.projection`

Its explicit profile loader may use another Table to select admitted surface
forms for partial binding:

    table:
        source
        name: `callable.partial.form`
        columns: class (materializer binding)
        rows:
                 `call-frame` lm_trans_materialize_call_frame_partial
                 partial      lm_trans_materialize_partial_callable
    end: `callable.partial.form`

The `call-frame` row admits the existing compact callable-slot spelling:

    callAdd(addBase(4))

The `partial` row admits the explicit spelling:

    callAdd(partial(addBase, 4))

These Tables do not affect the namespace merely by existing. The descriptor
receiver head:

    callable: AddX (int: value) int

is compact sugar for a closure-capable descriptor Structure. Normatively it
binds a descriptor with fields conceptually equivalent to:

    callable.partial[AddX] = 1
    callable.capture[AddX] = 1
    callable.projection[AddX] = c.closure-struct

It produces no Registry rows. Semantic rules test the descriptor's partial and
capture capabilities separately.

For the first L2 translator profile, post-declaration marker operations remain
reserved but rejected:

    lazy X
    eager X

That is, these words may not yet be applied to an already-declared descriptor as
source-level modifiers. Future profiles may admit post-declaration set/override
operations, but they must be explicit compile-time namespace/descriptor updates,
not ambient Table effects or runtime switches.

A descriptor without `callable.partial` does not admit the residual-callable
interpretation through the first L2 translator profile. A compact call in that
position must either be consumed by another explicitly declared
descriptor/receiver protocol or be lowered as an ordinary value-producing call
according to the expected argument type.

A descriptor without `callable.capture` does not admit returning an escaping
nested callable with captured environment. A descriptor whose
`callable.projection` is a bare C function pointer must reject partial binding
and escaping capture unless an explicit adapter or closure representation is
also selected.

19.15.1. Iterator function

Example:

fn: makePoints () iterator(Point)
    yield: Point(1.0 2.0)
    yield: Point(3.0 4.0)
    yield: Point(5.0 6.0)
end: makePoints

Another zero-argument example:

fn: words () iterator(Text)
    yield: "Hello"
    yield: "World"
end: words


Calling:

    makePoints()

creates an iterator state Structure. It does not return an `Array Point` and does not execute all `yield` statements immediately.

P0 reads the first definition structurally as a plain `fn` frame:

    fn(
      makePoints,
      (),
      iterator(Point),
      yield(Point(1.0 2.0)),
      yield(Point(3.0 4.0)),
      yield(Point(5.0 6.0))
    )

The `fn` receiver consumes field 2 as the return descriptor and fields 3..end as the executable body. The `yield` receiver then checks each produced value against the yield-protocol declared by the current function return descriptor.

19.15.2. Iterator state Structure

An iterator state Structure contains enough state to resume execution after each `yield`.

For a simple iterator, generated state may include:

    state index;
    captured arguments;
    local variables that survive across yields;
    cleanup registrations owned by the iterator frame;
    a reference to the generated `next(env)` implementation.

Source-level code does not depend on the physical layout of this state Structure.

19.15.3. `next(env)`

The generated `next(env)` implementation executes the iterator until it either reaches a `yield` or terminates.

At a `yield`, `next(env)` returns a produced value and records where execution must resume.

When the iterator terminates, `next(env)` returns a no-value result.

Conceptual result model:

Next: T
    boolean: hasValue
    T: value


?
The exact standard name and structural layout for the result of `next(env)` is not specified. A final iterator specification must choose canonical names and fields, such as `Next T`, `Option T`, `Some/None` or another equivalent representation.
?

19.15.4. `yield` value construction

For the `iterator(T)` profile, the canonical source form is:

    yield: value

The current `fn` return descriptor, such as `iterator(T)`, tells `yield` which
yield-protocol is active. The `yield` tail is the produced value. The value's
descriptor must implement `T`; any additional iterator operation checks are
ordinary `yield` receiver validation rather than another compatibility system.

There is no hidden flattening. If `value` itself has an iterator type, then `yield: value` yields that iterator value as the produced value when the active protocol admits it. Flattening or forwarding another iterator requires a separate receiver such as `yieldFrom`.

If `T` is primitive, `value` is checked against that primitive type:

    fn: words () iterator(Text)
        yield: "Hello"
    end: words

If `T` is a Structure descriptor, write the produced Structure explicitly:

    fn: makePoints () iterator(Point)
        yield: Point(1.0 2.0)
    end: makePoints

This example emits one `Point` value when `Point(1.0 2.0)` is a valid
construction under the visible `Point` descriptor.

If `T` is an Array descriptor, `yield` analogously consumes an
ArrayReference. Neither aggregate result is copied by `yield`; iterator
publication follows the descriptor and arena-lifetime contract.

19.15.5. Iterator consumption by `each`

Example:

each: point in makePoints
    draw: point

For a zero-argument iterator function, the source may also be written as an explicit compact call:

each: point in makePoints()
    draw: point

Both forms are valid. The bare form gives the callable binding to `each` as an iterator source; the compact-call form creates the iterator state Structure first and gives that Structure to `each`.

Semantic form:

iterator: makePoints

while: true
    result: next iterator\env
    if: not result\hasValue
        break
    ---
    point: result\value
    body


This is semantic pseudocode. A translator may lower it to a direct state machine or inline loop.

19.15.6. Iterator termination

An iterator-like `fn` terminates when its generated `next(env)` reaches the end of the executable body or reaches a profile-defined completion path.

A `yield` after termination is unreachable.

In the core profile, a bare `return` is not a valid `fn` return. If an iterator profile wants an explicit early iterator-completion operator, it must define that receiver/profile rule separately. `return: value` remains an ordinary `fn` runtime return form when admitted by the function's return descriptor; when written in a terminal trailer position, it may also close the `fn`.

`break` and `continue` inside an iterator body operate on the nearest active loop frame inside the iterator body, not on the consumer's `each` loop.

!!!
Iterator close semantics are not fully specified. A final specification must define when iterator-owned `finally` cleanups run if an iterator is abandoned before exhaustion, discarded, moved to another thread or terminated by diagnostic failure.
!!!

19.16. Labelled `break` and labelled `continue`

A control label may be attached to a loop frame.

Example:

outerLoop:
    for: row in rows
        for: cell in row
            if: found cell
                break: outerLoop
            ---
        ---
    ---

`labelName:` is an ordinary colon frame that the active profile materializes as a visible control target for the following block.

The label name is visible in the ordinary namespace as a control target, but it
is not callable merely by being mentioned. A standalone `labelName` item is a
compile-time error in the default profile: Lingvamyxa has no implicit `goto`.
Only control forms that define label consumption, such as `break: labelName`,
`continue: labelName`, failure handlers or other profile-defined control
operators, may consume a label as a target.

`break: labelName` exits the labelled loop frame.

`continue: labelName` continues the labelled loop frame if the target label names a loop frame.

Before the transfer, all `finally` cleanups registered in scopes exited by the transfer are executed in reverse registration order.

It is a compile-time error for `break: labelName` or `continue: labelName` to target a label that is not visible in the control-label scope.

It is a compile-time error for `continue: labelName` to target a non-loop frame.

?
The exact grammar rule for attaching labels to frames is not specified beyond the form `labelName:` before a frame. A final grammar must define whether labels can attach to retryable frames, blocks, handlers, iterator frames and arbitrary Structures.
?

19.17. `merge` / Table `join`

`merge` is an L2/L3 structural receiver/operator. It is not parser syntax, not a
preprocessor include and not runtime mutation of its source operands. The parser
builds an ordinary `merge:` Frame; the active receiver/operator namespace gives
that Frame structural-merge semantics.

This is the target contract. The current registered handler is instead a
descriptor-composition declaration: it resolves layout-describing sources,
builds a normalized `structuralFields` view, publishes that descriptor under the
explicit merge-target name and emits a hosted C type/layout projection. It does
not evaluate a source call merely because its declared result supplies a layout,
does not emit target-object storage and does not materialize a Structure
instance. Constructing a value from the resulting descriptor is a separate
explicit constructor operation.

The physical descriptor-composition path no longer emits implicit Registry
field relations; layout collection reads the descriptor view. Registry field
relations remain accepted as a compatibility fallback for an explicitly
authored or imported ABI layout whose namespace binding has no structural-field
view. Declaring either a merge target or an ordinary named-Structure descriptor
publishes no owner or field Registry metadata. This current handler does not yet
implement the generic ordered high-level Structure merge described below; when
that value-producing contract is implemented, its result is an arena-owned
StructureReference.

For example, the current physical form:

    merge: (BaseFields AuditFields) CombinedFields
        u32: flags
    end: CombinedFields

binds the composed descriptor as `CombinedFields` and may emit a C type for it.
It does not create a value named `CombinedFields`. A later form such as
`CombinedFields: combined ... end: combined` is the separate operation that
constructs and binds an instance.

Semantic form:

    merge(part0, part1, ..., partN)

Each part is consumed in a Structure-producing context. A part may be a visible
Structure/descriptor binding, a bounded or vertical Structure literal, or a
call/receiver result whose declared result descriptor implements `Structure`. If a
named Frame in the argument stream is not resolved as a callable receiver in the
active namespace, a tolerant structural profile may keep that Frame as an
ordinary named field of the merge result.

The default merge is ordered and append-only:

    1. Normalize each part to a field sequence.
    2. Append the field sequences from left to right.
    3. Preserve duplicate field names and duplicate receiver heads.
    4. Do not silently override, delete, rename or deduplicate fields.

Conflict interpretation is left to the consumer of the merged Structure. A
layout receiver may reject duplicate field names, a table receiver may accept
repeated rows, and another profile may define first-wins, last-wins or explicit
override rules. Those policies are receiver semantics above `merge`; the core
`merge` result is the ordered field stream.

Example:

BaseFields:
    int: id
    @: char name
end: BaseFields

AuditFields:
    u64: createdAt
    u64: updatedAt
end: AuditFields

merge: BaseFields AuditFields
    u32: flags
end: merge

Conceptual result:

    (
        int: id
        @: char name
        u64: createdAt
        u64: updatedAt
        u32: flags
    )

The vertical body of a `merge:` Frame is part of the same argument Structure as
its short tail. Therefore a trailing body naturally contributes trailing merge
parts. A receiver that uses `merge` to build a descriptor/import field list may
evaluate the `merge` result before validating the final descriptor.

Table profile:

In an L2 or L3 table profile, `table:` and `join:` are ordinary receiver Frames
whose bodies are valid LMX Structures. `table:` explicitly materializes one
table representation from Structure/Array data in the selected arena and,
when it names the result, introduces that binding in the current namespace.
The result is a StructureReference or ArrayReference according to the selected
Table descriptor; `Table` is a profile role, not a fourth Value category.
`join:` explicitly consumes such references and materializes one new table
representation; it does not mutate its operands.

The optional first body atom `source` requests a translation-time/module-owned
table value rather than an executable runtime construction site. It does not
select a hidden RegistryView, declare the names contained in cells or give a
column named `class` special language meaning. Any operation that imports table
contents into a namespace must be explicit. Each same-name binding it proposes
is admitted only by section 7.3.1; false or malformed comparisons are
diagnostics and leave the namespace unchanged.

All ordinary table values are composed from Structures and arrays and follow
their arena lifetimes. An ANSI C backend may emit constant/generated data only
when that representation implements the observable module/root-arena lifetime;
C `static` storage is not table source semantics. The current bootstrap's
direct static-row emission is implementation debt.

The L2/L3 receiver `join` materializes a new table value from several
table-producing sources:

    join: (sourceTable0 sourceTable1 ... sourceTableN) targetName
        tableFragment
    end: targetName

The bounded first argument is the ordered source-table list. Each source item is
consumed in a table-producing context: it may name a table value visible in the
active namespace, be a table literal/fragment, or be a receiver/call whose
declared result descriptor implements the table descriptor. The second
argument is the target table name. The `join` receiver normalizes that
argument as its `name`, so the canonical explicit close is `end: targetName`;
`end: join` remains the admitted receiver/head close alternative.

The vertical body is an optional target table fragment. It may be written
directly as table body fields such as `columns:` and `rows:`, or as an explicit
nested `table:` descriptor. If an explicit nested `table:` contains `name:`, that
name must match `targetName` after ordinary identifier normalization.

Default table join is ordered:

    1. Resolve source tables from left to right in the active namespace.
    2. Determine the target table schema from the explicit body `columns:` when
       present, otherwise from the first source table.
    3. Validate that every source row is compatible with the target schema.
    4. Append source rows in source-list order.
    5. Append body rows after source rows.
    6. Preserve duplicate row keys and duplicate relation facts.

`join` does not silently override, remove or deduplicate rows. If an explicit
consumer later uses last-visible-row, first-visible-row, relation-set or overlay
semantics, that is the consumer's policy, not a hidden side effect of `join`.

Example in an `.lm2` or `.lm3` source module:

join: (`Base.field.class` `Audit.field.class`) `Entity.field.class`
    columns: class  (field class)
    rows:
             flags  u32
end: `Entity.field.class`

Conceptual materialized table value:

table:
    source
    name: `Entity.field.class`
    columns: class  (field class)
    rows:
             # rows from `Base.field.class`
             # rows from `Audit.field.class`
             flags  u32
end: `Entity.field.class`


The remaining subsections define explicit Registry, message, service and runtime
receiver families that were formerly described as separate language levels.
They use only the L2/L3 profiles defined in section 1. A subsection may define
supporting Structures such as RegistryView, Evidence, ExecutionPlan or Message
Thread in addition to its source receiver spellings; those Structures are not
new levels or parser mechanisms.

Mechanism filter:

    A profile term is not a new language mechanism merely because it has a
