edge follows the lifetime of its declared provider or handle profile.

L3 excludes source operations which expose raw machine storage or addresses.
It may use a checked wrapper StructureReference, a Primitive handle admitted by
a descriptor, a message or a receiver which hides the raw representation and
provides automatic bounds/lifetime validation. This is a level-surface
distinction, not a claim that all L3 code is safe, pure, authorized or free of
effects.

For example, a checked high-level array operation may be available in both L2
and L3, while unchecked address arithmetic over its backing buffer is L2-only.
A Message Thread or service operation may be available at both levels, while a
raw OS-thread handle, atomic operation or allocator call is L2-only.

Raw low-level references must still obey their declared owner lifetime and must
not cross Message Thread boundaries contrary to the message profile. Dropping
to L2 does not silently weaken an already selected Message Thread isolation
contract; it gives the programmer explicit machine mechanisms whose use must
be described by the L2 profile.


19.29.11. Arena Selection and Explicit Ownership Operations

Arena selection is supplied by the ExecutionContext or by an explicit operation
argument. The memory model defines no ordinary `stack:`, `heap:` or `static:`
allocation receiver, and no Registry/Table selects object lifetime.

Ordinary Structure and Array constructors materialize in the current arena.
Module/top-level constructors use a module or root arena. A constructor may use
another arena only when its namespace-visible signature explicitly accepts that
arena as an argument.

`copy`, `move`, `borrow` and `own`, when implemented, are ordinary explicitly
bound operations. Their descriptors define target arena, edge semantics,
effects and failure behavior. They do not require separate ownership grammar.

Conceptual future forms are:

    own: saved m\payload
    borrow: view m\payload
    copy: dst src
    move: dst src

Current status:

    copy    proposed source operation; NOT IMPLEMENTED generically;
    move    proposed source operation; NOT IMPLEMENTED generically;
    borrow  proposed source operation; NOT IMPLEMENTED generically;
    own     proposed source operation; NOT IMPLEMENTED generically.

The low-level owner runtime already provides arena and lazy-edge helpers. Their
existence does not make these generic source operations available. Availability
requires an explicit receiver/callable binding and a namespace-visible
descriptor.

Tables may contain ownership policy as ordinary application data. Such data has
no effect until an explicitly bound operation receives and consumes it. The
meaning of a field follows the descriptor and operation contracts visible in
the namespace plus the current/explicit arena—not an ambient row lookup.

The absence of a lifetime annotation does not authorize a hidden copy at
`return`. Ordinary return remains exact-reference publication.


19.29.12. ANSI C Lowering Strategy

19.29.12.1. Current Hosted Bootstrap Profile

The current hosted C99 profile uses the `libown` runtime and the concrete arena
type `LmOwnArena`.

Every generated standalone program starts as one root Message Thread. The
generated root runtime state owns a root arena before any ordinary Structure or
Array is materialized, and destroys that arena when the program or root Message
Thread terminates.

The shared actor type is `LmMessageThread`; no separate generated
per-translation-unit thread helper remains. The current actor Structure records:

    root owner arena;
    RUNNING / STOPPING / STOPPED state;
    retained stop status;
    turn and collection counters;
    collector-failure state;
    opaque current-turn ExecutionContext pointer.

The generated external `main` creates an explicit unpooled root
`LmMessageThread` plus an `LmMessageThreadRuntime`, attaches the root to that
runtime, installs its diagnostic ExecutionContext through the explicit root
reference and drives the turn loop described in section 19.28.2.2. When source
`thread:` declarations exist, it additionally constructs one application pool,
starts and route-binds those mailbox actors, then drains committed work, joins
them and releases the pool. Finally it detaches and releases the root/runtime.
It does not acquire a singleton root/current object. The root is the first
ordinary Message Thread instance, not a second memory mechanism and not a
source level.

The worker-pool ABI is implemented in the same runtime library. Generated
standalone `main` functions always construct the application runtime and add a
pool only when they own local/imported `thread:` handlers. The direct root loop
and the pool scheduler share the same actor state, arena, outbox and end-turn
collection semantics.

This rule applies to ordinary applications and to bootstrap tools alike:

    parser;
    trans;
    make;
    finalize;
    buildCore;
    generated tests and utilities.

Thus a generated `make` utility depending on `libown` is modularly correct:
`make` is an executable Message Thread root with the same runtime lifetime
requirements as another generated executable. The build system must order its
stages so that the owner runtime exists before tools linked against it are
built or run.

The essential current arena API includes the explicit executing actor as its
first parameter. In an LMX managed function that argument is supplied by the
hidden `lm_lmx_message_thread` lowering parameter; it is still an ordinary C
parameter and never a global/TLS lookup. `owner_thread` names the actor whose
owner graph contains the arena and is normally the same explicit actor in the
current profile.

    LmOwnArena *lm_own_arena_new(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmMessageThread *owner_thread);
    void lm_own_arena_delete(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena);
    int lm_own_arena_init(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena,
        LmMessageThread *owner_thread);
    void lm_own_arena_destroy(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena);

    void *lm_own_arena_new_zero(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena,
        size_t size);

    void *lm_own_arena_array_new_zero(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena,
        size_t element_size,
        size_t count,
        size_t rank,
        size_t level);

    const LmOwnAllocationDescriptor *lm_own_arena_allocation_descriptor(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        const LmOwnArena *arena,
        const void *address);

    char *lm_own_arena_copy_bytes(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena,
        const char *source,
        size_t length);

    int lm_own_arena_add_lazy_edge(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *target,
        LmOwnArena *source,
        const void *source_ptr,
        size_t size,
        const void **patch_slot);

    int lm_own_arena_promote_lazy_edges(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena);
    int lm_own_arena_absorb(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *target,
        LmOwnArena *source);
    void lm_own_arena_freeze(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena);
    int lm_own_arena_is_frozen(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        const LmOwnArena *arena);
    LmMessageThread *lm_own_arena_owner_thread(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        const LmOwnArena *arena);
    int lm_own_tree_cut(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena);
    int lm_own_tree_cut_promote_lazy_edges(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmOwnArena *arena);

The essential current Message Thread ABI includes:

    typedef void (*LmMessageThreadEntry)(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        void *argument);

    LmMessageThreadRuntime *lm_message_thread_runtime_new(void);
    int lm_message_thread_runtime_delete(LmMessageThreadRuntime *runtime);

    LmMessageThreadPool *lm_message_thread_pool_new(
        LmMessageThreadRuntime *runtime,
        size_t worker_count);
    void lm_message_thread_pool_request_stop(LmMessageThreadPool *pool);
    int lm_message_thread_pool_delete(LmMessageThreadPool *pool);

    LmMessageThread *lm_message_thread_new(void);
    LmMessageThread *lm_message_thread_new_in(LmMessageThreadPool *pool);
    void lm_message_thread_delete(LmMessageThread *thread);

    int lm_message_thread_start(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        LmMessageThreadEntry entry,
        void *argument);
    int lm_message_thread_join(LmMessageThread *thread, int *result);

    int lm_message_thread_begin_turn(LmMessageThread *thread);
    int lm_message_thread_end_turn(LmMessageThread *thread);
    int lm_message_thread_collect(LmMessageThread *thread);

    void lm_message_thread_request_stop(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        int status);

    void lm_message_thread_request_failure(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        int status);

`request_stop` implements first-ordinary-stop-wins. `request_failure` is the
failure-dominant diagnostic/runtime channel and may replace an ordinary stop
status while the thread is RUNNING or STOPPING. `end_turn` always reaches local
collection and converts STOPPING to STOPPED. Status, owner, ExecutionContext
and counter accessors are also exported by the bootstrap ABI.

The ABI has no `lm_message_thread_root`, `lm_message_thread_current` or
`lm_message_thread_set_current` lookup. Managed entry and arena operations
receive the executing actor explicitly as described in section 19.28.10.

This is an implementation API, not source syntax. It may evolve without
changing the source rules, provided ordinary Structure/Array identity, arena
lifetime and return behavior remain conforming.

The current runtime represents at least these ownership edge kinds:

    LM_OWN_EDGE_BORROWED;
    LM_OWN_EDGE_OWNED;
    LM_OWN_EDGE_LAZY_OWNED;
    LM_OWN_EDGE_EXTERNAL.

It also records allocation descriptors containing the allocation address,
owner arena, byte size, element size, element count, rank and array level. This
metadata supports typed array/storage discovery without making an ordinary
Structure or Array into a C aggregate value.

The current `LmOwnArena` records allocations, allocation descriptors, lazy
edges and frozen state. Destruction releases all allocations recorded by the
arena. Lazy-edge promotion can copy explicit lazy-owned content into a target
arena and patch the recorded target slot. Arena absorption can move a complete
set of arena-managed allocations into another arena when the runtime contract
allows it.

The existence of global low-level helpers such as `lm_own_new_zero` and
`lm_own_array_new_zero` reflects bootstrap and implementation code. Ordinary
generated Structure materialization is being moved to root-arena operations.
Future implementation work may continue reducing uses of the unscoped helpers
where the allocation is semantically part of an owner region.


19.29.12.2. Current Root-Arena Materialization

The current migrated paths allocate explicitly constructed local and direct
module-level named Structures, local and direct module-level anonymous
Structures, `fm` result Structures, admitted ordinary Arrays in managed Message
Thread contexts (including extent-bearing `[]:` declarations and direct
module-level ArrayReferences), and closure environments/descriptors in the root
Message Thread arena. A named-Structure or physical-`merge:` descriptor
declaration performs no allocation. Non-source runtime tables may still use a
static C-backed representation. That path is separate implementation debt and
must migrate to module/root-arena materialization where it represents an
ordinary source value.

Conceptual generated form for an ordinary local Structure is:

    T *value = (T *)lm_own_arena_new_zero(
        lm_lmx_message_thread,
        lm_message_thread_owner(lm_lmx_message_thread),
        sizeof(*value));

The object is allocated once when its source operation materializes it. Local
bindings hold `T *`-shaped references. Assigning or returning such a binding
does not allocate another `T`.

Conceptual generated `fm` result form is:

    Result *lm_fm_result = (Result *)lm_own_arena_new_zero(
        lm_lmx_message_thread,
        lm_message_thread_owner(lm_lmx_message_thread),
        sizeof(*lm_fm_result));

    if (lm_fm_result == 0) {
        /* selected failure convention */
    }

    /* function body */

    lm_fm_result->first = first_value;
    lm_fm_result->second = second_value;
    return lm_fm_result;

The result allocation belongs to function/result materialization in the
prologue. It is not performed by `return`. All return paths publish the same
result reference for that invocation.

An Array constructor allocates its Array object/header and owned backing storage
in the selected arena through `lm_own_arena_new_zero` and
`lm_own_arena_array_new_zero`. The header is not a structural type descriptor.
Returning the Array publishes the existing ArrayReference; it does not recreate
the header or backing storage.

A closure implementation may similarly allocate an environment Structure and
callable descriptor in the selected arena before a return expression publishes
the descriptor. Returning a closure is not permission to allocate a second
environment or descriptor.

The root-only strategy means that independently returned objects remain valid
and distinct until root-arena destruction. It intentionally delays
reclamation. Later owner profiles may reclaim non-escaping regions earlier but
must preserve the exact same visible references and addresses.


19.29.12.2.1. Hosted Source-Ordered Module Initialization

The hosted ANSI C executable boundary materializes these direct module-level
ordinary value sites in the attached root Message Thread arena:

    named Structure construction;
    anonymous `():` Structure construction, including an empty Structure;
    ordinary `[]:` ArrayReference construction.

A descriptor declaration, function definition, raw `c.array` declaration or
source-only translation Table is not such a site. The mechanism implements the
ordinary arena rules; it does not introduce a module-static source Value kind.

The backend gives every value-producing source site a stable private identity
derived from the resolved source path and source span. It may emit a C
declaration or private anonymous-layout type needed by that site. For the value
itself it emits only a zero-initialized static reference slot: a `T *`-shaped
slot for a StructureReference or an `LmSlice *`-shaped slot for an
ArrayReference. The referent, Array header and every owned Array backing level
must be allocated in the supplied arena. A static Structure instance, static
anonymous aggregate, static ordinary-Array element buffer or pointer into such
backing is not a conforming implementation of this boundary.

Each site has one private initializer helper whose logical parameters are the
executing `LmMessageThread` and selected `LmOwnArena`. The helper performs the
same validation, allocation and initialization as the corresponding local
constructor. Named and anonymous Structure fields are initialized exactly once
in logical source order. An omitted field follows its ordinary initialization
rule, and an empty `():` site still allocates one non-null, distinct empty
Structure referent. For an ArrayReference, extent expressions are evaluated
exactly once in dimension source order before backing allocation, then present
payload expressions are evaluated exactly once in element source order. The
header and all backing levels belong to the supplied arena.

The helper publishes its private reference slot only after that site has
completed successfully. A typed exact-reference initializer forwards the
already visible reference into its own slot without copying or reallocating the
referent. Thus a later binding such as `Point: alias original` has exact
reference identity with `original`; reading either binding after module
initialization observes the same object. Two actual constructor sites allocate
distinct referents even when their descriptors and initializer values are the
same.

Module initialization is an ordered traversal, not a C initializer dependency
accident. Direct sites run in source order. At the first ordinary `import`
occurrence, the imported module's dependency traversal and direct sites run at
that source position before traversal resumes in the importer. A module and
each of its stable source sites run at most once in one generated executable,
keyed by the path produced by the import resolver. Repeated imports that resolve
to the same path therefore neither allocate another instance nor repeat
initializer side effects. A later import cannot retroactively make one of its
bindings visible to an earlier initializer. Modules selected by the established
hoisted `predef` traversal participate once in that traversal's deterministic
order; reaching the same resolved module path again through `predef` or
`import` does not schedule its value sites again.

The current resolver does not canonicalize lexically distinct relative path
spellings such as `x.lm2` and `./x.lm2`; they remain distinct module identities
until portable path normalization is added.

Every helper is emitted after the declarations and callable prototypes needed
by its body. The generated dispatcher is invoked only after the process root
Message Thread has been attached and its root arena is available. It receives
that actor and arena explicitly. It completes before the user `main` body,
before any declared worker actor is started, and before any route or inbound
provider can make worker execution observable. If initialization fails, the
hosted failure path does not enter user `main` or start workers; it tears down
the attached root by the ordinary generated cleanup path. Successfully
published references remain alive until that root arena is destroyed.

Initializer name resolution uses the namespace snapshot immediately before the
site. The pending result slot is not a provisional self-binding. Consequently,
an initializer cannot refer to the result being constructed unless an older
visible occurrence already exists, and it cannot refer to a binding introduced
later in the module traversal. An unresolved self-reference and a forward
reference are hosted diagnostics; the backend must not emit a tentative null
slot and let either expression observe it. The current occurrence-projection
limitations described in sections 19.20.1.1 and 19.20.4 may independently
reject publication of a result whose exact name already has an older visible
occurrence. That limitation does not make the pending occurrence visible as a
self-reference.

The initial hosted boundary is deliberately unconditional. A direct ordinary
value site nested in `os`, `ifdef`, `guard` or another conditionally emitted
top-level wrapper is rejected until conditional namespace replay and a
presence-matched initializer schedule are implemented. A module value inside
an `extern_c` wrapper is likewise rejected until that wrapper participates in
the dedicated slot/helper phases instead of recursively emitting a complete
module pipeline. A module value inside an L2/L3 level receiver nested in an
already active module surface is rejected for the same reason until nested
surface declaration and scheduling are replayed in source order. A local
constructor in an executable `if` remains an ordinary execution-time operation
and is not affected by this restriction.

The complete generated executable must provide exactly one non-imported hosted
`main` definition to own the attached root. A root translation containing
module-level ordinary value sites but no such entry is rejected until an
explicit library/module-initializer ABI is selected. An imported module need
not define `main`; its sites join the importing executable's dispatcher. A
`main` definition supplied by an imported module is rejected, as are multiple
candidate `main` definitions anywhere in the root import graph. A `main`
inside a conditional, `extern_c` or nested level wrapper cannot host this
unconditional module-value schedule and is rejected when module sites exist.
These are
hosted executable-boundary restrictions, not new source Value kinds or general
rules forbidding another runtime profile from exposing an explicit module
initializer.


19.29.12.3. Generated Cleanup for Explicit Low-Level Resources

An ordinary owner arena can be destroyed as one region. Explicit low-level or
external resources may additionally require generated cleanup before their
owner is released.

A C99 lowering may direct all exits through one cleanup label:

    int lm_generated_handler(
        LmMessageThread *thread,
        const LmMessage *message)
    {
        int status = 0;
        LmResource resource;
        int resource_initialized = 0;

        status = lm_resource_init(&resource);
        if (status != 0) {
            goto exit;
        }
        resource_initialized = 1;

        status = lm_generated_body(thread, message, &resource);
        if (status != 0) {
            goto exit;
        }

    exit:
        if (resource_initialized) {
            lm_resource_destroy(&resource);
        }
        lm_message_thread_safe_point(thread);
        return status;
    }

This example is conceptual. Exact runtime type names and error conventions are
profile-defined. Cleanup of an external resource is not a copy or destructor
for an ordinary Structure or Array referent. The aggregate remains
arena-managed through its reference; cleanup follows the explicit resource
descriptor.

Cleanup ordering must respect dependencies. If one resource's release method
uses another resource, the latter must remain live until the first release
finishes. A generated profile may record cleanup actions in reverse
initialization order or derive ordering from explicit visible descriptors.

A control exit first selects and publishes the surviving result/failure
reference, then retains, detaches or reparents its existing owner region as
required, marks and updates the owner graph, executes required explicit cleanup
and finally reaches its collection/release point. Cleanup must not destroy the
owner region containing an ordinary StructureReference or ArrayReference
already selected as a surviving result.


19.29.12.4. Conceptual Future Precise-Owner Runtime

A more precise future runtime may represent branch, frame, handler and
continuation owners explicitly. The following API is conceptual design, not the
current ABI and not source syntax:

    typedef struct LmOwnerArena LmOwnerArena;
    typedef struct LmOwnerBranch LmOwnerBranch;
    typedef struct LmMessageThread LmMessageThread;

    typedef enum LmOwnerEdgeKind {
        LM_OWNER_EDGE_OWNED,
        LM_OWNER_EDGE_LAZY_OWNED,
        LM_OWNER_EDGE_BORROWED,
        LM_OWNER_EDGE_EXTERNAL,
        LM_OWNER_EDGE_TRANSFERRED
    } LmOwnerEdgeKind;

    typedef struct LmOwnerTypeOps {
        void (*release_external_fields)(void *value);
        int (*copy_to)(LmOwnerArena *arena,
                       void **out_reference,
                       const void *source);
        int (*serialize)(void *writer,
                         const void *source);
        void (*trace_owner_edges)(void *owner_walker,
                                  const void *value);
    } LmOwnerTypeOps;

    typedef struct LmOwnerEdge {
        LmOwnerEdgeKind kind;
        LmOwnerBranch *source_owner;
        LmOwnerBranch *target_owner;
        const LmOwnerTypeOps *type_ops;
        void *value;
        void **patch_slot;
    } LmOwnerEdge;

    struct LmOwnerBranch {
        LmOwnerArena *arena;
        const LmOwnerTypeOps *type_ops;
        void *root_reference;
        LmMessageThread *owner_thread;
        unsigned flags;
    };

Possible precise arena operations are:

    void *lm_owner_arena_alloc(
        LmOwnerArena *arena,
        size_t size,
        size_t alignment);

    void lm_owner_arena_reset(LmOwnerArena *arena);
    void lm_owner_arena_destroy(LmOwnerArena *arena);

    int lm_owner_branch_detach(
        LmOwnerBranch *branch,
        LmOwnerBranch **out_detached);

    int lm_owner_branch_reparent(
        LmOwnerBranch *branch,
        LmOwnerBranch *new_parent);

    int lm_owner_branch_transfer(
        LmOwnerBranch *branch,
        LmMessageThread *receiver);

Generated operations may be selected from namespace-visible descriptors:

    release_external_fields
        releases explicitly owned external resources;

    copy_to
        implements an explicit `copy` or `own` operation, not return;

    serialize
        implements a selected message/codec boundary;

    trace_owner_edges
        supports local owner-tree walking or local branch-root reference
        tracking.

The old historical names `LmArena`, `LmBranch` and `LmThread` described this
same conceptual future direction. They are not the current API and should not
be presented as already implemented. The actual bootstrap API remains
`LmOwnArena`/`libown`; the generic future names in this subsection deliberately
avoid implying ABI compatibility.

An explicit future `own` expression may lower to a selected generated method:

    status = lm_copy_to_owner(
        current_owner,
        source_reference,
        &saved_reference);

or to a lazy-owned edge:

    status = lm_add_lazy_owned_edge(
        current_owner,
        source_owner,
        source_reference,
        &saved_reference);

Both lowerings implement an explicit ownership operation. Neither may be used
as the hidden lowering of ordinary `return`.

Whole-branch message transfer may lower by detaching the existing branch from
the sender owner graph, attaching it to the receiver and invalidating all
sender-side handles covered by the transfer contract. The objects are not
copied and retain their identity inside the transferred branch.

Optional per-Message-Thread tracing or reference counting may track owner
roots. It remains local; the source model does not require a global heap scan.


19.29.12.5. Lowering Requirements and Open ABI Details

A conforming C99 or other backend must preserve:

    StructureReference and ArrayReference representation;
    arena allocation of every ordinary aggregate referent;
    module/root arena lifetime for top-level values;
    stable object identity;
    exact-reference return;
    owner validity across every published reference;
    no raw mutable inter-Message-Thread references;
    explicit copy/serialization/transfer semantics;
    cleanup of declared external resources;
    bounds metadata for checked high-level arrays;
    correct low-level lifetime for explicit L2 machine storage.

The following physical details are implementation/runtime matters rather than
fixed source syntax:

    binary layout of arenas and owner handles;
    lazy-edge table representation;
    immutable-store representation;
    local owner-tree metadata;
    allocation descriptor indexing;
    message serialization format;
    OS-thread and scheduler mapping;
    synchronization primitives;
    status/result conventions;
    generated copy method signatures;
    exact tree-cut promotion algorithm;
    transfer invalidation representation;
    memory-pressure heuristics.

These details may be described by a runtime ABI specification or explicit
backend profile. They must not be allowed to contradict the normative rules in this
section.


19.29.13. Shared Low-Level Resources and Large Read-Only Data

The Message Thread memory model is message- and branch-oriented. It is the
ordinary model for active runtime state, handler data, high-level events,
configuration, explicit Registry queries and service messages. It should not pretend
that a very large shared raw buffer is an ordinary copied message payload.

If a program needs a large resource that many Message Threads read directly,
such as:

    a numeric table;
    a shared search index;
    a memory-mapped dataset;
    a model-weight array;
    an external database page cache;
    a read-only media buffer;

the raw resource belongs to an explicit low-level L2 or external-provider
profile.

No generic source storage receiver is defined here. An explicit L2 provider
binding or typed primitive handle may expose such a resource. The resource is not an
ordinary mutable Message Thread branch: it is explicitly immutable/shared or
externally managed, with a declared lifetime and publication contract.

An application may pass a Table of provider configuration such as:

    storage.kind[BigArray] = external
    storage.element[BigArray] = f64
    storage.access[BigArray] = shared_readonly
    storage.lifetime[BigArray] = provider
    storage.mapping[BigArray] = memory_mapped
    ownership.edge[BigArray][data] = borrowed
    operation.method[BigArray][read] = BigArrayRead

These rows do not register or activate a provider by existing. Provider and
access descriptors must be explicitly bound in the namespace. A checked
Structure wrapper, when used, is allocated in an arena; only the operation that
exposes raw storage or unchecked machine access is L2-only.

Shared immutable data may be read from several Message Threads when the active
provider guarantees:

    immutable contents after publication;
    stable lifetime while handles exist;
    thread-safe publication;
    suitable alignment and representation for the selected backend;
    checked bounds in high-level L3-facing accessors.

Because no thread can mutate the shared object through an alias, this does not
violate the no-shared-mutable-reference rule.

Shared mutable data is different. If multiple host or low-level threads must
write or coordinate over one resource, it must use an explicit L2 profile with
defined:

    atomics;
    locks or read/write locks;
    barriers;
    memory ordering;
    ownership publication;
    cancellation behavior;
    external-resource lifetime;
    failure and recovery.

Such a raw resource remains L2 even if a checked higher-level L3 receiver wraps
it. L3's inability to access the raw buffer directly is not a general claim
that the wrapped service has no effects or concurrency.

The default distinction is:

    Message Thread message boundary
        copy, serialization or whole-branch ownership transfer;

    shared read
        immutable/read-only or externally published resource with typed
        handle;

    explicit shared mutation
        L2 raw/shared buffer plus synchronization and lifetime contract;

    L3-facing use
        checked Structure/handle/message receiver which hides the machine
        operations.

The long-term bootstrap direction is that language-visible storage, ownership,
synchronization and lifetime rules are described by namespace-visible L2/L3
receiver/operation descriptors. Explicit Tables may configure those operations;
L1 remains the external substrate used to lower them to C99 and platform APIs.

Neither shared immutable storage nor an external provider changes ordinary
aggregate return. A source-visible handle is either a Primitive machine value
or a wrapper StructureReference; returning it publishes that exact value. A
provider-specific retain operation, if required, must be explicit in the
handle/operation contract and must occur before return publication; it is not a
hidden copy performed by `return`.


19.29.14. Control Exit, Diagnostics and Message-Thread-Local Collection

Implementation status for this subsection:

    `return`, `stop`, unlabelled `break`, unlabelled `continue` and `assert`
        have current bootstrap lowering;

    recoverable `guard` / `fail` / `throws` / handlers, `retryable` / `retry`,
        `redo` and labelled `break` / labelled `continue`
        are PROPOSED memory contracts and NOT IMPLEMENTED as those source
        receivers.

The currently registered `guard:` is an unrelated module/prelude guard. It
does not implement the recoverable-failure `guard` described below. References
to the proposed operators in this subsection specify their required memory
behavior if and when their receivers are added.

Current `stop:` is a cooperative state transition rather than an immediate
control transfer. It reaches collection when execution arrives at the common
end-turn boundary. Ordinary stop requests retain the first accepted status;
diagnostic and collector/runtime failures use the failure-dominant channel so
that a preceding `stop: 0` cannot hide a later fatal condition in the same
turn.

The memory model has two related local mechanisms:

    Message Thread local collection;
    owner-tree cutting and owner-region maintenance.

Memory reclamation is performed through owner lifetime and these mechanisms.
A control operator does not free memory merely because it changes control flow.
It may leave a frame, handler, retry attempt, transaction, branch owner or whole
Message Thread. Such an exit changes the owner graph and creates a possible
collection safe point.

Local collection may run at these points:

    after normal frame completion;
    after handler completion;
    after transaction completion;
    after `return`;
    after `break`;
    after `continue`;
    after labelled `break`;
    after labelled `continue`;
    after `fail`;
    after `retry`;
    after `redo`;
    after an `assert` diagnostic exit;
    after a requested `stop` reaches end-turn;
    after owner replacement;
    after owner removal;
    after local branch/subtree detachment;
    after incoming or outgoing message-boundary completion;
    before or during Message Thread termination;
    under implementation-defined memory pressure.

The collector is local to one Message Thread. It does not:

    stop unrelated Message Threads;
    scan foreign owner/arena graphs;
    require one global heap lock;
    perform global tracing as a source-semantic requirement.

It may inspect:

    the current Message Thread owner tree;
    local arenas and branch roots;
    allocation descriptors;
    explicit lazy-owned edges;
    borrowed-edge metadata;
    local reachability metadata;
    continuation and future roots;
    message-frame roots;
    implementation priority and memory-pressure metadata.

It may:

    cut unreachable local branches;
    release frame-local arenas;
    release handler-local arenas;
    release transaction arenas;
    release abandoned retry-attempt arenas;
    release detached branch arenas no longer rooted;
    promote explicit deferred-owned edges when required;
    pin source owners when required;
    detach surviving branch roots as independent owners;
    absorb or reparent complete existing owner regions;
    destroy the complete Message Thread owner graph at termination.

An explicit lazy-owned edge may require data copying as part of its own
contract. An ordinary Structure or Array `return` does not. The collector must
preserve the returned aggregate's exact reference and may retain, detach or
reparent its existing owner region rather than materializing another object.

Actual release is implementation-defined within these constraints. A profile
may release immediately after a local cut, batch releases until the end of the
current handler, or retain ordinary Structure and Array memory until the entire
Message Thread is destroyed.

This is memory collection by effect because it can reclaim memory
automatically. It is not a global tracing collector by mechanics. Its units of
collection are owner regions:

    frame arena;
    handler arena;
    retry-attempt arena;
    transaction arena;
    detachable branch arena;
    continuation arena;
    complete Message Thread owner graph.


19.29.14.1. Terms: Collection and Owner-Tree Cutting

This document does not define a general high-level source operation which
releases an arbitrary ordinary Structure or Array referent.

The term collection means a local memory collection pass for one Message
Thread.

The term owner-tree cutting means a structural update of that Message Thread's
owner graph after edges or roots change.

A collection pass may include:

    owner-tree cutting;
    arena reclamation;
    explicit deferred-owner promotion;
    source-owner pinning;
    branch detachment;
    branch reparenting;
    complete thread-arena destruction.

These mechanisms are not finalization syntax and are not ordinary control-flow
handlers. They are runtime memory machinery attached to owner lifetime.

An owner-tree cut is logically distinct from physical release. A branch may be
removed from one parent and immediately become a detached surviving root. A
region may be marked reclaimable but physically retained until a batch safe
point. A frozen region may be absorbed into another owner. These choices must
preserve all live references and edge contracts.

The current root-only bootstrap implements only coarse parts of this process:
root allocation, arena-wide destruction, lazy-edge support, arena freezing,
arena absorption and tree-cut/lazy-promotion helpers. More precise per-frame
or per-handler reclamation is future work.


19.29.14.2. Control Exit and Owner Lifetime

Control exits are connected to memory only through owner lifetime.

Generic sequence:

    select the control exit;
    evaluate and preserve any result/failure reference required by that exit;
    establish every surviving owner edge or root;
    mark affected frame, handler, attempt, transaction or branch owners as
        exited, abandoned, detached or destroyed;
    update the local owner graph;
    run required explicit resource cleanups in valid dependency order;
    run a Message Thread local collection pass if this point is selected;
    physically release regions permitted by the active implementation profile;
    complete the control transfer.

For an ordinary StructureReference or ArrayReference return, the result-specific
ordering in 19.29.5 is normative: evaluate and publish the exact existing
reference before any owner which could invalidate it is cut.
Retain/detach/reparent operations may update the owner graph but may not
allocate a replacement aggregate.

The control operations relate to safe points as follows:

    `return`, `break`, `continue`
        leave ordinary executable or loop scopes and may create local
        collection safe points;

    labelled `break`, labelled `continue`
        may leave several nested frames and therefore mark several owner
        regions before one collection pass;

    `fail`
        leaves recoverable failure scopes and may create a safe point;

    `retry`
        abandons one retry-attempt owner and may collect that attempt before
        the next attempt begins;

    `redo`
        restarts the receiver-defined repeatable unit and may abandon its
        attempt-local owners;

    `assert`
        exits through the diagnostic path and may abandon frames or the whole
        Message Thread;

    `stop`
        requests current-Message-Thread termination but does not itself leave
        the current lexical scope; cleanup and collection occur through
        ordinary control flow and the common end-turn boundary;

    Message Thread termination
        destroys the complete ordinary owner graph except for explicitly
        transferred or externally managed resources.

No control operator is a memory operation by itself. Reclamation follows
because owner lifetime changes.

`finally` and generated cleanup handlers run because their enclosing scope or
resource contract requires cleanup. They must not invalidate a surviving
result or failure reference. If a cleanup method itself sends a message or
fails, the selected profile must define ordering and diagnostic behavior rather
than leaving partially destroyed ownership state observable.


19.29.14.3. `assert` and Memory

`assert` represents diagnostic invariant failure, not ordinary declared
recoverable failure flow.

When an assertion condition is false, the runtime records an
`AssertionViolation` diagnostic and exits through the diagnostic path selected
by the execution-context profile.

Default Message Thread sequence:

    the assertion condition is false;
    record the AssertionViolation diagnostic;
    preserve diagnostic data in an owner which survives reporting;
    transfer control to the Message Thread diagnostic root;
    terminate the current task or Message Thread by default after reporting;
    run the local collection pass;
    destroy or release the abandoned owner graph according to the termination
        policy.

An assertion violation is not part of the declared `throws` set.

An assertion violation is not caught by ordinary failure labels or recoverable
failure handlers unless a non-default diagnostic profile explicitly bridges
the two channels.

An assertion violation does not execute `fail` merely because both paths leave
the current body.

Assertion exit may reclaim memory because it abandons owner regions. The
reclamation is performed by the local collector, not by an `assert`-specific
free operation.

If a diagnostic profile keeps the Message Thread alive after the assertion,
only regions abandoned by the diagnostic exit are cut. Persistent state still
reachable from the Message Thread root remains owned unless the diagnostic
policy resets, detaches or destroys it.

Diagnostic payloads obey normal ownership rules. If the diagnostic records a
reference to a value in an owner which will be destroyed, the diagnostic must
already have a surviving representation before destruction. This may be a
preallocated diagnostic record, an explicit serialization/copy operation, an
immutable stable value or a detachable existing owner region. The assertion
exit must not silently reinterpret ordinary Structure or Array `return`
semantics.


19.29.14.4. `guard`, `fail`, `throws` and Handlers

`guard`, `fail`, `throws` and failure handlers define declared recoverable
failure flow. They are distinct from `assert`.

    guard / fail / throws / handlers
        declared, expected, recoverable failure path;

    assert
        diagnostic invariant violation path.

`guard` may detect an expected failure condition and select a failure path.

`fail` exits the current executable body with a declared failure label and an
optional payload.

Default recoverable-failure sequence:

    `guard` detects an expected condition or another operation produces a
        declared failure;
    `fail` selects the declared failure label and optional payload;
    establish the payload reference in an owner visible to the selected
        handler/caller;
    leave the current body for the nearest visible handler or caller failure
        channel;
    mark affected frame, handler or attempt owners abandoned or exited;
    update the owner graph;
    run required cleanup;
    run a local collection pass if this is a selected safe point;
    deliver the structured failure to the handler or caller.

`fail` does not transfer to the diagnostic root by default.

`fail` does not use the assertion channel.

`fail` does not directly release memory. It creates a possible safe point by
leaving an owner context.

A failure payload which must survive the abandoned owner must already be owned
by a surviving owner, be represented in a preexisting result/failure owner, or
reside in an existing region which can be detached/reparented consistently.
An explicit future `copy`/`own` operation may construct a separate failure
payload before the exit. The failure transfer itself must not accidentally
copy an ordinary payload while claiming to preserve its identity.

If a failure crosses a Message Thread boundary, it uses ordinary message
boundary behavior: copy, serialization or whole-branch ownership transfer. A
raw reference into the failing Message Thread owner graph does not cross.

Failure handlers may be lexical handlers, executable labels, caller-side
receivers or message handlers according to the selected syntax/profile. In
every case, memory validity follows the same owner, borrow, exact-return and
message-boundary rules.

Handler re-entry must not observe an abandoned attempt's invalid borrowed
references. A compiler or runtime may reject such a retained borrow, clear the
binding according to its descriptor, or maintain the source owner if the edge
was explicitly established as owned before the failure.


19.29.14.5. `retryable`, `retry` and Attempt Arenas

`retryable` defines a restartable frame. `retry` abandons the current attempt
and re-enters the nearest or named retryable frame.

Default retry sequence:

    select the nearest or named retryable frame;
    preserve any retry control metadata in the outer retryable owner;
    abandon the current attempt;
    mark attempt-local owners as abandoned;
    update the owner graph;
    run required attempt cleanup;
    run the local collector at the retry safe point;
    release or retain abandoned attempt arenas according to the implementation
        profile;
    recreate bindings declared inside the retryable frame;
    begin the next attempt.

`retry` is re-execution, not automatic transactional rollback.

It does not roll back external side effects.

It does not automatically restore outer mutable bindings unless the selected
retryable receiver defines and implements a stronger transaction/snapshot
policy.

Values which must survive `retry` belong to an owner outside the abandoned
attempt. A borrow into attempt-local storage cannot remain valid after that
attempt is destroyed. Survival may use an already outer-owned object, a
preallocated retry-state Structure, an existing detachable region, or an
explicit ownership operation performed before retry.

Conceptual distinction:

    attempt-local value
        collectible when the attempt is abandoned;

    value materialized directly in the arena controlled by the outer retryable owner
        survives the abandoned attempt;

    value explicitly copied/owned into the outer owner
        survives according to that explicit operation contract;

    value stored in the Message Thread root
        survives retry unless explicitly replaced, transferred or cut.

This permits repeated attempts to reclaim their temporary regions without a
global tracing collector. Each abandoned attempt loses its local owner graph,
and the next attempt begins with fresh attempt-local bindings.

If a `retry` path carries a diagnostic or reason Structure to the next attempt,
that Structure follows the same rule: it must already live in the outer owner
or an existing surviving region before the attempt arena is cut. Retry does not
perform an implicit return-style promotion or copy.


19.29.14.6. Owner Replacement and Null-Like Removal

A plain-name rebind appends a namespace occurrence and does not remove the
ownership edge held by the preceding occurrence. When an occurrence ceases to
be active, or when an addressable owner replacement, explicit removal or
profile-defined null-like operation actually removes an ownership edge, the
implementation may run the local owner-tree cut procedure.

Conceptual sequence:

    identify the old owner edge;
    establish the replacement edge, if any;
    remove the old edge from the local owner graph;
    determine whether the old branch/subtree still has a surviving owner root;
    process explicit lazy-owned edges reachable from surviving owners;
    promote, pin or redirect those explicit edges as their contracts require;
    preserve a detached branch only when it became an independent surviving
        owner;
    mark every otherwise unreachable region reclaimable;
    physically release according to the implementation profile.

This is the ordinary memory-cutter path for local owner-tree mutation.

It does not scan all objects in the process.

It does not stop other Message Threads.

It does not inspect another Message Thread's private owner graph.

A null-like primitive stored in a field is not necessarily an owner operation.
Whether assigning it removes an ownership edge depends on the field descriptor.
A borrowed view can be cleared without releasing its source owner. An owned
edge removal may make a whole branch collectible. An external handle may
require an explicit close/release operation exposed by a namespace-visible
descriptor.

Replacing one local reference slot does not destroy an ordinary Structure or
Array if another surviving edge or owner root still refers to its region. Conversely,
keeping only an invalid borrowed pointer in an untracked low-level slot does
not keep an owner alive; explicit L2 pointer use follows the low-level lifetime
contract and may become invalid after the owner is released.


19.29.14.7. Emergency Memory Pressure

Emergency memory pressure is runtime policy over owner cancellation and
destruction. It does not turn the language into a global object-by-object
collector.

A runtime may urgently reclaim memory by cancelling, failing, aborting or
destroying selected:

    Message Threads;
    handlers;
    transactions;
    retry attempts;
    branch owners;
    caches or immutable-store generations;
    external-provider mappings.

Selection may use priority, scheduling policy, service-level policy,
diagnostic state, retry count, memory size, responsiveness or explicit resource
budgets.

Default emergency sequence for destroying a Message Thread:

    select a victim Message Thread or task;
    request cooperative cancellation, declared failure, diagnostic termination
        or forced termination according to profile;
    prevent new work from publishing roots into the selected owner graph;
    mark the owner graph abandoned or terminating;
    complete or abort external-resource cleanup according to the emergency
        profile;
    run the local collector if execution state permits it;
    destroy the selected owner regions or complete Message Thread arena graph;
    return the region memory to the implementation allocator;
    emit failure/diagnostic evidence through a surviving supervisor channel.

This is garbage collection by effect because memory is reclaimed
automatically. It is not global tracing by mechanics. The unit selected for
emergency reclamation is an owner region rather than an arbitrary individual
object.

Forced termination may be unable to run ordinary user cleanup safely. A
profile which admits it must distinguish:

    region memory which the runtime can reclaim mechanically;
    external resources which require cooperative close/rollback;
    whole-branch payloads already transferred elsewhere;
    immutable shared storage owned by another provider;
    diagnostic data which must survive in the supervisor owner.

Emergency collection must not reclaim a region which has already been
successfully transferred to another Message Thread. Sender-side termination
invalidates sender handles but does not destroy the receiver-owned branch.

The current root-only bootstrap normally reclaims its ordinary owner region at
process/root-thread termination. Fine-grained victim selection and supervisor
policy are future Message Thread runtime work.


19.29.14.8. Collection Ordering Rule

For every control exit which changes owner lifetime, owner/result state
transitions are ordered before local reclamation.

Required general abstract order:

    1. decide the control exit, recoverable failure, retry, diagnostic exit,
       message transfer or termination;

    2. evaluate result, failure and diagnostic expressions while their source
       owners are valid;

    3. publish exact surviving ordinary StructureReferences or ArrayReferences
       only to same-owner preexisting caller, continuation, handler or
       supervisor roots;

    4. perform any explicitly requested copy, serialization or whole-branch
       transfer operation for an inter-Message-Thread boundary, commit it and
       publish the resulting receiver-owned representation or transferred
       branch to the target message root;

    5. retain, detach or reparent existing owner regions as required, without
       replacing ordinary returned aggregates;

    6. mark affected owners exited, abandoned, transferred or destroyed;

    7. update the local owner graph and remove obsolete edges;

    8. execute required resource cleanup while its dependencies remain valid;

    9. run the local collection pass if the point is selected;

   10. release arenas whose owners are destroyed and whose implementation
       profile permits release now;

   11. complete the control/message transfer.

For ordinary StructureReference or ArrayReference `return`, steps 3 and 5 are
owner/root metadata updates only. They perform no object allocation, clone,
copy, lazy promotion or materialization.

For a future explicit same-Message-Thread `copy`/`own` expression, allocation
belongs to evaluation in step 2 because that operation itself requests a new
owned representation. A boundary copy or serialization may instead allocate in
step 4. The subsequent `return`, if any, still returns exactly the representation
which the explicit operation already produced.

For whole-branch transfer, step 4 changes ownership of an existing region and
invalidates sender-side edges. It need not change object identity.

Borrowed references into a destroyed owner are invalid after the cut/release.
A surviving owner may retain valid data only if:

    it already owned the data;
    the existing data owner remains alive;
    the existing region was detached/reparented/transferred consistently;
    an explicit copy/serialization operation created another representation;
    immutable/external sharing is allowed by the selected profile.

The runtime must avoid a window in which a collector sees a selected result as
unrooted. Publishing or reserving the result edge must occur before the source
owner becomes collectible.

In a precise future implementation, compiler/runtime metadata for a return may
be reserved in the caller/continuation frame before entering the callee. The
callee can then publish a reference without allocating on the return path.


19.29.14.9. Mandatory Message-Boundary Collection Policy

The default Message Thread collector scheduling policy is regular rather than
arbitrary.

In the full default Message Thread model, a local collection pass runs at
message boundaries.

A boundary is completed after any of these events:

    an incoming message payload has been copied, reconstructed by
        serialization, or ownership-transferred into a valid receiver owner;

    an outgoing message payload has been copied, serialized or transferred to
        the target queue, transport or target owner;

    a message handler completes normally;

    a message handler exits by `return`, `fail`, `retry`, `redo`, assertion
        diagnostic exit, cancellation or Message Thread termination;

    a reply/failure has been committed to its future, continuation or target
        message owner.

At each completed boundary, the full default runtime runs one local collection
pass before continuing ordinary message processing.

The pass may be a cheap no-op when the local owner graph did not change.

It is local to the current Message Thread.

It does not stop other Message Threads.

It does not scan foreign arena graphs.

It does not require a global heap lock.

It does not perform global tracing collection.

It may inspect only the current Message Thread's owner graph, local arenas,
branch metadata, explicit deferred-owned edges, borrowed-edge metadata,
continuation roots, message-frame roots and implementation priority metadata.

The collector must preserve:

    owner validity;
    no-escaping-borrow;
    exact ordinary StructureReference/ArrayReference return identity;
    no hidden return allocation/copy/promotion;
    no raw mutable cross-Message-Thread references;
    copy/serialization/whole-branch-transfer boundary rules;
    valid external-resource ownership and cleanup ordering.

The regular processing pattern is:

    receive message
        -> commit payload into receiver ownership
        -> run local collection pass
        -> validate, schedule or execute handler work

    send message
        -> commit payload transfer to queue/transport/target owner
        -> run local collection pass
        -> continue current handler

    finish handler
        -> publish result/reply/failure roots
        -> run required cleanup
        -> run local collection pass
        -> wait for or receive next message

The current hosted bootstrap's root-only arena may implement these collection
calls as no-ops for ordinary root-owned Structures and Arrays and release
everything at root termination. That is a valid initial implementation. It must still
preserve boundary ownership semantics when actual inter-Message-Thread
transport is introduced.

Concretely, both the generated direct-root `end_turn` path and every
pool-scheduled end-turn path run actor-local collection. A normal callback turn
increments both turn and collection counters. An actor stopped after it was
queued may skip entry execution but still crosses the final
collection/end-turn boundary before becoming STOPPED.

The collection call invokes `lm_own_tree_cut(root_owner)`. At present
`lm_own_tree_cut` performs lazy-owned-edge promotion. It does not yet discover
and reclaim arbitrary unreachable objects or precise frame, handler, attempt
and branch regions. Consequently this pass is normally a collection safe
point/no-op for ordinary root-owned Structures and Arrays, which remain
allocated until Message Thread destruction.

The next precise reclamation step is a turn/handler owner beneath the Message
Thread root. Turn-local materialization may use the arena controlled by that
owner; references which must survive are materialized in an arena controlled
by a preselected surviving owner or preserve their existing region by
retain/detach/reparent operations according to section 19.29.5 and the ordering
rule in section 19.29.14.8.
After those edges are established, the non-surviving turn owner can be
destroyed as one region. This refinement must not allocate, copy or promote an
ordinary Structure or Array merely because `return` publishes its reference.

A failed collection records collector failure and uses the failure-dominant
runtime request. It therefore cannot be hidden by an earlier successful
`stop:` or process-entry `return: 0`; the stopping turn retains a nonzero
failure status.


19.29.14.10. Long Message Thread Computations and Supervisor Termination

A Message Thread in the default profile is expected to remain message
responsive.

The default runtime is not required to interrupt arbitrary deep computation
inside one handler merely to run the local collector. If a Message Thread enters
a long computation and does not reach a message boundary or another declared
cooperative yield point within its watchdog limit, a supervisor may cancel,
fail, abort or destroy that Message Thread according to scheduling and
diagnostic policy.

This is intentional. Long computations which must coexist with message
scheduling and regular local collection should be expressed as stepwise or
resumable work.

Possible designs include:

    lazy receiver/operator evaluation;
    iterator or `yield`-style production;
    query/search receivers split into bounded steps;
    continuation messages to the same or another Message Thread;
    futures whose producers execute bounded work units;
    retryable frames with bounded attempts;
    explicit handler-level work slicing;
    profile-defined cooperative yield points.

The corresponding source/runtime operators such as `send`, `await`, `yield`,
`schedule` and `thread` have independent implementation status. The conceptual
memory model does not falsely mark an unimplemented source receiver as
available.

If an algorithm must execute as one long uninterrupted low-level operation, it
belongs to an explicit L2 execution profile with defined memory,
synchronization, cancellation and lifetime behavior. Such a worker is not
automatically a Message Thread merely because it runs on an OS thread. It may
be wrapped by a Message Thread facade which sends it commands and receives
typed results.

Therefore:

    default Message Thread
        message-driven, collected at boundaries, supervisable;

    long Message Thread algorithm
        split into lazy, stepwise, continuation or message-visible units;

    long uninterrupted machine algorithm
        explicit L2 worker/profile, normally hidden behind a high-level
        Structure/handle/message adapter for L3 clients.

Emergency memory pressure uses the same ownership principle. A supervisor may
reclaim memory by terminating low-priority or unresponsive Message Threads. The
memory reclaimed is the selected owner regions or whole selected thread graph,
not individual objects discovered through one process-wide tracing heap.


19.29.15. Future L2 Low-Level Threads, Shared Blocks and Explicit Release

This structural memory model does not yet define the complete source API for:

    standalone low-level execution threads;
    raw shared memory blocks;
    direct allocator calls;
    explicit `freemem`-style release;
    low-level cancellation and join;
    complete atomic/memory-order operations.

Portable host-thread, mutex and condition operations now exist as an
implementation/runtime ABI. This subsection remains future work for a generic
source-facing L2 machine-thread and shared-memory API. The host ABI is not
currently a `machine.thread:` or another public source receiver.

These are future L2 operations because their source semantics expose machine
storage, addresses, synchronization or allocator lifetime. L1 remains the
external implementation/lowering substrate and is not a Lingvamyxa source
level. L3 may use checked wrappers but does not directly expose these machine
operations.

The ordinary Message Thread model remains:

    local owner/arena graph;
    owner-tree cutting and local collection;
    exact-reference ordinary Structure/Array return;
    message-boundary copy, serialization or whole-branch transfer;
    no raw mutable reference crossing an inter-Message-Thread boundary.

A future L2 profile may define operations for:

    creating a standalone low-level execution thread;
    joining, cancelling or terminating that thread;
    allocating a raw memory block;
    allocating a shared memory block;
    mapping a file or external buffer;
    publishing shared storage;
    synchronizing with atomics, locks and barriers;
    defining memory-order rules;
    releasing raw/shared storage through a matching explicit operation;
    wrapping the resource in a checked arena-allocated StructureReference.

Conceptual future L2 sketch:

    machine.thread: workerThread workerEntry workerArgs
    mem: sharedBlock bytes shared(mutable)
    freemem: sharedBlock

This sketch is not normative current syntax. The corresponding operators are
NOT IMPLEMENTED unless an active L2 profile explicitly installs their bindings.

A low-level execution thread is not automatically a Message Thread. It may
expose raw addresses, shared mutable buffers and platform synchronization under
its explicit L2 profile. A Message Thread is the higher structural runtime
unit with an owner graph, queue and message boundary.

L3 code may see:

    a checked typed handle;
    a descriptor;
    a capability;
    a service/message adapter;
    an automatically bounds-checked collection;

rather than the raw address, allocator or synchronization operation.

An explicit low-level `freemem` operation, when implemented, is not:

    a local Message Thread collection pass;
    an owner-tree cut;
    an ordinary Structure/Array destructor;
    a message-boundary rule;
    a high-level `return` operation.

It is a low-level release primitive for memory allocated by the matching L2
allocation profile. Its validity requires a contract which prevents double
release, mismatched allocators, use after release and release while another raw
user remains active.

Future profile work must define at least:

    exact L2 source spellings;
    allocation ownership and matching release rules;
    shared-block publication rules;
    memory ordering and synchronization;
    thread start/join/cancel semantics;
    interaction with Message Thread wrapper handles;
    interaction with service and device adapters;
    diagnostics for double release and use after release;
    cross-level escape diagnostics;
    which operations are always L2-only;
    external-provider failure and cleanup behavior.

Until such a profile is specified and implemented, raw shared blocks and
standalone low-level threads remain L2/L1 implementation mechanisms, while
ordinary L2/L3 Message Thread code communicates through messages and
arena-owned Structures/Arrays.


19.29.16. Normative Summary and Implementation Status

The normative memory rules are:

    ordinary Structure / Array
        stable reference to an arena-allocated referent;

    current arena
        default destination of ordinary construction;

    explicit target arena
        available only through an operation whose namespace-visible signature
        accepts it;

    module/top-level value
        allocated in a module or root arena;

    binding / argument / return
        forwards the existing reference without aggregate copy;

    return
        publishes the exact existing reference without allocation, copy,
        materialization, owner-copy, lazy promotion or identity change;

    borrow
        does not extend source arena lifetime;

    explicit ownership operation
        may copy, share, detach or establish a lazy-owned edge according to its
        explicit namespace-visible contract, independently of return;

    Message Thread
        owns an isolated local arena/owner graph;

    inter-Message-Thread boundary
        copy, serialization or whole-branch transfer; no raw mutable reference;

    collection
        local owner-region reclamation, not required global tracing;

    L2
        may expose low-level machine allocation, raw storage, addresses,
        synchronization and explicit release;

    L3
        excludes those low-level machine operations but is not a sandbox,
        authority or purity level;

    stack / heap / static
        not ordinary Lingvamyxa object-lifetime categories;

    Registry / Table
        ordinary Structure/Array data with no implicit allocation or ownership
        role;

    ANSI C storage duration
        backend detail that must implement, not define, arena lifetime;

    L1
        external implementation/lowering substrate, not a Lingvamyxa source
        level.

Current hosted-bootstrap implementation:

    `libown`, `LmOwnArena` and actor-local owner graphs exist;
    `LmMessageThread` state and RUNNING / STOPPING / STOPPED transitions exist;
    generated standalone programs create an application runtime, attach an
        explicit unpooled root actor and drive its external process-entry turn
        loop directly;
    no process-global current/root Message Thread or TLS scheduler binding exists;
    portable pthread, Win32 and `single` host providers exist;
    `LmMessageThreadRuntime`, N-worker pools and FIFO actor scheduling exist;
    native pools execute different actors concurrently while serializing turns
        of each individual actor;
    an actor may migrate between host workers without moving or sharing its arena;
    `single` performs the same actor scheduling cooperatively without OS workers;
    pool-owned actor start, self-stop/failure, controller join, pool stop and
        lifecycle destruction exist;
    every scheduled end-turn performs actor-local collection;
    `stop:` is an implemented L2/L3 cooperative actor receiver;
    process-entry `return` requests root-loop stop after generated cleanup;
    ordinary stop is first-request-wins, while diagnostic/runtime collection
        failures use a failure-dominant status channel;
    explicitly constructed local and direct module-level named/anonymous
        Structures, `fm` result Structures, arena-backed local/module Array
        values and closure environments/descriptors are root-arena managed;
    named-Structure and physical-`merge:` descriptor declarations emit only
        namespace descriptors and hosted type/layout projections, never object
        storage or arena allocations;
    direct module-level named Structure, anonymous `():` and ordinary `[]:`
        sites use a source-ordered initializer after root attachment and retain
        only private static reference slots; non-source runtime tables remain a
        static-backed path which must migrate to module/root arenas;
    arena allocation, array allocation, descriptors, lazy edges, freeze,
        absorption and tree-cut helpers exist;
    root-arena destruction releases ordinary generated Structures, Array
        objects/headers and owned Array backing storage;
    partial-callable binder ownership and other remaining unscoped allocation
        paths still require separate owner integration;
    generated standalone programs construct a worker pool only when `thread:`
        handlers are present; the application runtime and attached root exist
        in either case;
    pooled mailbox actors, owned-LMX FIFO inbox/outbox queues and exact local
        route-to-MessageThread delivery are implemented;
    empty/NULL endpoint selects local delivery; a per-runtime REST/LMX provider
        ABI accepts normalized absolute HTTP(S) targets and complete binary LMX
        bodies, while an unconfigured runtime fails remote sends explicitly;
    the hosted outbound adapter implements that ABI with libcurl on supported
        Unix-like builds, WinHTTP on Windows, or an explicit `none` provider;
    the transport-neutral inbound admission ABI copies complete binary LMX
        bodies into route-selected FIFO inboxes, wakes idle actors and rejects
        new external work once application exit or route shutdown begins;
    the hosted inbound REST/LMX adapter implements byte-exact endpoint-base and
        route mapping with CivetWeb, or an explicit `none` provider; generated
        startup opens it after source routes and blocks in server stop before
        route, pool and runtime destruction;
    source `thread:` declarations create one route-bound mailbox actor and lower
        one dequeued LMX document to one `IncomingMessage` handler turn;
    source `send:` is implemented for local and explicitly addressed HTTP(S)
        forms, evaluates its arguments once in order and lowers through the
        transactional per-turn outbox ABI;
    root and actor outboxes roll back a failed turn; successful entries publish
        FIFO, and teardown drains cascaded local messages to pool quiescence;
    source `exit:` is implemented as a first-request-wins cooperative
        application shutdown; the requester completes its turn, the generated
        controller stops and joins the application pool, completes the root
        boundary and releases runtime resources before host return;
    the neutral remote-provider hook, delivery-result mapping, outbound HTTP
        client adapter, transport-neutral inbound admission and inbound HTTP
        server adapter are implemented; message parsing/validation, direct
        inbound TLS, reply routing and envelope/address
        consistency checking remain separate work;
    `reply`, `await` and `schedule` are not yet implemented as generic source
        operations; shutdown across several manually constructed runtimes or
        pools is not coordinated by the current source profile;
    a runtime frame/cleanup stack for unwinding through nested Message Thread
        calls is not yet implemented;
    a distinct disposable per-turn/handler arena is not yet implemented;
    current tree cutting promotes lazy-owned edges but does not reclaim general
        unreachable root-arena objects;
    precise frame/handler/branch collection is not yet complete.

Proposed but NOT IMPLEMENTED as generic source operations:

    copy;
    move;
    borrow;
    own;
    reply;
    await;
    schedule;
    general low-level freemem/thread/shared-block profile.

Conceptual future runtime material in this section describes required semantics
and possible lowering shapes. It must not be confused with already registered
source operators or with the current C ABI.


19.30. Explicit Reactive Registry and Query Receivers

19.30.1. Reactive Layer

An explicitly held Registry value may be updated by an operation that also
produces events. Events are messages. Query receivers run inside Message Threads
and receive the Registry/Table to query as an explicit operand.

19.30.2. Agent Candidates

An agent receiver may propose candidate rows or messages. A validator must
accept, reject or rank the candidate before an explicit operation updates the
target Registry/Table. Namespace state is unchanged.

19.30.3. Boundaries and Bootstrap

Reactive Registry processing obeys Message Thread memory/message boundaries. A
minimal bootstrap may implement only:

    row inserted;
    query receiver;
    candidate row;
    validation;
    accepted row.


19.31. HTTP, Service and Transport Operators

19.31.1. Status

HTTP Lingvamyxa REST is a built-in service transport profile. It is not the
core execution model.

19.31.2. Service Instance

A service instance is a bundle of Message Threads connected by standard messages.
The bundle is not a shared mutable heap.

19.31.3. HTTP to Message

HTTP request handling is:

    HTTP request
        -> route table lookup
        -> IncomingMessage
        -> receiver validator/merge
        -> executor
        -> result/failure message
        -> HTTP response mapping

The route map is an ordinary explicitly configured value, commonly a Table.
External HTTP data becomes an LMX message before service dispatch.

The standard request target is the normalized `MessageAddress.endpoint +
MessageAddress.route`. A remote sender posts the complete LMX Message document
as the request body. The receiving application resolves only the route part in
its local route table. An HTTP route never names an OS worker or exposes a raw
MessageThread handle.

The core runtime does not own an HTTP client singleton. Its per-runtime
REST/LMX provider receives the already normalized request URI and an exact
borrowed byte span, performs one POST with media type `application/lmx`, and
returns either an HTTP admission status or a native transport failure. This
keeps the message address, outbox and shutdown semantics identical for a test
provider, libcurl, WinHTTP or a later transport implementation.

19.31.4. Serialization, Client/Server and Service Boundaries

Serialization adapters are namespace-visible codec operations, optionally
configured by explicit Tables. HTTP client/server adapters are services or
hosted native adapters that inject messages into Message Threads.
Service boundaries obey the same memory and authority rules as Message Thread
boundaries, with stronger default isolation for remote input.

The initial REST/LMX media type is `application/lmx`. HTTP status and response
mapping describe transport admission and, in a request/response profile, may
carry the resulting LMX reply. An asynchronous profile may acknowledge inbox
admission first and later deliver the semantic reply to `replyTo`; in both cases
the semantic result/failure is an ordinary LMX Message with `correlation` equal
to the request `id`.


19.32. Authentication and Authorization Operators

19.32.1. Status

Authentication and authorization are not a separate role system. They are
explicit verifier/policy values, validators and evidence attached to a message
flow. Tables are one optional representation.

19.32.2. Primary Credential and Verifier

A verifier is a protected Structure/value that can validate a primary
credential without storing the credential itself.

The default password verifier may use libsodium password hashing in the C99
runtime profile.

19.32.3. Default Installation Credential

A fresh installation may have a system user name and an empty primary password.
This is not global security policy. It is explicit initial verifier state. A
user or administrator may replace it with a stronger verifier.

19.32.4. Verifier Realms and Protected Objects

Protected objects may have their own verifier realm:

    protected module;
    protected service;
    protected archive;
    protected directory;
    protected administration operation.

Password reset may reset an access verifier to an empty/default state. It does
not recover separately encrypted content whose key depended on the old verifier.

A protected object may require:

    one verifier;
    multiple verifiers for one user;
    a quorum of users/sessions;
    creator-parent tracking.

Parent/creator rows do not grant content rights by themselves.

19.32.5. Delegated Ticket

After primary credential validation, the runtime may issue a delegated ticket
for a module or service. A profile may represent ticket/evidence data in a Table:

    ticket[user][module] = ticket_id
    ticketAuthority[ticket_id][operation] = allowed
    ticketExpiry[ticket_id] = time

Changing or resetting the primary verifier invalidates dependent tickets by its
explicit policy descriptor.

19.32.6. External Authentication Modules

OAuth or OS-login modules are ordinary L2/L3 modules. How they obtain authority
is defined by the selected authentication/authorization profile, not by their
source level. They do not replace the explicitly configured verifier. They may request or
receive delegated tickets after the primary policy allows it.

19.32.7. Authentication Evidence Rows

Authentication validation produces an explicit `AuthEvidence` value,
represented by a StructureReference or ArrayReference. Its fields/rows may
include:

    authUser[flow] = user
    authVerifier[flow] = verifierRealm
    authTicket[flow] = ticketId
    authority[flow][operation] = allowed
    guarantee[flow] = sessionQuorumSatisfied

Message validators receive that evidence reference explicitly and may require
these fields/rows before producing a ConcreteExpression or ExecutionPlan. No
ambient evidence table is implied.

19.32.8. ANSI C / libsodium Runtime Sketch

A C99 runtime profile may use libsodium for:

    password hashing/verifier creation;
    random ticket material;
    constant-time comparisons;
    secure memory helpers where the runtime owns the memory region.

UI password entry is outside libsodium's control unless the UI layer is also
implemented with memory handling that the runtime owns.

19.32.9. Standard Statement

Authentication is a validation/evidence layer over the message pipeline.

    credential/verifier -> validator -> authority evidence -> message validation

Authorization is an explicit operation over that evidence and policy. The
operation may query a supplied Table; it is not a global role hierarchy.


19.33. Operator and Runtime Summary

This integrated model reduces to these rules:

    Value = Primitive | StructureReference | ArrayReference;
    Structure / Array = arena-owned referent observed through its reference;
    namespace = Name -> ordered Binding occurrences at a source point;
    ordinary lookup = newest still-active occurrence of the exact name;
    declaration / import / plain-name rebind = explicit namespace-changing operation;
    construction = resolve descriptor, allocate in the current arena,
                  initialize and return/bind a reference;
    construction side effects = no class/layout/field/backend rows and no
                  namespace change except an explicit result binding;
    descriptor = ordinary StructureReference explicitly bound in namespace;
    compatibility / implements = first-level-name subset comparison of
                  namespace-visible descriptors;
