    table / Registry / RegistryView = optional ordinary values composed from
                  Structures and Arrays;
    table name / key / row / cell = data, never an implicit namespace binding;
    table query / join / overlay = explicit operations over explicit operands;
    expression = Structure whose receiver/operator names resolve in namespace;
    provider = explicit namespace binding/descriptor, optionally configured by
                  ordinary Tables;
    ANSI C layout/storage = backend projection, never source-semantic evidence;
    message = one LMX document with a standard envelope and ordinary LMX expression;
    message address = optional REST/LMX application endpoint plus required inbox route;
    message id = opaque identity of one message;
    replyTo = optional result/failure MessageAddress;
    correlation = relationship to another message id, never a destination;
    message expression = ordinary LMX admitted by receiver policy;
    validation = staged merge from IncomingMessageStructure to ExecutionPlan;
    executor = run only ExecutionPlans produced by validation/merge;
    autonomous/constrained profiles = explicit tables for budgets, freshness,
                uncertainty, idempotence/replay, effect fences, degradation and
                decision logs;
    L3 retry/retryable = local execution-frame restart, not transport resend;
    generator = create proposed messages from result/state/table rules;
    L2/L3 aggregate return = executable-body control exit that forwards the exact
                StructureReference/ArrayReference without copy or promotion;
    message-boundary reply = boundary wrapper from result/failure to ordinary message record;
    Message Thread = isolated actor with state, arenas and namespace/execution
                context; Registry/Table state is optional ordinary data;
    Message Thread pool = explicit FIFO scheduler which maps actor turns onto
                zero cooperative or N native host workers;
    host thread provider = compile-time backend `auto | pthread | win32 | single`;
    stop = implemented cooperative request to finish the explicitly executing
                actor after its current/final turn boundary;
    exit = implemented cooperative coordinator for the generated application
                runtime/root and optional pool; it stops and joins declared
                Message Threads before host-process return;
    memory/auth/service policy = explicit descriptors and values consumed by
                explicitly resolved operations.

20. L2 Primitive, Address and Parser Operators

20.0. Scope of This Section

This section defines the minimal L2 operator/parser-form set.

L2 has the common Value ontology plus explicit low-level typed-address
primitives. Explicit machine storage/allocation forms produce typed-address or
foreign-handle primitives. Receiver signatures and parser profiles assign final
semantics to Structures built from source syntax.

The minimal L2 profile therefore has no ordinary Structure syntax for C-style
direct aggregate value access. Primitive/scalar values may be direct. Explicit
machine aggregate storage is reached by typed address, while every ordinary
Structure or Array expression yields its arena-owned reference. This keeps
`u32: x` simple and prevents an embedded record from
silently becoming a copyable whole-Structure expression.

This section specifies the L2-specific low-level machine operator/parser-form
surface. Its machine forms are not part of L3; ordinary shared forms mentioned
for contrast retain their separately specified L2/L3 availability. In this
sense L3 is smaller than L2: L3 keeps the
high-level algorithmic/control, data/query and runtime surface and excludes raw
storage, address, physical layout-storage and unchecked access. A parser form
from this section is L2-only only when its source semantics expose such machine
state; parser involvement by itself is not a level boundary.

20.1. Pointer and Field Syntax Exclusions

L2 does not use `*`, `&`, `^`, `.`, or `->` as pointer/field syntax.

This rule should be stated once as an L2 invariant instead of repeated at every address example.

Consequences:

    `*`  remains available as a multiplication operator token.
    `&`  is not address-of.
    `^`  is not pointer dereference and is not a field-follow marker.
    `.`  is source indentation only when it appears in the leading dot prefix; it is not field access.
    `->` is not a Lingvamyxa field-follow operator.

L2 address and field forms are:

    @ⁿ: T x     reserved address-depth declaration head, for example `@@@: int ppp`
    @x          address of storage location `x`
    \x          raw load from address expression `x`
    x\field     typed field-follow through address/reference `x`
    x\[n]field  zero-based exact-name occurrence field-follow through `x`
    x[i]        indexed access through address/reference/container `x`

Field-follow and index chains bind tighter than prefix `@` and prefix `\`.

The C-profile mnemonic table in section 11.3 shows how the L2 forms cover the C declarator/expression roles of `*`, `&` and `->` without using those tokens as Lingvamyxa pointer/field syntax.

20.2. Primitive, Array, Address and Indexed Forms

The ordinary `[]:` family constructs an ArrayReference. It is not an ANSI C
array declaration and does not select stack, heap or static storage.

    Primitive: name [initialValue]
    []: ElementType name size [initialValue...]
    []...[]: ElementType name size1 ... sizeN [initialValue...]
    [size1]...[sizeN]: ElementType: name [initialValue...]
    @ⁿ: BaseType name

Examples:

    int: count 5
    []: int leafs count
    @: int p
    @@: int pp
    @@@: int ppp

The `leafs` constructor materializes one Array object/header and its owned
backing storage in the current arena, initializes the requested cells and binds
the resulting ArrayReference. Repeated bracket groups specify rank and
dimensions. Both empty and payload-bearing bracket groups are accepted as
described by the parser profile; missing dimensions are a diagnostic.

The leading `@` fields before an Array element descriptor specify an admitted
low-level address-depth element form. `@ⁿ:` itself produces the explicit L2
typed-address primitive described by the selected low-level profile; it is not
an ArrayReference.

Indexing an ArrayReference uses Array semantics and bounds metadata. Indexing a
raw typed address is a separate L2 low-level operation. If a backend needs raw
fixed C-array storage, it must expose a distinct explicit L2 machine-storage
operation and must not assign that meaning to ordinary `[]:`.

The distinct hosted C99 machine-storage receiver is `c.array`. It has exactly
one logical child, in one of these two forms:

    c.array: ArrayDeclaratorFrame
    c.array: const: ArrayDeclaratorFrame

`ArrayDeclaratorFrame` must have a head made only of one or more bracket groups.
The optional `const` Frame must itself contain exactly that one bracket Frame.
No other child, sibling declaration or qualifier is admitted by the minimal
receiver. Examples:

    c.array: [3]: int: top_values 2 4 6
    c.array:
        [][]: int matrix 2 2
            []: 1 2
            []: 3 4
    ---
    c.array: const: [2]: int: fixed_values 9 11
    c.array: [rows][columns]: int: matrix

The array rank is the number of bracket groups and must be at least one. A
non-empty group such as `[rows]` supplies its own extent expression. Each empty
group consumes exactly one following declarator field after the element type
and binding name, in bracket order. After all empty groups have consumed their
extents, the remaining fields are initializer clauses. A missing extent is a
`c.array` receiver diagnostic; an initializer is never used to infer a missing
extent. An extent supplied after an empty group must be a single atom in the
minimal hosted profile; a compound extent expression is written inside a
non-empty bracket group. Empty and non-empty groups may be mixed.

The element type uses the existing L2 C-declarator type forms, including an
admitted address-depth type for pointer elements. The optional `const` wrapper
has the same C-profile meaning as other declaration composition: it qualifies
the base element type. It does not turn the declaration into an ordinary
immutable ArrayReference.

`c.array` declares target-machine C array storage. It allocates no Array
object/header, arena backing, owner edge, length/shape/stride record or bounds
metadata. The storage is not a Lingvamyxa Value and the declared name is a
machine-storage designator, not an ArrayReference. Where the hosted C expression
profile admits array-to-pointer conversion, consuming the designator produces
the corresponding typed-address Primitive. Its address depth is determined by
the element's admitted address depth plus the one remaining array dimension:
indexing may expose a rank-one subarray for decay, while indexing every array
dimension exposes the element itself. A designator with more than one remaining
dimension is not silently flattened to an unrelated pointer type. Conversion to
or from an ordinary ArrayReference requires an explicit operation which states
the raw backing address, shape and lifetime contract.

At block scope the declaration has C automatic storage duration. Runtime extent
expressions select the admitted C99 variable-length-array rules; a VLA cannot
have an initializer. At module/top level the hosted lowering emits internal-linkage
`static` storage, requires constant extents and applies the C99 requirements for
static initializers. With no initializer, automatic elements are indeterminate
and static elements are zero-initialized. A present initializer follows C99
aggregate initialization. In the conservative hosted bootstrap, a
multidimensional initializer must spell each nested dimension with an explicit
`[]:` or Structure brace group; flat brace-elided scalar clauses are rejected.
Omitted elements are zero-initialized and excess or incompatible initializers
are diagnostics. The conservative hosted bootstrap additionally
requires a top-level address initializer to name storage whose declaration has
already been emitted; a source-forward machine-storage initializer is rejected
rather than reordered behind its dependency.

A block-scope `c.array` has the ordinary lifetime of its C automatic storage.
Capturing that storage into an arena-owned callable would not extend its
lifetime. Until an explicit raw-lifetime capture/adapter operation exists, the
hosted backend rejects such a capture instead of emitting a potentially dangling
closure pointer.

Postfix indexing dispatches from the binding category. For `c.array`, `a[i]` is
unchecked raw C-array indexing and does not use the checked ArrayReference
operation. One bracket suffix consumes one dimension, so multidimensional raw
storage uses `a[i][j]`, not an ArrayReference multi-coordinate shorthand. A
partial index denotes the remaining raw subarray; a full-rank index denotes an
element lvalue and may be read, addressed or used as an update head. No view is
created and no bounds check is synthesized. Statically known dangerous accesses
remain subject to the selected C99 undefined-behavior policy.

Taking the address of a whole or partially indexed C array produces a C
pointer-to-array type rather than another ordinary typed-address depth. The
minimal hosted profile does not yet expose pointer-to-array declarators as a
Lingvamyxa type and rejects that expression instead of misclassifying it as
`T **`; address-of remains admitted after all array dimensions have been
indexed to an element.

The `const` wrapper qualifies the base element, following the declarator rule
above. For an array of pointers, the pointer slot itself remains assignable while
an update that traverses the declared pointer depth and reaches the const base
element is rejected.

The hosted bootstrap provides this exact `c.array` receiver. Intentional raw
arrays in production bootstrap code and low-level fixtures use `c.array`,
including buffers that require C array-to-pointer decay, `sizeof(array)` or a
raw-pointer ABI. `c.array` remains C storage and is not affected by the ordinary
ArrayReference lowering described below.

In the current hosted migration stage, an admitted ordinary `[]:` declaration
inside a managed function or directly on an executable module surface
materializes a persistent `LmSlice` Array header plus arena-owned backing
storage in the executing Message Thread's root owner arena (the generated root
actor's arena at process entry). A direct module site uses the source-ordered
initializer of section 19.29.12.2.1. This applies to both trailing extents and
payload-bearing bracket groups and to repeated declarations. Extent expressions
are evaluated exactly once in source order. The current bootstrap admits
statically typed integer extents in the non-negative `long long` range;
malformed or out-of-range C99 integer literals are rejected before C emission,
and a negative runtime result enters the hosted construction-failure path before
any backing allocation. Untyped `c.*` atoms and calls are not admitted as
ArrayReference extents in this stage, except for the dedicated `c.sizeof(...)`
expression form. Each repeated binding receives a distinct header, and any backing
allocations created for it are distinct rather than shared. Backing storage is
initially zero, after which present initializer elements are assigned in source
order; a multidimensional initializer uses explicit nested groups in this
conservative bootstrap. The hosted header currently stores only `ptr` and the
first-dimension `length`. The translator separately tracks binding-local rank,
element type/address depth and constness; when statically known, it checks them
at typed argument, return and whole-reference-assignment boundaries. Passing,
returning or assigning the ArrayReference publishes the existing header and
does not copy its backing. Prefix `@` applied to the binding addresses the
ArrayReference slot, while `arrayReference\ptr` remains the explicit raw-backing
bridge described below.

At a direct module/top-level site this stage retains only a private static
`LmSlice *` reference slot; the header and backing are root-arena allocations.
The hosted translator rejects such a site when the complete executable has no
unique non-imported root `main`, or when the site is conditionally emitted, as
specified in section 19.29.12.2.1. It also rejects ordinary Array construction
in an external function that has no managed Message Thread context. The current
unchecked indexing projection accepts only an unquoted direct ArrayReference
binding followed by enough immediate bracket suffixes to consume every rank. An
exact-quoted or parenthesized reference or a callable result must first be
rebound to an admitted unquoted ArrayReference binding before a complete index
chain is used. A chain that consumes fewer ranks is rejected until partial-index
Array views are implemented.
Capturing an ordinary ArrayReference through a closure environment, runtime
element descriptors, complete runtime rank/shape/stride/storage metadata in the
Array header, runtime bounds checking for ArrayReference indexing at every rank,
complete multidimensional shape checking and partial-index Array views remain
pending hosted work. Allocation descriptors record owner, count, rank and level
for current backing allocations, but that implementation metadata does not by
itself complete the source-level view contract.

The current hosted validator deliberately accepts a conservative subset of C
constant expressions before emitting top-level storage or an initialized VLA.
Literal/operator expressions and unresolved C macro or enum atoms are admitted;
known runtime bindings, call Frames and call-shaped `c.*(...)` atoms are
rejected. Cast and `sizeof` constant-expression classification is pending a
full C-expression classifier and may therefore be rejected even when a C99
compiler could prove the expression constant. This is a hosted limitation, not
a change to the C99 constant-expression rules above.

Until an explicit raw-storage/ArrayReference adapter receiver is registered,
the hosted translator conservatively rejects a typed boundary expression that
directly contains a `c.array` designator where an ArrayReference is required,
or an ArrayReference where a raw pointer is required. This includes casts and
compound expressions whose result category is not proven independently of their
root storage. For a direct `c.array` chain the hosted classifier tracks declared
rank, consumed postfix indices and element address depth: a rank-one remaining
subarray may decay to its corresponding raw address, and a full-rank index is an
element rather than the whole designator. Partial-array update heads remain
invalid because C arrays are not assignable. It must not emit an incompatible
pointer conversion and delegate the distinction to the C compiler.

The existing hosted spelling `arrayReference\ptr` (and a cast wrapped directly
around that spelling) is the current explicit backing-address bridge. It yields
only the raw data address; it does not carry length, rank or lifetime, and the
consumer is responsible for the missing contract. It is retained for bootstrap
compatibility until the dedicated adapter receiver replaces it.

Names that contain spaces use exact quoted identifiers as ordinary fields:

@: int `second slot`

This declares an address-depth-1 primitive slot with base type `int` and
introduces the exact quoted name `second slot`.

The same short-tail whitespace rule applies to address-depth declarations:

@@@: int ppp

This declares `ppp` as an address-depth-3 slot with base type `int`. The spelling `@@@: int, ppp` is the same declaration with an explicit comma field separator. The spelling `@@@: int; ppp` is different: the semicolon terminates the `@@@:` short form after `int`, so `ppp` is no longer the binding-name field of that declaration. The following `ppp` is parsed as the next source item at the current level.

20.2.1. Namespace Descriptors and ANSI C Layout Projection

At the parser level, a form such as `Name:` is a Structure/Frame and has no
intrinsic C-layout meaning. A structural descriptor affects resolution only
when an explicit declaration or import binds that descriptor in the namespace.

Constructing an instance resolves its descriptor, allocates one Structure in
the current arena and returns/binds a StructureReference. At module level the
current arena is the module or root arena. Construction does not create
descriptor rows, `layout.backend` facts, field tables or other Registry data.

A backend may derive a physical layout from a namespace-visible descriptor or
from a compile-time-known Structure shape. It may emit an ANSI C `typedef
struct`, accessors or internal layout metadata. These artifacts do not mutate
the namespace and are not Lingvamyxa values. A projected ordinary instance
remains arena-managed; ANSI C automatic/static duration never defines its
source lifetime. Static backing is conforming only when it is an internal
implementation of a module-arena contract.

For example, a descriptor explicitly bound as `LmTransSymbol` may expose the
logical fields `name`, `kind` and `depth`. A hosted backend may project it as:

---ANSI C---
typedef struct LmTransSymbol LmTransSymbol;

struct LmTransSymbol {
    LmP0Text *name;
    LmTransSymbolKind kind;
    unsigned depth;
};
---ANSI C---

This C declaration is a projection, not the identity or definition of the
source descriptor. A Table may be passed to an explicit descriptor-conversion
operation as authoring data, but the table name, keys and cells declare nothing
by themselves.

The current named-Structure fallback is confined to descriptor-driven C
type/layout projection and creates no source value or backing storage. Remaining
top-level untracked static backing in other bootstrap paths is not normative
language semantics and must be removed or confined behind an explicit backend
representation of the module/root-arena contract.

20.2.1.1. Source Descriptors and Target Projection Are Separate

The source-semantic input is an explicit descriptor bound in the namespace.
That descriptor is an ordinary Structure. A Table may store descriptor-like
data, but table names and rows do not implicitly introduce descriptors, fields
or namespace bindings.

Type checking and `implements` read namespace-visible descriptors. Backend
layout projection consumes an already resolved descriptor and may choose C
structs, pointers, handles, universal records or accessors without changing
source semantics.

A projected non-primitive Structure field contains a StructureReference or
ArrayReference, never an embedded Lingvamyxa aggregate value. An ArrayReference
may project to a header plus backing allocation, but both remain governed by
their arena contract.

`LmSlice` and similar C records are backend ABI helpers, not mandatory
source-semantic registry classes. Likewise, C `const`, `struct`, `enum`,
`union`, pointer depth, alignment and forward declarations are target concepts.
An explicit backend profile may store projection choices in ordinary Tables,
but those Tables do not define the source namespace or Value ontology.

Thus source description and backend projection may coexist in one bootstrap
translator while remaining one-way dependencies:

    namespace descriptor -> resolved Structure/Array semantics
                         -> selected backend representation

The backend representation never creates the source descriptor retroactively.

A backend may maintain implementation-private projection state to ensure that a
target declaration is emitted once. The hosted C bootstrap scopes this state to
one output translation and shares it across imported declarations, prelude,
prototypes, bodies and bounded lexical-scope replays. It is reset before another
output is translated. This state is not a namespace binding, descriptor,
Registry or Table, and source code cannot observe or modify it. In particular,
a Table or relation named `typedef.emitted` has no privileged effect on backend
output.

Projection-state identity is the identity of the target artifact, not evidence
that a source descriptor exists. For a hosted C typedef managed by this shared
projection state, the collision key is its target identifier namespace, C
lexical scope and canonical emitted C identifier. Each declaration at that key
also carries a normalized complete target-declaration fingerprint and a
structural target-presence path. The fingerprint includes every token that
changes the projected C type, including struct/union/function-pointer category,
ordered fields, effective pointer depth, constness, array shape, return type and
parameters. The current bootstrap uses the exact unguarded declaration bytes
emitted at canonical indentation as this fingerprint, so comparison cannot
drift from generated C.

A prior declaration may suppress a later emission only when their fingerprints
are equal, the prior presence path dominates the later path and the prior
declaration is not later in final target-output order. The hosted C bootstrap
emits the prelude before the body, irrespective of the order in which translator
operations happened to request those outputs. Thus a body claim made first
cannot suppress a later prelude claim. Equal declarations under independent
guards therefore remain separately guarded emissions. A declaration emitted in
one conditional branch is not recorded as unconditional.

Different fingerprints at one collision key are an error whenever their
presence paths may overlap. They may coexist only in branches that the emitted
target structure proves mutually exclusive, such as siblings of the same
`#if`/`#elif`/`#else` group. Independent guards are not assumed exclusive.
Leaving a branch removes it from the active dominance path but does not erase
its declaration from per-output conflict validation. A later unconditional
declaration must consequently be compatible with every earlier conditional
alternative that it can overlap.

True block-local typedef fallback remains scoped to its C block and does not
publish translation-unit projection state or a translation-unit target guard.
It may be skipped only when an identical already-projected declaration dominates
that block. The projection state and generated target guard must not introduce a
source name, establish source compatibility, select a layout, or make a
first-declaration-wins choice.

For C tag-backed projections, a `c.struct` or `c.named-struct` layout is
compatible only with a `c.struct` forward declaration, and a `c.union` or
`c.named-union` layout only with a `c.union` forward declaration. Mixing the tag
families at one projected name is a projection error.

The common projection state covers named structures, physical `merge:`,
non-forward `c.struct`/`c.union` layout typedefs, L4 alias/forward declarations,
function-pointer typedefs and implementation-private generated ABI typedefs.
The latter include callable closure layouts and aliases, capture and binder
environments, `fm` return layouts, Table row layouts and fixed
Message-Thread/runtime prelude types. Their generated names and ordering are
backend constraints and do not define language semantics. Translation-unit and
block-local claims use the same state; local claims additionally carry the
identity of their C lexical scope.

The current hosted bootstrap rejects creation of a lazy callable binder while
emitting a structural conditional branch. Its helper functions cannot yet be
hoisted safely when the source callable or prototype exists only in that
branch. This is an explicit backend limitation, not a source-language
restriction or a license to emit a branch-local typedef outside the shared
collision domain.

True block-local named/merged Structure and Table-row fallback typedefs are
claimed in that common state before emission. Incompatible declarations in one
lexical scope are rejected; an inner scope may explicitly shadow an outer
claim, and sibling scopes do not collide. An identical dominating outer claim
may suppress redundant local output. Local scope claims neither publish
translation-unit guards nor create namespace entries.

20.2.2. L1 Substrate Escape and Level Receivers

`L2` and `L3` are level receivers. `L1` is instead an external-substrate escape
receiver. All three consume one body Structure; since a Structure contains an
ordered field sequence, this is enough to consume any number of source fields.
The bootstrap records this distinction explicitly: `L1` is in
`receiver.substrate`, while only `L2` and `L3` are in `receiver.level`. Thus the
ability to embed a necessary substrate fragment does not make L1 a third
Lingvamyxa source-semantic level.

The `L1` receiver walks its body fields in order. If a field is a string/payload
atom, `L1` writes that payload at the current generated L1 position. This rule is
not tied to one spelling: ANSI-style strings, python-like quoted raw strings and
block strings are all string payload forms. If a field is a Lingvamyxa Structure
or Frame, `L1` dispatches it back to the translator. Thus an `L2` frame can be
nested inside `L1`, and an `L1` implementation fragment can be nested inside
`L2`.

A hosted ANSI C bootstrap profile may provide small L1-output receivers at the
root level and inside `L1`. These receivers are translator conveniences, not
new high-level descriptor/value categories.

Example:

include: "<stdio.h>" "<windows.h>"

This writes:

---ANSI C---
#include <stdio.h>
#include <windows.h>
---ANSI C---

An include string whose payload is already enclosed in `<...>` or `"..."` is
copied as the C include target. Otherwise the payload is treated as a local
quoted include target.

An `os` receiver may select L1 output branches by target platform:

os:
    win:
        include: "<direct.h>" "<windows.h>"
    unix:
        include: "<unistd.h>"
    default:
        "/* fallback L1 text */"
end: os

The bootstrap profile may lower these branch heads to target preprocessor
conditions such as `_WIN32`, `__APPLE__` or `__unix__`. The branch
body is still ordinary L1-output content: string payload, `include`, nested `os`
or another translator receiver admitted by the profile.

In an L2 translator context, `os` is also a namespace-import receiver. If a
branch body contains L2 declarations such as `fn`, `sub` or named Structure
descriptors, the translator imports their visible names into the current lexical
namespace view before later L2 bodies are lowered. Platform branches are treated
as alternative implementations of a structurally admitted interface: the same
function or Structure name may appear in several
`win`/`mac`/`unix`/`default` branches. Each
same-name occurrence must pass the common directional
`implements(old, new)` check before it is imported. Branch declarations are
considered in source order, so that order determines which descriptor is `old`
and which pending descriptor is `new`. After such an import, later L2 code
resolves the newest admitted occurrence normally:

os:
    win:
        fn: platformValue () int
            return: 1
        end: platformValue
    default:
        fn: platformValue () int
            return: 2
        end: platformValue
end: os

external:
    fn: main () int
        return: platformValue() = 0
    end: main
end: external

The explicit `c.platformValue()` spelling is still available as a low-level
C-surface escape, but it is not required for names imported by L2 `os`.

The `L2` receiver also consumes one body Structure, but it does not print
standalone strings as ANSI C. The rule is relative to the Structure being
translated to ANSI C: if a string field appears at level 0 of the body that `L2`
is asked to translate, and no receiver consumes that field, it is a compile-time
error. Nested Structures owned by another receiver are not reclassified by this
generic rule; that receiver defines what its own body fields mean. If a receiver
asks `L2` to translate such a nested Structure, the same level-0 rule applies
again inside that translated body. This is a phase error of the active consumer:
for `L2` it is reported by the compiler/translator, while an interpreter profile
would report the corresponding error in its own execution phase. The `c.name`
form is likewise consumed by the `c` receiver/projection: it prints an ANSI C
ordinary identifier only at the position where that explicit receiver is used.

Example:

L2:
    L1:'''typedef enum LmTransSymbolKind {
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_VARIABLE = 1,
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_FUNCTION = 2,
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_PROCEDURE = 3,
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_LABEL = 4,
        LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_STRUCTURE = 5
    } LmTransSymbolKind;'''

    fn: main () int
        return: c.LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_VARIABLE
    end: main
end: L2

The enum constants are not imported into the Lingvamyxa namespace. They remain
ordinary ANSI C names and are referenced explicitly through the permanent C
surface escape, for example `c.LM_TRANS_SYMBOL_VARIABLE`.

When the bootstrap translator reads explicit configuration Tables such as
`lm2/trans_registry.lm2`, keys follow the ordinary identifier rule. A valid
ordinary identifier is written bare:

    u32
    prefix
    sqrt_ansi_c

If a key contains operator characters, dots, arrows, whitespace,
punctuation, active structural words in that table position, or any spelling
that must round-trip exactly, it is written as an exact identifier:

    `return`
    `+`
    `!=`
    `c.*`
    `operator.infix`
    `translator class-->spelling`

The backticks are not part of the key. They are the exact-identifier delimiter,
and the resulting atom is an exact identifier, not a string literal.

The same canonicalization rule applies here as in ordinary source: if exact
identifier content is a valid bare identifier, the bare and exact forms name
the same key. This rule is purely lexical and does not make the key a class or
namespace binding.

The current bootstrap first reads configuration from `lm2/core.lm2` and then
from `lm2/trans_registry.lm2`. It uses an explicit loader to convert selected
rows into the initial receiver/operator/backend bindings required to start the
translator. That conversion is an implementation mechanism. Loading the Tables
does not itself mutate a Lingvamyxa namespace, and arbitrary application Tables
are never scanned this way.

At the earliest bootstrap point, the restricted loader recognizes root
`table:` and ordered `join:` Frames because the ordinary receiver bindings do
not yet exist. Only `.lm2` profile candidates are currently accepted. This is a
bootstrap limitation, not a second table model and not source semantics.

For every ordinary `table:` form, the first column is simply the row key by the
selected Table profile. A column named `class`, a Table named `class`, or a key
spelled like a source descriptor has no special namespace effect. The first
body atom `source`, when present, requests a translation-time/module projection
without a public C table symbol; it does not select a RegistryView. L2 and L3
both produce ordinary arena-owned Table references. A backend may optimize
their physical representation without changing that rule.

Inside `columns:`, a bare atom is a column name. An anonymous Structure uses
its first atom as the column name and the remaining atoms as descriptors, for
example `(l2 binding)` names column `l2` and records descriptor `binding`.
`columns` itself is not a namespace declaration; it is consumed by `table`.

Rows are dense. The number of row cells must be divisible by the number of
columns. The loader does not use physical source lines to infer missing cells.
The selected Table profile defines empty-cell representation. Expression-level
`None`, if available, is a separate explicit namespace binding/descriptor and
is not defined by empty cells. A pretty-printer may align columns; alignment is
not semantic.

A bootstrap implementation may lower Tables to a flat `row: table key payload`
internal representation. That is a temporary backend shape, not a different
semantic model.

L2/L3 may also construct a Table through the `join` receiver in section 19.17:

    join: (BaseTable OverlayTable) TargetTable
        columns: class  (value char)
        rows:
                 local  value
    end: TargetTable

This is not an import directive or parser syntax. It explicitly resolves the
source Table bindings, creates an arena-owned result Table and binds that result
only when requested by the form.

L2 `fn` and `sub` definitions lower to `static` ANSI C functions by default.
This keeps ordinary L2 helper procedures local to the generated translation
unit. The `external` receiver wraps exactly one `fn` or `sub` and requests
external ANSI C linkage for that definition:

L2:
    external:
        fn: main () int
            return: 0
        end: main
    end: external
end: L2

Declaration qualifiers are ordinary receiver composition in the C-profile
projection. For example, `const: @: LmP0Node p` lowers to `const LmP0Node *p`.
The same qualifier form may appear in structure fields, parameters and local
declarations when the active L2 lowering profile supports the corresponding C
declarator role.

Address-producing and address-consuming forms:

    @count              address of the primitive storage location `count`
    p: @count           store that address into an address-depth-1 slot
    pp: @p              store the address of `p` into an address-depth-2 slot
    \p                  raw load through the explicit typed address `p`
    leafs[3]            checked ArrayReference access at index `3`
    \(rawBase + 3)      raw load after address arithmetic when `rawBase` is an
                        explicit typed address from an L2 machine-storage operation


20.3. Primitive Expression Operators

The minimal L2 primitive operator profile assigns explicit operator roles:

    grouping        (expr)
    prefix          @expr, \expr, ++expr, --expr, +expr, -expr, !expr, ~expr
    postfix         expr++, expr--
    infix           +, -, *, /, %, =, !=, <, <=, >, >=, &&, ||, &, |, ^

Source style: prefix operator spellings such as `@`, `\`, `++`, `--`, `!` and
`~` should be written adjacent to their operand when the operand is on the same
physical line, for example `@slot`, `\item`, `++index` and `!ready`. This is a
style convention, not a P0 tokenization requirement; P0 still tokenizes
operator spellings as separate operator-token fields where appropriate.

Indexing is a postfix expression operator with the bracket form `target[index]`,
specified with storage and address forms above. For an ArrayReference it is
checked Array indexing; for an explicit typed-address Primitive it is the
separate L2 raw-address operation. The `index` part is consumed as its own
expression range by the active L2 expression profile. Nested index operators,
calls, bounded Structures, strings and ordinary operator tokens inside the
brackets are therefore lowered by the same expression machinery as ordinary
argument expressions. Field-follow remains the L2 `\field` form, not ANSI C
`.` or `->` source syntax.

The bracket in `target\[occurrence]field` is not this postfix operator: its
position immediately after the field-follow backslash makes it the
occurrence qualifier for the following exact field name. Therefore
`target\field[index]` and `target\[index]field` are distinct forms.

This bracket form is separate from the `[]:` receiver spelling. `[]:` is a head
symbol used by Array constructors. `target[...]` is a
postfix index/access suffix. A bare `[a b]` is not a portable L2 grouping form;
use `(a b)` for a bounded Structure unless a profile explicitly admits standalone
square-bracket values.

The token `=` is comparison/equality for expression/operator consumers. It is not native assignment.

The C-profile lowering maps the listed primitive operators to ANSI C operator
spellings, except that Lingvamyxa `=` lowers to ANSI C `==`, `@expr` lowers to
address-of and `\expr` lowers to raw load/dereference when it is not a
field-follow token. Shifts, overflow rules, integer division rules,
floating-point rules and conversion rules remain L2 profile details unless a
later specification selects a mandatory set.

20.4. Update and Callable Consumption

L2 uses the same head/update surface form as the rest of the structural core:

    target: value

Examples:

    count: 6
    tree\branch: branch
    leafs[3]: 10

After resolution, a plain-name form applies `implements(old, new)` and appends
the admitted binding occurrence; an addressable field/index form mutates the
selected storage location; and a function/subroutine/receiver form is consumed
as a call. A backend may reuse physical storage for the first case only when
the observable ordered-occurrence semantics is preserved. This is compile-time
resolution, not a separate assignment syntax.

The update operator tokens `++` and `--` are part of the minimal L2 C-profile as
both prefix and postfix expression operators. They are expression operators, not
a second assignment syntax. Other profiles may lower them differently, but they
still compose with the same operator-token consumption model.

20.5. Minimal L2 Control Receivers

The minimal L2 executable-body control set is:

    if / else
    while
    synchronized
    until
    for(init, condition, step)
    return
    break
    continue

These are source-level control receivers. They are not raw jumps in the source
semantics, even if a translator lowers them to labels and branches. In
particular, `else` is a receiver in the namespace, not parser syntax.

The first ANSI C bootstrap translator needs only:

    if / else
    while
    synchronized
    return
    break
    continue

`until` and `for` remain part of the minimal L2 semantic set, but may be added
after the first procedural bootstrap subset.

20.5.1. `if` and `else`

L2 `if` consumes:

    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `if` receiver

L2 `else` is a separate statement receiver. It consumes:

    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `else` receiver

`else` is valid as the false branch only when it immediately follows an `if`
statement at the same statement level.

Example:

if: count > 0
    print: count


With a following sibling `else`:

if: count > 0
    print: count

else:
    print: 0


20.5.2. `while`

L2 `while` is a pre-condition loop.

Semantic lowering shape:

    while(condition, statementStructure)

Example:

while: i < n
    print: i
    i: (i + 1)


20.5.3. `until`

L2 `until` is a conditional close for the current executable block.

Example:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

In this example, `record` is an explicit compact-call argument of `endOfInput`. The `until:` line consumes the whole tail `endOfInput(record)` as a boolean-producing condition Structure. The condition producer is the compact call `endOfInput(record)`, not the invalid space-separated spelling `endOfInput record`.

More explicit condition form:

readLoop:
    readNext: record
    process: record
until: endOfInput(record)

Conceptual lowering shape:

    until(currentStatementStructure, condition)

The body executes first. The loop repeats until the condition is true.

If the condition is false, control transfers to the beginning of the executable block closed by `until`. If the condition is true, the block completes and execution continues after the `until` line.

20.5.4. `for(init, condition, step)`

L2 `for` consumes:

    init      -> initialization Structure
    condition -> boolean-producing expression
    step      -> iteration-step statement Structure
    body      -> statement Structure consumed by the `for` receiver

Vertical body form:

for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++
    print: i


Compact body form:

    for: int(i, 0) (i < n) i++ print(i)

20.5.5. `return`

L2 `return` exits the current executable body.

Bare runtime form inside a body:

    return

Value runtime form inside a body:

    return: value

For an `fn`, `return: value` consumes a Primitive, StructureReference or
ArrayReference whose descriptor implements the declared return descriptor and
exits. For a Structure or Array result it forwards the exact reference under
sections 8.8 and 19.29.5. A bare `return` is not a valid `fn` return in the core
profile.

For a `sub`, `return` exits without producing a normal return value. `return: value` is invalid for `sub` unless a later profile defines status/result subroutines.

At a valid terminal trailer position for an open executable definition, an admitted return form may be terminal return. Terminal return appends the corresponding runtime return as the final body item and closes the executable definition, in the same checked close position where the executable's admitted `end:` target would otherwise be expected. For an `fn`, that target is the function name exposed by the `fn` receiver, such as `end: square`, and the terminal return form is `return: value`. The common compact position is the executable definition's own source level; a smaller source level may also be valid when the ordinary source-level cut reaches the visible executable tail and intervening frames admit that closure.

Example:

fn: square (int(x)) (int)
return: x * x

20.5.6. `break` and `continue`

`break` exits the nearest active L2 loop frame.

`continue` starts the next iteration of the nearest active L2 loop frame.

For `while`, `continue` transfers control to the next condition check.

For `until`, `continue` transfers control to the post-condition check for the current iteration.

For `for`, `continue` transfers control to the iteration-step Structure, then to the next condition check.

20.5.7. `synchronized`

`synchronized` consumes an object/address expression and a statement Structure
owned by the `synchronized` receiver. It is a structured synchronization
receiver, not a raw lock/unlock pair in source syntax.

In the minimal L2 profile, `synchronized` has an object-expression plus
statement-tail receiver signature. The source after `synchronized:` is still the
ordinary P0 argument Structure.
Inline fields remain ordinary fields of that Structure. If an inline object
expression is followed by a child source level, that child part is materialized
as a nested Structure field; therefore:

    synchronized: @lock
        break

is structurally equivalent to:

    synchronized: @lock (break)

while:

    synchronized: @lock break

keeps `break` in the inline object-expression field sequence. A receiver may
then reject that expression semantically, but P0 must not silently reinterpret
the same-line word as a body. If the consumed body tail is a single Structure,
that Structure is consumed as its fields, as in the general
single-Structure-as-N-arguments rule.

Example:

synchronized: @object
    updateObject: object

The first ANSI C bootstrap projection may lower this to runtime hooks:

---ANSI C---
lm_synchronized_enter(&object);
{
    updateObject(object);
}
lm_synchronized_leave(&object);
---ANSI C---

The runtime hook must implement the selected monitor policy, including
Java-style reentrant entry for the same thread when that profile is selected.

The first ANSI C bootstrap translator registers each active `synchronized`
monitor as a cleanup entry. Ordinary `return`, `break` and `continue` transfers
emit the required `lm_synchronized_leave(...)` calls before leaving the protected
region. A value-return inside `synchronized` is lowered through a temporary of
the current function return type, then the active monitors are released, then
the temporary is returned. For a Structure or Array result this temporary is
only a reference slot: it preserves the exact referent and introduces no
allocation, aggregate copy, promotion or arena migration.

Diagnostic failure across a synchronized boundary belongs to the wider
runtime/translator cleanup rules.

20.6. Outside the Minimal L2 Set

The following section 19 operators are not part of the minimal L2 control set
unless an L2 profile explicitly enables or redefines them:

    match / default-pattern receivers
    each
    finally
    guard / fail / throws / handlers
    retry / retryable
    assert
    redo
    yield
    labelled break / labelled continue

They may still exist in L3 or a richer L2 profile.

This subsection describes the first/minimal L2 bootstrap control subset, not a
level-order rule where L3 is wider than L2. L2 remains the wider low-level level
because it also owns the machine forms in sections 20.1 through 20.4; L3 never
exposes those forms.

21. Lexical Namespace State, Rebinding and Local Updates

21.1. Namespace Is Ordered Name-to-Binding State

At every source point, the lexical namespace is conceptually:

    Namespace = Name -> ordered Binding occurrences

A Binding records the visible value/reference or translator-known descriptor
role required to resolve that name. Bindings include variables, functions,
subroutines, receivers, callable/structural descriptors, labels, explicit Table
values and ordinary values.

Conceptual example:

namespace:
    fn: IntToInt (int) int
    factorial: IntToInt
    print: Sub(...)
    fn: AddX (int) int
    String: TypeDescriptor
    int: TypeDescriptor
    ---

The example is notation for name-resolution state. A translator may represent
it with an arena-resident Structure/Table, lexical maps, symbol indexes,
persistent overlays, direct references or any equivalent mechanism. Repeated
exact names and their order are source-semantic; ordinary lookup selects the
newest still-active occurrence.

The rule is shared lexical lookup, not global lookup. A view may be local to an
executable definition, block, receiver context, module, service, explicit
namespace import or runtime profile. Names in the visible chain share lookup and
the same `implements(old, new)` admission rule, but the language does not define
one global dictionary for all programs and services.

Inside one active lexical namespace view, Lingvamyxa does not use separate
function, variable and label namespaces. A name introduced as a variable,
function, subroutine, label, local executable, callable descriptor, receiver,
structural/protocol descriptor or imported descriptor participates in the same
ordered occurrence sequence for that exact name. The newest still-active
occurrence is the one found by ordinary lookup. The value behind it may be used
in any way admitted by the consuming code; the namespace does not choose a
different binding according to whether the next form looks like a call, a
rebind or a control target.

21.2. Local Namespace View

Entering an executable definition creates a local namespace view.

The local view may be implemented as a persistent overlay, copy-on-write map,
statically resolved lexical environment or another representation preserving
the same behavior. No runtime namespace object is required.

A plain-name rebind inside an executable body applies `implements(old, new)` and
appends the admitted occurrence to the local namespace view. It does not mutate
the preceding or outer occurrence. Updating an addressable field/index location
is instead storage mutation and does not append a namespace occurrence.

Local namespace occurrences do not mutate the outer namespace and do not change
the externally visible descriptor of the executable definition.

21.3. Structural Compatibility of Local Redeclarations and Rebinds

Local redeclaration and plain-name rebinding use exactly the same rule as every
other namespace redeclaration:

    implements(OldType, NewType)

The first-level names of `NewType` must be a subset of the first-level names of
`OldType`. There is no additional callable-signature, variance, mutability,
effect, ownership or conversion algorithm in the language core. If an
application wants to validate any such property, it represents that validation
as ordinary data and code. A first-level name such as `validate` participates
in `implements` only as a required name.

The old descriptor is captured before the pending local occurrence is visible.
On success the new occurrence becomes the result of ordinary lookup; on failure
the local namespace is unchanged.

21.4. Local Function/Subroutine Name Rebinding

Because function/subroutine names are ordinary namespace bindings, a same-name
local declaration or plain-name rebind uses the common structural rule. There
is no separate callable replacement rule.

Example:

fn: IntToInt (int) int

fn: compute (int(x)) (int)
    fn: `slow path` (int(v)) (int)
    return: v * v
    fn: `fast path` (int(v)) (int)
    return: v << 1
    IntToInt: strategy `slow path`
    if: x < 10
        strategy: `fast path`
return: strategy(x)

Here `strategy` is explicitly introduced as a local binding whose initial value
is reached as `slow path`. Rebinding it to the value reached as `fast path` is
valid only when the captured old descriptor implements the pending new
descriptor by first-level name. The later call `strategy(x)` is separately
validated by the resolved call consumer.

This operation does not replace `slow path`, `fast path`, `compute`, or any outer namespace binding. It only changes the local namespace view used inside the current executable body.

21.5. Directional Structural Redeclaration

Redeclaration is neither unrestricted replacement nor nominal type equality. It
is the one directional first-level-name check from section 7.3.1.

Therefore:

    int: x 1
    x: "hello"

is invalid when the descriptor of the old `x` occurrence does not implement the
descriptor proposed by the new occurrence.

And:

    factorial: "hello"

is invalid when the old `factorial` descriptor does not expose every first-level
name exposed by the pending text descriptor. The fact that one value is
callable is not checked by a second hidden compatibility system.

21.6. Interaction with Return and Function Names

The canonical return mechanism for an `fn` is `return: value`. When `return: value` is written in a valid terminal trailer position, it also closes the `fn` source frame. The current function name is an ordinary namespace binding, not a separate return syntax.

The same visible name may participate in ordinary function/subroutine lookup,
recursion and local namespace overlay operations. If a plain-name rebind appends
an admitted non-function value under that exact name, ordinary lookup selects
the new occurrence. Later recursive call consumption through that spelling then
fails unless the new value itself admits the requested call.

21.7. Translation and Optimization

The source semantics allow a local namespace copy or overlay, but implementations do not have to allocate an actual dictionary at runtime.

A translator may optimize local namespace operations by:

    avoiding a copy when no local updates occur
    using copy-on-write for a small number of updates
    representing the overlay as a persistent map
    replacing lookups by direct references when types and bindings are static
    inlining a newest callable occurrence when it is non-escaping
    lowering an admitted callable rebind to a local reference/descriptor update
    rejecting or de-optimizing cases that escape the local namespace view beyond the executable lifetime

The source rule is structural: local declarations and plain-name rebinds append
namespace occurrences only after `implements(old, new)` succeeds when an older
exact-name occurrence is visible. No particular runtime namespace
representation is required.

The current hosted backend does not yet expose source access to an arbitrary
older occurrence or project every occurrence sequence as addressable target
data. Such optimization is conforming only while newest-occurrence lookup and
scope restoration remain observable as specified.

The hosted migration currently applies the descriptor gate when binding named
Structure and physical `merge:` descriptors. General plain-name runtime rebind
lowering and materialization of all binding occurrences remain implementation
work; legacy target-storage assignment is not the normative source model above.

The current low-level hosted bind API transfers unique ownership of a raw
descriptor object and therefore rejects passing the exact descriptor pointer
already owned by the preceding occurrence. That is an implementation memory-
ownership guard, not an additional source compatibility rule. A general rebind
of the same StructureReference remains admissible because
`implements(old, new)` compares its first-level names; its lowering must retain,
clone or reference-count the value instead of creating two raw owners.

?
Escaping local namespace overlays, closure capture of locally rebound
function/subroutine values, mutation of outer bindings and explicit
global/external update syntax are not specified in this document. They belong to
a future execution-model and runtime specification.
?
